## B.1 Executing Entity Information

CRDB Bank Plc.

## B.2 Executive summary (max 750 words; approximately 1.5 pages)

CRDB Bank, in line with the Acceded Country of the GCF, is submitting the Tanzania CRDB Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme (TACATDP) to unlock the climate change adaptation potential and enhance food security for smallholder farmers in the agriculture sector. TACATDP will support CRDB Bank, Tanzania's largest lender to the agriculture sector, with the resources and capacity to offer innovative financial products to farmers. As such, TACATDP is expected to increase access to finance for smallholder farmers by providing credit, and capacity required to combat current and future climate contexts and agronomic specificities in Tanzania.

Agriculture is essential to Tanzania's economic and social fabric, accounting for 27% of its gross domestic product (GDP) and 67% of jobs. However, due to technical, environmental, ecological, and social factors, the agricultural sector is particularly vulnerable to the adverse effects of climate change. These include increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as erratic rainfall patterns, drought, shifting weather patterns, and increased disease incidence which will continue to threaten food security in Tanzania. The country's vulnerability to climate change has also been partly attributed to a decline in agricultural productivity. Over the past two decades, the sector has experienced a 1% annual decline in agricultural output per hectare of cultivated land since 2012. The World Bank found that agricultural productivity in Tanzania already suffers at least USD 7 billion annually due to climate change and other related factors.

Recent studies have shown that climate change may have cost 17% of Tanzania's economic growth potential in 2018, with the agriculture sector being most affected by climate change and other related factors. The value-losses of agricultural GDP over the coming five decades is estimated at approximately USD 27 billion. With agriculture playing an integral role in the lives of the country’s most marginalized groups, it is imperative that smallholder farmers are provided with the necessary tools to adapt to these changes. Smallholder farmers and 98% of economically active rural Tanzanian women are engaged in farming – the agricultural sector's mainstay. They are disproportionately harm those groups and hamper the country's sustainable development.

In response, to transform the country’s agriculture sector and ensure the long-term adaptation capacity, the TACATDP proposes a multipronged approach through two (2) components. The first component is focused on strengthening the institutional framework for climate change adaptation and resilience in the agriculture sector and for smallholder farmers in particular. This includes developing a national agricultural climate change action plan, as well as establishing an advisory committee to provide technical guidance and oversight of TACATDP implementation.

The second component focuses on providing financial products and services that will enable smallholder farmers to adapt to current and future climate contexts and agronomic specificities. The proposed financial products include microfinance loans, insurance, and other innovative financing mechanisms such as agricultural value chain finance. These products are designed to provide smallholder farmers with the necessary tools to mitigate risks associated with climate change and other related factors.

The TACATDP is expected to increase access to finance for smallholder farmers by providing credit, and capacity required to combat current and future climate contexts and agronomic specificities in Tanzania.


---
**Page 7**
---

Agricultural Resilience and Adaptation (ARA) lending operations and supporting a guarantee credit-enhancement facility to ease lending towards smallholder farmers pursuing adaptation investments. The ARA component will be implemented through the launch of a dedicated ARA insurance scheme focused on smallholder farmers. The results under this component will help justify and further scale up innovative technologies and climate resilience measures in agriculture, including agribusinesses.

The successful implementation of TACATDP will also increase private investments into agriculture and promote the development of new financial products to support adaptation activities.

Complementing such activity will be a technical assistance (TA) and capacity building component to ensure the long-term sustainability of climate adaptation financing in the country in years to come. The comprehensive targeted TA comprises five (5) capacity building sub-activities and five (5) pilot projects. These will focus on providing technical assistance to agribusinesses and climate adaptation activities, with the ultimate goal of supporting large-scale investments into climate-resilient agriculture.

Financial institutions, agriculture end-users, regulators, and other stakeholders will be given the opportunity to participate in these pilot projects. The TA component will also provide training for profitable climate-compatible agricultural financing, including agribusiness. Capacity-building activities under this component will focus on building the capacity of financial institutions and integrating climate risk management within their agricultural lending portfolios, as well as working with regulators to ensure that they are able to support the development of these products.

Under this component will help build, strengthen, and disseminate the data tools, climate knowledge, institutional capacity, and policy interventions required to integrate climate risks and climate adaptation into financial institutions' lending activities in Tanzania.

By transforming the country's climate financing processes to better address climate adaptation in the sector, TACATDP will achieve a paradigm shift in the country's approach towards climate change and adaptation assistance. The TACATDP is estimated to realize 52,086 direct beneficiaries per year (1,241,286 direct beneficiaries over the programme’s lifetime) and an additional: 245,152 indirect beneficiaries per year (4,903,056 indirect beneficiaries over the programme's lifetime). This will provide immediate and long-lasting support to 1.2% of Tanzania’s population as direct beneficiaries and 4.67% of the country’s population indirectly, of which 30-50% are women.

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1500 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### Background

Agriculture is the backbone of the United Republic of Tanzania (URT) economy. The sector is central to achieving higher and more inclusive economic growth. It accounts for 27% of the country's gross domestic product (GDP) and 67% of jobs. Small-scale farmers still dominate the sector. Production is highly vulnerable to climate change, with a high degree of uncertainty due to extreme vulnerability to climate change. The agricultural sector in Tanzania is dominated by smallholder crop production—crops those used mainly for exports—are coffee, cotton, cashew, nut, pyrethrum, tea leaves, horticultural crops, and vegetables. Small-scale farmers produce about 83% of the country's food crops—71% to agricultural GDP and grows at 4.6% per annum. Food crop production increases at about 2.5% per year, while livestock production has increased by an average rate of 0.9% for about 10 years. Food and cash crops account for about 70% of rural income. Considering these factors, agriculture is the most important sector in Tanzania's economy and a key driver of the country's socio-economic development, particularly in rural areas. However, this prospect is affected by multiple factors, including a high degree of uncertainty due to extreme vulnerability to climate change.

CRDB Bank Plc—Banking, Lending, Cards, Insurance & CRDB Bank Plc is an African bank and a leading commercial provider in Tanzania with current presence in Tanzania and Burundi. For Africa. CRDB Bank Plc is a major lender to the agriculture sector, providing 45% of all credit to agricultural enterprises. The bank has been recognized as a key player in promoting financial inclusion and sustainable contribution to the society. As a systematically significant financial institution and Tanzania's sole national GCF Direct Access Certified Accredited Entity (DAE), CRDB Bank has a crucial role to play in supporting the country’s climate change adaptation efforts. As the largest commercial bank in Tanzania's largest commercial bank, it is uniquely positioned to channel international support efficiently into the economy through its extensive network of branches and ATMs, as well as microenterprises, and small- and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), through which climate finance vehicles that benefit from capital markets such as bonds, loans, guarantees, equity.

CRDB Bank offers a comprehensive range of corporate, retail, business banking, premier bancassurance and wholesale microfinance services through a network of 260 branches, 551 ATMs, 18 Deposit Points, 349 Microbranches, and 700+ agents. The bank also has 450 microfinance partner institutions through which pertinent services are rendered to customers.

As CRDB Bank scales up its management of climate-aligned investment and grows its climate-compatible business lines, CRDB Bank can not only strengthen its own performance but can also contribute significantly to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) for clients, the communities it serves, and the wider business and financial ecosystem in Tanzania.

#### Evidence of climate change in the URT

While a detailed assessment of the current and future climate characteristics in the URT is supplied as Annex 22, background information and a brief description of climate change are provided below:

1. World Bank (2009). World Development Indicators.
2. United Republic of Tanzania (2015). Agricultural Sector Development Programme Phase II.


---
**Page 9**
---

The climate of Tanzania is complex due to the simultaneous impacts of several key factors, ranging from the El Niño Southern Oscillation and global modes of ocean-atmosphere variability, interacting with the underlying heterogeneous surface features. The large-scale drivers include Inter-tropical Convergence Zone (ITCZ), equatorial Pacific Sea surface temperature (ENSO), Indian Ocean Dipole (IOD) and other climate indices. The complex topography surrounding Mount Kilimanjaro, dense forests, the coast, and the Lake Victoria basin further complicates the climate system.

The projected increase in atmospheric CO2 concentration could have a substantial impact on the climate of Tanzania in terms of warming trends and changes in precipitation patterns. Several studies have been conducted to assess how climate change over East Africa under different emission scenarios. Most of these studies are either focusing on the present-day climate or future climate, with few studies having detailed regional and local analysis that have difficulty representing local-scale processes. However, detailed regional and local analysis is more important for understanding the impacts of climate change in Tanzania as it has a high value.

This section presents an assessment of the mean climate and sector-specific extreme indices over Tanzania for the present-day climate as well as projected changes for three future horizons based on two emission scenarios (RCP2.6 and RCP8.5). The analysis considered two emission scenarios – RCP4.5 (low) and RCP8.5 (high) – for future climates.

**Present day climate:**

Trend analysis of the historical period (1976-2005) revealed the evidence of a drying and warming trend in Tanzania (see Figure A1.3). Observations show the drying trend to be fastest over northeastern regions at a rate of approximately 8mm/month/decade. While the warming trend is observed throughout, the highest warming trend of approximately 0.35°C/decade is noted over the western regions.

The results also highlight that the daytime warming trend is stronger than the nighttime warming trend.

Figure A1.2 shows the spatial pattern of extreme indices related to water availability/flood risk indicator derived from both observations and simulations. The ensemble mean model simulation shows similar spatial characteristics despite underestimating the intensity.

Future climate:

Projected changes to mean temperature and precipitation show an increase in temperature for the whole region with increases projected only to the northeastern region (as seen in Figure A.4). For western boundary areas, however, precipitation is projected to decrease by 10% at the end of the century in RCP8.5 while warming levelled to approximately 2°C in RCP4.5, demonstrating the importance of temperature increase on precipitation change. The projected increase (decrease) in precipitation could reach around 15mm/month for the northeastern boundary region and 30mm/month for western boundary regions under RCP8.5. In contrast, the precipitation increase (decrease) in the northeast (western) boundary regions are smaller in magnitude and are not statistically significant in the RCP4.5 scenario.

**Page 12**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 9 OF 78

B

While all heat stress indicators are projected to increase in future climates, the highest and most significant increase is noted for warm spell duration index (WSDI). The duration of warm spells is projected to increase by approximately 30 days at the end of the century under the RCP2.6 emission scenario (see Figure A1.4), and by approximately 245 days at the end of the century under the RCP8.5 emission scenario (see Figure A1.5). The WSDI is derived from temperature data alone, which explains why it increases in all regions.

The projected increase in short-lived heavy precipitation events followed by a long dry spell and/or heat stress events will have critical implications for local water resources management and agricultural production.

Following is an overview of the projection of other climate indices relevant for this programme.

In the high-warming scenario, temperatures in the near future period (2026-2055) are projected to increase by up to 3°C compared with the reference period. The frequency of extreme hot days and nights will also increase. In the far-future period, temperatures are projected to be higher than those observed during the reference period. The frequency of warm days and nights is also projected to increase by 116 days and by 98 nights in the future compared to the reference period. In the future period, precipitation in the rainy season will decrease on average by up to 20% while the number of dry days will increase by approximately 35%. The frequency of extreme wet events is projected to remain similar to that observed during the reference period.

Investigations into the impact of climate change on crop yields and livestock production suggest that with increased GHG emissions, the dry season length is projected to expand. The projected expansion of the dry season length is higher for RCPS5 and towards the end of the century. The likelihood of extreme droughts in the future period is also projected to increase compared to the reference period. These findings suggest that alternative adaptation measures such as irrigation or switching to better-adapted alternative crops, worth considering.

Specific sectoral impacts

Agriculture:

The agriculture sector comprises approximately 27% of Tanzania’s GDP as well as 67% of jobs.* Most of Tanzania's rural population is solely dependent on agriculture for their livelihoods. Agriculture in Tanzania is dominated by smallholder farmers, many of whom are cultivating farms of less than two hectares. The majority of the country's food production comes from these small-scale farms and areas cultivated by hand labor. Food crop production dominates the agriculture economy. Maize is the country’s most important staple food crop, grown on more than 90% of all arable land in Tanzania regions of the country. Most of Tanzania is classified broadly as a “Mixed-Mixed” farming system, with an emphasis on maize and other cereals. Rice is also widely cultivated throughout the country, being the second most important staple in Tanzanian agriculture. Rain-fed paddy rice production by smallholder farmers accounts for 90% of total rice production in Tanzania, mainly concentrated in Tabora. Other major food crops include sorghum, millet, wheat, pulses, cassava, potatoes, bananas, plantains, sugar, groundnuts, sesame, coconuts, and soybeans. Much of Tanzania’s sorghum and millet are grown on small-scale farms in the country's northern regions. Most of the maize is grown in the country's southern western regions.

Dependency on rain-fed agriculture makes Tanzania's agriculture sector particularly vulnerable to weather variability. The most significant risk from rainfall in its intensity and distribution currently the most frequent and damaging production risk facing the sector, while drought is also a significant concern. Climate change will likely increase the frequency of extreme weather events and lead to changes including increases in agro-diversity. For instance, a significant declining trend in the production of maize, wheat, and sorghum has been observed over the last 30 years. This decline could have food security for 30% of the area considered? The negative trend of yield is attributed to the declining mean annual rainfall. Another study also found a reduction in coffee (C. arabica) yield over northern Tanzania as a result of climate change. A study by the International Coffee Organization (ICO) in Timor will reduce the annual coffee yield by about 140 kg/ha.

Climate variability and change and their associated adverse impacts play a fundamental role in influencing agricultural production systems. El Niño and La Niña explain up to 35% of the global variation on wheat, oil seed, and coarse grain.* In a modeling study over Tanzania, a 17% increase in mean annual rainfall is projected by 2080 under RCP4.5 scenario with an average mean temperature by 2050.* Irrigated agriculture, in areas in which it is implemented, helps to stabilize yields of crops such as maize and wheat, but has little effect on farm productivity and income, and produce high-value crops such as vegetables and flowers.

Evidence of the impacts of climate variability on agriculture sector in Tanzania includes shifts in agro-ecological zones, prolonged dry episodes, unpredictability in rainfall, uncertainty in cropping patterns, increased frequency of extreme weather events, changes in temperature regimes, and other factors pertaining to pests and diseases. The shortening and/or changing of the growing season, a trend that has already been observed over the last 30 years, is another significant impact of climate change on changes in rainfall patterns. Between 2015 and 2019, drought was most frequently reported in the northern regions (Arusha, Mara, Kilimanjaro, Manyara, Singida, Tanga, Mtwara, Morogoro), and southeastern regions (Mtwara and Lindi). **rainfall patterns in the north correspond with this pattern.**

Overall: national food production is projected to decrease by 8-13% by 2050 due to increased heat stress, drying, erosion, flood damage, and post-harvest losses. Bean, sorghum, and rice yield projections follow similar trends, with decreases of 5-9%, projected by 2050. Increasing heat stress and prolonged dry spells are expected to reduce the potential yields for maize and wheat from the current 225 kg/ha to less than 100 kg/ha by 2060. Along the coast, cassava and rice crops are subject to increased flooding due to sea level rise and more intense storms.

In Tanzania, the agricultural GDP from the impacts of climate change over the coming five decades is estimated at US$73 billion (Tanzanian Shillings 145.8 billion) by 2060. The total economic loss is projected at US$90 million (Tanzanian Shillings 66,000 billion). With an expected increase of disaster incidents, early warning systems and climate risk insurance are essential to support the development of adaptation strategies, including a crop insurance scheme. It has to be noted that other climatic drivers such as decreasing rainfall variability and increasing temperatures will have significant impacts on agricultural productivity significantly. For instance, declined fertilizer consumption accounts for 59% of the variability in the total annual production of rice and maize over Usangu plains in Tanzania.

For comparison, the average yield per hectare is projected to decrease by about 20%.

From both policy and investment perspectives, the adaptation of the agricultural sector as it relates to crop production should consider the synergies with other closely related sectors, including pest control, water management, soil fertility maintenance, and livestock. Improved land use and resource management can improve the resilience of rural incomes. Due to the removal of biomass, e.g., deforestation, and the associated loss of organic matter in soils, improved land use and resource management is essential for maintaining soil fertility; more than 60% of the land used to produce crops, livestock, and forest products has been degraded. Improved land use and resource management can also improve water and soil nutrients, grow crops, provide forest products, assure water availability and quality, and provide other ecosystem services. This results in significant savings for the environment and the economy. Moreover, modern agricultural practices are often difficult or impossible to hard break the cycle of poverty. It is important to highlight that agriculture in Tanzania accounts for an estimated 80% of total employment and contributes about 45% of GDP, which is more than double the African average of about 20%.

Considering the importance of this sector for the national economy and social development, Tanzania must urgently address climate risk and environmental degradation to ensure the long-term productivity of its agricultural sector. This transformation of Tanzanian agriculture requires a shift in policy from short-term gains to long-term sustainability through improved water use and land management. An efficient agricultural adaptation strategy should also monitor any adverse effects on biodiversity and ecosystem services. The current model of transformation of agriculture is driven by factors such as increased purchased inputs per unit of land, mechanization and cultivating more land.

**Water resources:**

Increasing rainfall variability and prolonged droughts cause significant pressures on the country's available water resources. Severe and recurrent droughts in the past decade triggered a decrease in water availability for irrigation and livestock grazing, which is critical to the development of hydropower dams. Furthermore, some of Tanzania’s perennial rivers have changed to seasonal rivers due to climate change. The decline in rainfall has also led to reduced access to water resources by increasing climate change and variability is further exacerbated by degradation caused by pollution, over-abstraction, and encroachment of cultivated areas for various land uses (e.g., agriculture, livestock grazing, urbanization). Water availability is projected to decrease.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2 | PAGE 14 OF 78

## Cassava, a key food staple in Tanzania, is produced extensively in the country in almost all districts.

Conversion of cassava land to rice varies from 0.3 million hectares (in central districts) to 5.9 million hectares (very suitable) in the western districts. Cassava's climatic suitability is projected to decrease in nearly all areas of the country, with a decrease of >15% between now and the 2040s. Beans are projected to increase in suitability by up to 30%, while maize suitability will remain relatively stable.

### Climatic Suitability

* **Cassava:** Climatic suitability ranges between 0.6 and 1 in all districts, reflecting beans' relatively good adaptability to climatic change.
* **Beans:** Climatic suitability is projected to increase by up to 30% across the country due to a decrease in temperature extremes.
* **Maize:** Climatic suitability is projected to remain stable.

To counter the potential negative consequences of climate change on crop productivity in Tanzania, new agricultural technologies and practices will be introduced. These include:

1. Improved seed varieties with higher yield stability under changing climatic conditions.
2. New irrigation systems that are more efficient and better suited for rainfed agriculture.
3. Better soil management techniques to improve water retention and nutrient cycling.

### Adaptation Strategies

* **Improved Seed Varieties:** Beans will be introduced in areas where they currently have low suitability, while maize varieties with higher yield stability under changing climatic conditions will be promoted.
* **Irrigation Systems:** New irrigation systems that are more efficient and better suited for rainfed agriculture will be introduced to improve water use efficiency.

### Overall Objective

The overall objective of the programme is to support farmers with adaptation tools, knowledge, and capacity to enable and unlock the best adaptation options to current and future climatic contexts and agronomic specifics.

Alignment with the country's national priorities and strong country ownership approach.

This project will provide a direct and substantive contribution to the implementation of key climate change adaptation strategies framed in Tanzania, including:

* **Tanzania's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC):** It provided an initial estimate of immediate and start-up financing needs for enhancing adaptive capacity at approximately USD 1.5 billion per year by 2030. The NDC aims to increase the country’s climate change adaptation and build resilience through 2020, increasing to USD 7 billion per year by 2030.
* **Tanzania's National Agriculture Policy (NAP):** It aims at addressing challenges that hamper the development of agriculture in Tanzania such as low productivity, overdependence on rain-fed agriculture, climate change, inadequate participation of the private sector, environmental degradation, and crop disease. The policy promotes a shift from rainfed to irrigated agriculture.

- **The Tanzania Agriculture Climate Resilient Plan**, an elaborated policy for the period of 2014–2019, suffered from a lack of operationalization due to a lack of resources to support its implementation. The plan identified four key areas as strategic entry points for climate change activities and investment priorities that aim at: (i) improving agricultural land and water management; (ii) increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers in the face of short-term climate shocks; and (iv) strengthening knowledge and system to target climate action.
- The recently adopted National Climate Change Strategy prioritizes for adaptation: The strategy covers broader areas and actionable strategic objectives that need to be implemented and relates to the national development plan. It identifies agriculture as a key sector where climate change adaptation remains amongst the highest priorities for Tanzania. This Strategy provides the basis for the implementation of the National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) and existing and emerging climate change threats. The adaptation strategies identified in this section are: (i) Promote sustainable land management practices; (ii) Promote agroforestry (NAPs); and Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) which identify medium- and long-term adaptation actions. Agriculture is central to the strategy, which recommends the following: (i) Increase access to climate information services and tools; (ii) Promote new technologies; Reduce post-harvest losses and promote value addition of agricultural produce; and Promote the development of climate risk management mechanisms.
- The programme is in full accordance with the Tanzania’s National Vision 2035* and the Five Year Development Plan. By providing a clear path for taking on markets, it will enable the utilization of the allow for mobilized private investments in the agriculture sector as outlined by the Agricultural Sector Development Programme (ASDP). It provides a clear path for agriculture through 2028. Adopted in 2016, this ASDP is set up with goals to promote transformation by promoting commercialization, prioritizing high-potential commodity value chains and improving access to markets. The programme acknowledges that the current transformation of agriculture offers opportunities for private sector investment and growth, particularly among the poor. Private investment is central to financing Tanzania’s strategy for sustained growth, and it is believed that private sector investments will be sufficient to meet its objectives and that private investment is essential. Of the total national budgeting of USD 45 billion, private investment is expected to contribute USD 20 billion.
- The programme remains fully in accordance with the 2007 National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) and the 2016 National Climate Change Strategy. It includes international financial and technical assistance, including: (i) Water efficiency in crop production irrigation to boost production and conserve water in all areas; (ii) Alternative farming systems and water harvesting techniques for smallholder farmers; (iii) Improved livestock management practices; (iv) Community based catchments conservation and management programs; (v) Explore and invest in renewable energy sources, including solar power generation and hydropower; (vi) Lost hydro potential; (vii) Promotion of application of cogeneration in the industry sector for lost hydro potential. # Main root causes and barriers that need to be addressed with this proposal

The overall performance of agriculture has been less than ideal in recent past years. Little private investment has gone into agricultural activities, and the rate of growth in agricultural output has averaged only around 0.5% against an overall 6% annual growth rate generally considered necessary to reduce poverty and improve food security.

Despite this, the sector's contribution to GDP has stagnated despite land expansion accounting for most agricultural growth. This observation signals potentially combined effects from climate change and current agricultural practices. As a consequence of these factors, it is likely that the private sector will not be able to meet its potential in terms of output and employment targets without employing new technologies and approaches.

The agricultural sector will not be able to attain its growth, social, and employment targets without employing new technologies and approaches. The private sector should contribute to better management of the potential impacts of climate change on agriculture, including those related to water availability, soil quality, environment and related ecosystems. Considering the high reliance of economic growth on agriculture, it is imperative to better manage ecological impacts of current agriculture intensification and transformation.

The agricultural sector will not be able to attain its growth, social, and employment targets without employing new technologies and approaches, such as technological upgrades and changes in farming practices, have a huge potential to harmonize the production of food with environmental sustainability. These include reducing emissions of greenhouse gas and other negative environmental impacts. Other such benefits include increased productivity (with attendant mitigation benefits), yield, and livelihoods.

Despite its growing importance in the economic development of Tanzania, the private financial sector still plays a limited role in the agricultural sector. This is partly due to the fact that farmers are not only exposed to high production risks but also the growing risk induced by climate variability and change. As an example, in neighboring countries such as Kenya and Uganda, where the majority of smallholder farmers operating in the agricultural sector were related to deficient production due to extreme weather events. Climate risk thus poses a two-fold intertwined consequence on the participation of the private sector in agricultural finance. First, it increases the financial risks associated with lending activities in agriculture, making them risky and therefore more profitable sectors (real estate, retail, etc.). As a consequence, fewer financial actors are willing to invest in agricultural finance. Second, the high costs of credit for farmers operating in the agricultural sector have to account for the higher risk associated with farming activities (e.g., production risks, climate change). This is further exacerbated by the lack of access to low interest rates and the low creditworthiness of farmers (when credit history is even available) limit the capacity of those in the agriculture sector to access short- to long-term credit, contributing to stagnation and decline in agricultural activity in the sector. Due to the lack of investment, leading to a downward vicious economic cycle.

## B

Agriculture in Tanzania is primarily a price-taker in regional and global markets because of its small size and its trading exchange rate. In addition, the applicable fiscal policies are mostly set outside agriculture, leaving little title use to domestic price polices alone to affect agricultural incentives. Due to the low productivity levels, the sector has been unable to compete with imports from neighboring countries. This is expected and necessary. However, there is an assumption that, in some areas, such as subsidies for fertilizer and fuel, these policies may be undermining competition. This increases the need for the consideration of a progressive shift of public spending on agriculture towards providing private incentives towards core public goods, in order to catalyze sustainable private investments and provide support towards core public goods, in order to catalyze sustainable private investments and provide support towards core public goods.

This programme will provide such positive incentives and advocate for the reduction of regulatory barriers and distortions that are currently hindering private sector investment. The project will also promote higher quality products through effective adaptation measures and the strengthening of the entire value chain, including post-harvest handling and storage facilities. This will be achieved with controlled rebound effect.

The implementation of this project will also incentivize the removal of policy distortions and barriers to trade that discourage private sector investment while making land tenure more secure for smallholder farmers. The project will also provide a substantial base for agri-food based tax revenue needed to support governmental revenue collection and will be implemented through a strong gender lens, ensuring specific targets for women farmers groups and female agriculturalists.

The comprehensive stakeholders' engagement survey undertaken as part of the project preparation identified additional gaps. Major barriers and risks associated with this programme are summarized in Table 8.1.1: Major Barriers and Risks

### Table 8.1.1: Major Barriers and Risks

#### Financial/Credit
- Regulated and perceived risk among financial institutions of lending and engaging with the agriculture sector including lack of collateral.
- Lack of awareness/motivation to engage with the wide side of private actors responsible for managing the majority of agricultural land in Tanzania.
- Lack of tailored climate investment instruments.

#### Regulatory Barriers
- Regulatory barriers limiting the extent of private sector engagement in agriculture investments and lending which is a major barrier to private sector investment.
- Weakness of financial resources for strategic investment, development, and project formulation.

#### Knowledge/Information
- Lack of climate risk and vulnerability knowledge among end-users in the agriculture sector as well as by financial institutions. Lack of available climate risk data and analysis at the macro and micro level to support and direct climate investments.
- Gaps in knowledge/skills needed for 21st-century-compatible, transformational design goals.
- Lack of climate risk and vulnerability knowledge among end-users in the agriculture sector as well as by financial institutions. Lack of available climate risk data and analysis at the macro and micro level to support and direct climate investments.

#### Production
- Limited investment in water harvesting and storage system for irrigation.
- Shifting cropping calendars that are unpredictable to farmers.
- Poor crop protection surveillance, early warning systems.
- Droughts leading to soil erosion, erosive runoff, deforestation, and unsustainable farming practices.

## Sector characteristics and market dynamics
The considerable growth of Tanzanian agriculture, especially since 2008, has been primarily due to the rapid expansion of cropped areas rather than production increases. This expansion is largely driven by deforestation, erosion, and inadequate fertile land responsible for the degradation of more than 60% of the land presently used for the production of crops, livestock, and forest products and services, and to provide food security in Tanzania.

The effects of climate risks and attendant lack of adaptation in the agricultural sector are detrimental to the achievement of national development plans. The government has recognized this as a critical nature of agricultural transformation for the success of the national development plans and to achieve sustainable development goals (SDGs).

According to the 2016/2017 Annual Agriculture Sample Survey (AASS)², there is an estimated 8,763,267 farm operators in Tanzania, out of which 19.5% (1,756,235) are officially registered. Moreover, 56% of these operators are engaged in growing crops, only 2% in livestock, and the remaining 42% in both crops and livestock. The majority of farm operators are small-scale enterprises, small-and-medium-sized enterprises, or larger corporations), their support services are primarily provided by informal service providers. The increasing commercialization of agriculture sector is manifested through an increase in the number of medium-size farms, which are estimated to be 10% of all farms.

Tanzania revealed an increasingly commercial orientation to crop production, with farmers marketing a larger share of their crops. Between 2005 and 2017, these operators rose from 38 to


---

---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 19 OF 78

#### B

41%, which represents a greater penetration of traders into villages and improved market access for crop farmers. Yet, despite its agricultural resources and market opportunities, Tanzania itself is a major importer of foodstuffs having bought nearly 3.5 million metric tons (MMT) of maize, wheat, and rice in 2017. These dynamics highlight the need to support the sector with climate-smart agriculture, upholding sustainability, climate change adaptation, and resilience as the cornerstone for future growth.

CRDB Bank's product offerings largely target agricultural development and modernization and there is limited overlap with adaptation and climate resilience. There is a climate capacity gap and a need for support in building the technical capacity of financial institutions to better understand how to further CRDB Bank’s commitment to becoming a leading bank with established climate and sustainability practices. The proposed program will seek to transform the country's agricultural sector while building its resilience to climatic variability and the adverse effects of climate change. In doing so it will strengthen the financial and technical capacity of both the financial sector (including CRDB Bank) and the agricultural sector, which is currently underdeveloped in terms of access to finance and support for adaptation and mitigation related sectors as well. The Technology and Practice Needs Assessment (TPNA) prepared under the Project Preparation Facility (PPF) for the Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Designation (TACAD) project identified 12 agriculture practices and technologies that are climate-smart and will support resilience building and adaptation of Tanzanian agriculture.

The TPNA also found significant capacity gaps and room for alignment between CRDB Bank's portfolio and Tanzania’s national agricultural priorities. The analysis showed that the CRDB Bank portfolio found that none of the 12 agriculture techniques identified in CRDB Bank's portfolio for current lending align with the ASPF. Additionally, there are 64 agriculture techniques and technologies which do not align with the ASPF but have been identified as climate-smart by the Climate-Smart Agriculture Strategy (irrigation, drip irrigation equipment, and fishing gear) and 3 align with ASPF (irrigation, drip irrigation equipment, and fencing equipment). The TPNA also found that there are 64 agricultural techniques and practices which can be incorporated into CRDB Bank's portfolio to advance and align with Tanzania’s national agriculture, adaptation, and resilience goals.

#### B.2 Theory of change (max. 1000 words; approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

The proposed program will seek to transform the country's agricultural sector while building its resilience to climatic variability and the adverse effects of climate change. In doing so it will strengthen the financial and technical capacity of both the financial sector (including CRDB Bank) and the agricultural sector, which is currently underdeveloped in terms of access to finance and support for adaptation and mitigation related sectors as well. From 2006 through 2016, the agriculture sector experienced an average 3.5% annual growth rate compared to Tanzania’s overall national GDP growing 6% annual growth.

Theories:

If the proposed programme leverages both CCF and additional private climate finance targeted at tackling the adverse effects of climate change in the domestic agricultural sector, then the Tanzanian government will have a greater ability to support the sector with adaptation measures and mitigation co-benefits, thus increasing the share of climate-compatible investments in the agriculture sector.

### Activities

#### Component 1: Policy Development and Institutional Reforms

- Activity 1.1: Support the development of a national climate change policy framework.
- Activity 1.2: Develop and implement a national MRV system for GHG ARA using existing tools and methodologies.
- Activity 1.3: Support the implementation of the National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS) and the National Adaptation Plan (NAP).
- Activity 1.4: Support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy.

#### Component 2: Policy Development and Technical Assistance to advance the development of carbon markets and climate resilient agriculture technologies

- Activity 2.1: Develop and implement a national MRV system for GHG ARA using existing tools and methodologies.
- Activity 2.2: Support the implementation of the National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy (NCCAS) and the National Adaptation Plan (NAP).
- Activity 2.3: Support the development of a national climate change adaptation strategy.

### Barriers & Risks

- Financial constraints in implementing climate change mitigation and adaptation activities.
- Lack of institutional capacity to implement climate change mitigation and adaptation activities, including lack of awareness among key stakeholders about the importance of climate change mitigation and adaptation strategies.
- Governmental policies that are not supportive of climate change mitigation and adaptation efforts.

### Assumptions

- There will be global climate hazards across all or many countries in the region, affecting agricultural sectors.
- There will be continued interest and demand for climate mitigation and adaptation interventions by key stakeholders.

The introduction of tailored agriculture adaptation financial instruments, the establishment of a dedicated climate change fund, and innovative agricultural lending operations will facilitate the development of climate-smart technologies and tools, providing key stakeholders technical assistance and capacity building will contribute to a paradigm shift in Tanzania’s financial and agricultural sector towards the prioritization of climate-resilient investments. This will help address maladaptation from spontaneous adaptation efforts by financial institutions, agribusinesses, and farmers. The programme aims to increase the adoption rate of climate-smart technologies that offer higher returns, avoided greenhouse gas emissions, and the advancement of sustainable development goals. It will also support the integration of climate change considerations into the decision-making process for agricultural lending through the use of science-based climate risk assessments. This will enable financial institutions to make informed decisions on private investment in climate change adaptation and resilience measures in the sector.

The programme aims to create a national market for adaptation products and services, contribute to rapidly evolving resilience needs, and support Tanzania’s economic growth and climate-resilient development.

B.3 Project/programme description (max 200 words; approximately 1 page)

The share of CRDB Bank in the Tanzanian agriculture sector is very significant, comprising approximately 45% of total CBT commercial lending to the agriculture sector over the past five years. The agricultural lending portfolio of CRDB Bank as at end-December 2020 comprised 38% of CRDB Bank’s total lending portfolio in 2020 directed towards the agriculture sector (23% when not including loans to agribusinesses). This is a significant increase from approximately 19% of CRDB Bank’s portfolio in 2015 to 13% in 2020, and CRDB Bank’s total lending towards agriculture has increased by approximately 48% over the same period.

Agriculture is a high-risk sector, owing to its susceptibility with climatic variability and the interconnectedness with ensuring climate risks. Climate change presents cascading risks through the loss of livelihoods, food security, and economic growth, which in turn have significant implications for the country’s growth potential and overall development outlook.

In order to avoid further drops in an important sector and restore performance, proactive management strategies are required. The key challenges that need to be addressed include: i) CRDB Bank’s agricultural lending portfolio and consequently farmers’ revenues and national food security; ii) the lack of adoption of adaptation technologies that are: a) most suited to local contexts, b) catered to effective climate change risk assessments, c) cost-effective, d) have high returns, e) have demonstrated market demand and high revenue generation potential. Some of these technologies can require high upfront costs and hence the need to promote their acquisition through financial instruments that are tailored for agricultural adaptation measures. Even those adaptation technologies that are cheaper remain relatively expensive, and since most farmers in Tanzania do not have access to credit facilities, they cannot afford to purchase them. Further, farmer operators remain creditworthy keeping under control credit needed for agricultrual activities as well as additional investments required to resently increasing their cropping system yield.

By integrating climate risk assessments into agricultural lending operations and establishing a dedicated climate change fund for agribusiness investment, this programme will facilitate the deployment of innovative financial instruments that are tailored towards the adoption of technologies to boost resilience to climate change. Innovative financial instruments will be informed by science-based climate risk assessments to reduce the climate risks of investments and therefore create value for investors. The Green Climate Fund (GCF) through its implementing partner CRDB Bank will establish a facility dedicated to resilient and innovative financial instruments to facilitate farmers, communities, and other key stakeholders’ investments in cost-effective adaptive solutions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 22 OF 78

## B

To build up the resilience of the agriculture sector, TACAF is expected to increase the adaptive capacity and enhance the resilience of vulnerable farmers to climate change. This programme provides farm operators with affordable credit to invest in productive assets to combat recent rapid drying and warming trends, especially those observed in the last decade. The investment can be made in a wide range of technologies such as micro-harvesting, solar pumps, and land restoration; for example, will protect farmers against extreme weather conditions like droughts and floods. In addition, this programme also protects farmers from lost income and provides food security.

The facility will address the need for (i) seasonal operational expenditure and short- to medium-term adaptation investments, such as micro-harvesting, solar pumps, and land restoration; (ii) fixed asset capital or capital expenditure for cost-effective adaptive technologies. The pricing structure and improved climate risk management tools will allow farmers to access affordable credit for assets and productive investments in farm operations. Indeed, it is anticipated that the financial products, coupling long-term adaptation investment with short-term productive loans, will reduce the impact of weather-related shocks on farm operators by allowing them to diversify their income sources while also allowing lenders to expand access to credit among farm operators and consequently reduce interest rates.

The facility will be based on a portfolio of technologies identified through robust decision-making methods accounting for a large number of parameters such as climate, soil type, crop choice, and socio-economic conditions. In other words, once borrowers have invested in new adaptive technologies and begin repaying their loans, they can continue to access credit at lower interest rates, thereby ensuring continued funding flow to climate resilient agriculture overall. The product offerings as well as their pricing will also be designed to guarantee affordability for different types of farmers and cost-effectiveness for both parties.

The first draft of the market study being finalized for this programme includes a huge appetite among all categories of farm operators for the solutions envisaged as part of this program. Further insight into the potential demand is provided in the annexes of the elaboration of the Funding Proposal. A non-exhaustive list of eligible adaptation measures is as follows:

| Technologies & practices needed | Related Climate Hazards/Risks |
|---|---|
| Access to water and irrigation | All: drought, extreme heat, excess rainfall leading to flooding |
| Advanced modern/undercover growing solutions, including drip irrigation | Extreme heat primarily |
| Agrometeorological services | All: primary seasonal shifting due to uncertainty in precipitation timing and quantity, and planting optimization. Drought, extreme heat, excess rainfall (leading to flooding, mudslides, etc.) |
| Commercial valorization of microorganisms as substitute for or in diminution of mineral N fertilizer and pesticides | All |
| Crop management technologies | All |
| Digital farming systems | All: especially excess rainfall, which leads to flooding |
| Efficient water use through artificial run-off reduction & drainage measures | Drought, extreme heat, excess rainfall |

## Programme Components

This programme is being developed through the implementation of 2 components. One first component comprises 3 interrelated activities to support the deployment of several blended financial instruments, including a dedicated credit line for smallholder farmers in Tanzania and an insurance scheme for smallholder farmers – to de-risk agricultural investments by offering new financing options that are more affordable than existing ones and to create a viable market for adaptation technologies in the URT. In addition, a second component of technical assistance will be provided to support the implementation to complement and support Component 1 interventions. The two components are as follows:

### Component 1: Innovative financial products for adaptation and climate resilient agriculture technologies including:

#### Activity 1.1: Establishment of a dedicated credit line for CRDB ARA lending operations

Objective: To ensure that the sector is led by the leading economic sector in Tanzania, yet it remains highly vulnerable to climate change and with limited capacity to implement critical adaptation and resilience measures. The objective will be achieved through leveraging USD 100 million from financial institutions due to associated risks and high costs financing for sector participants. With GCF funding, CRDB Bank will be able to scale up its lending appetite for adaptation agriculture at relatively low cost compared to the current market interest rates, which are currently in practice to modern and environmentally friendly farming.

#### Agriculture Resilience and Adaptation (ARA) activities will be financed through the USD 170 million loan from GCF. CRDB Bank, by providing senior debt to the programme, will enable CRDB Bank to leverage USD 100 million from financial institutions. The total amount of financing is USD 70 million from GCF which is thus unlocking USD 100 million from CRDB Bank to be extended in ARAs loans to smallholder farmers and other actors in the agricultural sector. This will enable larger corporates (estimated at approximately 1% of expected borrowers under the TACATCP). Loans will be directly provided by CRDB Bank to end-users in the agriculture sector: smallholders including women, youth, and vulnerable groups; cooperatives; and agribusinesses and farm operators. CRDB Bank will be responsible for the ARA lending products development and pricing, credit risk management, loan disbursement, monitoring and reporting of loans through CRDB Bank directly. Aara loan monitoring, Aara loan repayment by farmers to CRDB Bank and other financial institutions will be facilitated through a single blended resource (i.e., GCF proceeds and CRDB co-financing blended as a single tranche loan).

CRDB Bank's adaptation credit will be used to:

- Introduce innovative, simplified, and affordable financial products tailored to increasing farmers' access to finance for climate-resilient agricultural practices and sustainable services.
- Increase financial inclusion for smallholder farmers (as an estimated 31% of smallholder farmers have access to formal credit in Tanzania), helping to make high-cost technologies more accessible. This will include providing access to affordable financing, particularly in rural areas, thus boosting smallholder farmers' productivity.
- Build the capacity of CRDB Bank and other financial institutions to provide blended resources (i.e., GCF proceeds and CRDB co-financing blended as a single tranche loan).

### Activity 1.2: Establish and implement a dedicated ARA Guarantee Credit Facility.

**Objective:** The objective of Activity 1.2 is to support the operationalization of a guarantee credit-enhancement facility for smallholder farmers and agricultural SMEs in Tanzania, supporting their resilience and adaptation lending, and to spur financial institutions' participation in the program.

GCF is being requested to provide the initial capital and liquidity for a USD 10 million guarantee credit enhancement facility (hereinafter referred to as the “Facility”) that will support smallholder farmers and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) engaged in the agriculture sector with risk sharing in the ARA lending program. The Facility is expected to be operationalized by April 2023.

The Facility is not subject to conflicts or other potential risks and to create an information barrier and transparency for all parties involved. The sub-tasks are to partially guarantee by GCF under Activity 1.2 a pool of loans provided by financial institutions in the ARA lending program.

With the establishment of a guarantee credit-enhancement facility to help de-risk smallholder farmers and agricultural SMEs, it is expected that the uptake of ARA lending will increase. Such a facility will provide a partial credit risk guarantee to cover the losses with an estimated ratio of 50% of losses (up to 60% if losses are women). As evidenced by decreasing and increasing interest rates in Tanzania, financial institutions have been reluctant to lend to smallholder farmers due to elevated interest rates and higher collateral requirements that preclude them from accessing credit. The guarantee credit-enhancement facility is an essential feature of the program, as it will help CRDB Bank overcome several barriers to lending to smallholder farmers and agricultural SMEs in Tanzania.

The risks of borrowers which lead to elevated interest rates and higher collateral requirements that preclude them from accessing credit are addressed by the guarantee credit-enhancement facility. This will address these barriers and help unlock private investments for ARA lending. The beneficiaries are local farmers, individually and through AMCOG, micro-enterprises, SMEs, and corporates across the agriculture value-chain that will realize greater available credit.

### Activity 1.3: Establishment of an insurance scheme dedicated to ARA for smallholder farmers.

**Objective:** The objective of Activity 1.3 is to promote the creation of an innovative insurance scheme in Tanzania that will provide coverage on smallholder farmers. As has been piloted in other markets, “index” or “parametric” insurance products are a relatively new instrument for the agriculture sector and have not yet been widely adopted by financial institutions in Tanzania. The objective of this activity is to work with CRDB Bank and other insurance companies (there are a limited number of local crop insurance providers in Tanzania) to conduct all necessary studies as a first step to launching a new

### Product Design Objective of these studies is to identify and refine potential targeted recipients and insurance policies, and the pilot seeks to reduce the vulnerability of smallholder farmers to climate change by protecting their incomes and overall livelihoods through an affordable insurance scheme.

As noted in the previous section, the study area is located in the northern part of Tanzania. There are increasing temperatures and unpredictable rainfall patterns in some parts of the country, which have a significant impact on crop yields and food security for smallholder farmers. This question. Depending on the key crops which will be the main funding beneficiaries (the emphasis of the TACATDP is on food crops as opposed to cash crops). CRDB will design a new climate insurance scheme, based on the lessons learned from the existing Tanzania Agricultural Risk Insurance (TARI) product and launch a pilot phase in conjunction with the lending program. This component will be supported by GCF funding. The pilot will be implemented through the TACATDP, which is part of the insurance program, and cost-sharing for the insurance premiums (at a cash-on-payment ratio to be determined between CRDB and the GCF) will be provided to smallholder farmers in the study region (Tanzania). CRDB will finalise and approve the cost-sharing mechanism (premium subsidies) after integration with TACATDP.

There is no insurance scheme or insurance mechanisms dedicated to strengthening and targeting RRA activities currently in Tanzania. TACATDP includes grants from GCF to support the implementation of a climate risk insurance pilot for smallholder farmers. The eligible beneficiaries under Activity 1.3, which will access the premium subsidies under the insurance scheme, are limited to the sub-borrowers selected under Activities 1.1 and 1.2. The TACATDP steering committee (TACATDP SC) in collaboration with CRDB will have final approval over the selection of targeted beneficiaries to access this insurance scheme.

To establish the scheme and prove its sustainability, a GCF grant will be used to pilot the initiative, serving as a "market maker" to induce insurance providers to extend climate-related index or parametric insurance products to smallholder farmers. The premium subsidy for the insurance transfer mechanism is directed towards the borrowers (i.e., smallholder farmers) and is meant as a way of being able to access credit from CRDB and which would otherwise be unable to do under a scenario in which their food crops were not being currently insured, and which would have been unable to repay their loans. CRDB is still developing a more robust business model, which requires further discussions with partners and the market studies, and a more granular view on the flow of funds regarding the use of GCF grant funding for this pilot phase. The aim will be to make the insurance programme become sustainable, scalable, and commercially independent in the long term.

The main crops to benefit from a new form of index or parametric insurance could be selected based on the following criteria: they should be considered the main food crops, but cassava and beans as well are good export crops, all of which are functioning as cash crops. The number of farmers who will need to be established (this means a large enough number of smallholder farmers who would become eligible for insurance) is estimated at 10,000 in the study region in Tanzania, perhaps in the northern part that faces increasing droughts and greater food insecurity.

CRDB plans to launch the five-year insurance pilot by offering a 100 percent premium subsidy to eligible beneficiaries in the first year beneficiaries sign up. In doing so, CRDB hopes to encourage high participation rates among smallholder farmers. The GCF grant will be used to provide premium subsidies, gradually reducing the ratio by 15 percent over the last four years of the pilot. For example, if a farmer pays $20 for insurance in year one, he would pay $3 per year from year two onwards and CRDB would be responsible for the remaining 15 percent through cash contributions. Altogether, the loans made to farmers will help promote food security and more income through savings in ARRA initiatives while climate insurance provides protection against risks and financial benefits. However, a digressive approach to the insurance pilot is part of an exit strategy aiming to empower beneficiaries with the

## Typology of beneficiaries of the innovative financial instruments

The beneficiaries of Component I are local farmers and communities that will realize greater available credit.

Further, Agricultural and Marketing Cooperative Societies (AMCOS) are agriculture marketing cooperatives societies. All members are engaged in Agriculture with similar crop or activities joining for better management of their resources. AMCOS is a cooperative society which has its own management that is responsible and answerable to members of the particular AMCS. AMCOS are their own legal entities, registered under the Companies Act 1956. The AMCOS are regulated by AMCOS are highly regulated by The Registrar of Cooperatives. The AMCOS have formal designations from the Registrar of Cooperatives. AMCOS has a board of directors and a chairman who is elected by board of from 7 to 10 board members. All the decisions regarding AMCOS operations, including but not limited to financial matters are made at the Annual General Meeting (AGM). The AMCOS need to get clearance from Registrar of Cooperatives before they can start their business.

AMCOS are normally issued with Maximum Limitation Certificates (MLC) which states the amount to be borrowed by each member. AMCOS is a formalized structure and has its own management team as an essential aggregation point for smallholder farmers dealing with various strategic crops in the country. The AMCOS can also be used as a platform for other financial services such as savings, loans, insurance, etc., which are offered by banks or other financial institutions.

## Component 2: Policy interventions and Technical Assistance to advance ARA in Tanzania

Activities will be financed by a GCF grant of USD 7,034 million. This grant will be managed and monitored directly by CRDB Bank through its Programme Management Unit (PMU).

**Beneficiaries:** The beneficiaries of this component are CRDB Bank, the Government of Tanzania, other financial institutions, AMCOs, NGOs, associated farmers, and overall local communities.

**Objective:** The objective of Component 2 is to facilitate the implementation of the entire Programme and the achievement of its goals. This will be achieved by providing a comprehensive technical assistance package to provide all segments of the agricultural value chain with services to support climate adaptation.

Overview: This comprehensive targeted TA comprises five (5) capacity building sub-activities and five (5) diagnostic sub-activities. The TA is designed to build the capacity of CRDB Bank, Government, and other stakeholders in Tanzania to develop and implement climate-compatible agribusinesses and promote the large adoption of climate-compatible adaptation solutions. CRDB Bank, the Government and the other stakeholders will be given the opportunity to innovate and scale up their efforts by providing access to innovative financial products, including agricultural financing, increasing agriculture. Capacity-building activities will include upgrading the CRDB Bank’s capacity for climate risk assessments and integrating climate risk management within their agricultural lending portfolio.

The TA will enable the Government of Tanzania via governmental agencies to deepen its understanding of climate change impacts on agriculture in Tanzania through the provision of updated analysis, knowledge, and insights. Strengthened by this experience and evidence, the Government can then engage with other stakeholders (e.g., private sector) to diagnose climate-related constraints with agricultural investments, investigate opportunities for profitable investments in the sector, and incentivize greater investment in climate-compatible agribusinesses.

CRDB will be the Executing Entity for the Technical Assistance and will procure the services through third parties' partners competitively selected in line with CRDB procurement policy.

Following are the sub-activities of the targeted technical assistance:

**Activity 2.1: Integration of climate risks assessments in CRDB Bank lending operations through the development of a climate risk management interface and training support for the CRDB Bank to use, maintain and upgrade the tool overtime.**

## TECHNOLOGICAL INNOVATION WILL BE AT THE CENTER OF THIS PROJECT TO SUPPORT THE TRANSFORMATION OF THE AGRICULTURE SECTOR TO ADDRESS CLIMATE CHANGE.

Indeed, facing climate challenges requires globally innovative solutions, especially in the context of limited access to finance for financial institutions. To account for the results of climate-crop modeling, associated risks, and an impacts assessment tool for prioritization of adaptation measures in agriculture will be designed to help CRDB identify and manage climate-related risk exposure in their agriculture loan portfolio and credit products.

The state-of-the-art analytical tools will be integrated into a web-based platform that will allow the bank to assess climate risk exposure in their agriculture loan portfolio and will reflect the climate risk associated with bank counterparty. The tool, integrated within the CRDB’s existing lending process, will help the bank identify risks, manage and respond to risks (See Figure B.2 for examples of assessments to be performed).

The Decision Support System (DSS) is a web-based platform that will provide decision support tools that should lead to a complete overhaul of the way agriculture is undertaken in Tanzania. These tools are designed with the specific needs of smallholder farmers and the agribusiness sector in Tanzania, the financing for the sector, as described below, as well as the ways associated risks will be managed at the design, implementation, and capitalization stage. Such institutional arrangements will include:

- **Mainstreaming robust decision-making for agriculture:** Agricultural planning is crippled with uncertainties such as the future climate, the reaction of crops to temperature or water stress, commodity prices, etc., which makes it difficult for farmers to plan their activities in a sustainable and resilient options. The Online Interface implements a high-resolution adaptation model built using state-of-the-art technology that will be calibrated and validated for Tanzania and calibrated for Tanzania to enable the robust prioritization of adaptation options specific to various types of crops, the timeline of the intervention, and the exact location of the farm.

- **Demonstrating climate-smart farming:** After the calibration and validation by CRDB Bank, rural agriculture advisors and extensions services providers as well as local research institutions in Tanzania, the results will be made available to participating cooperatives and cooperative banks. User-friendly tools will be developed for farmers (see Table B.1) and extension service providers (see table) to all offices. With the new developed app, CRDB Bank, its sub-lenders, and other stakeholders can use these tools.

To estimate farmer’s climate risk rating depending on their location, type of crops and the management practices already implemented by the farmers.
- To provide a set of recommendations for adaptation options that will improve productivity (including reducing the water yield gap) and increase resilience to climate-related shocks. These recommendations are based on the best option of those offered by the UCP.
- To re-evaluate farmer’s climate risk rating in light of the implemented adaptation and resilience effects of the deployed technology.

- **Expanding and/or deploying innovative adaptation technologies:** The Online Interface will include state-of-the-art strategies and practices for improved natural resources management (including water) and agricultural resilience. Some technologies are already available, such as drip irrigation or precision agriculture techniques (such as Zai), while some are proven technologies that are relatively uncommon and underutilized in Tanzania, such as the use of intercropping to reduce soil erosion in controlled climates, devices to improve resilience towards strong winds and insects). In addition, the model will provide a set of recommendations for adaptation options appropriate considering local demand, among other factors on the ground. The model will assess the relevance of the measures to the specific farm context (crop, geography, etc.). The farmers’ constraints will be identified through participatory workshops with the support of local public or private extension services.

## Financial Services Provider based in Dar Es Salaam in the United Republic of Tanzania with presence in Burundi. CRDB Bank's history can be traced back to when it was established in 1947 as Land Bank of Tanganyika, later renamed as Tanganyika Development Bank (TDB). TDB merged with Co-operative and Rural Development Bank (CRDB). CRDB was privatized in 1996 and listed on the Dar Es Salaam Stock Exchange in 2009. Over 70% of shareholding stock belongs to local Tanzanians. CRDB is a member of the African Development Bank Group and International Finance Corporation. CRDB extends short, medium, and long-term loans, working capital and purchasing credit for agriculture, industry, trade, and infrastructure development in Tanzania since 2000. CRDB's total loans and advances stood at USD $1,653,766,027.39 in diversified sectors that are aligned with the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals. CRDB also sides a 1% of its net profit as a Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) fund to invest in education, health, and protecting the environment.

CRDB does not have extensive experience on adaptation technologies. However, the purpose of TACATISP is to equip CRDB with the financial and non-financial (TA capacity building) resources to offer more ambitious and innovative adaptation technologies, especially to smallholder farmers, who typically lack access to finance and other inputs. The project will be implemented in three phases: engage several key government agencies and third-party service providers mentioned below to help facilitate certain activities under TACATISP.

CRDB Bank will disburse funding (received from the GCF according to the detailed disbursement schedule) through direct payments to end beneficiaries using the most appropriate internal credit lines and processes.


Relevant CRDB Bank departments to be involved in the process, include the Sustainable Finance Unit (SFU), Credit Department, Corporate Banking Department, Retail Banking Department, and Department of Risk Compliance. All credit lines will be centrally managed by the SFU from CRDB Bank’s Board Credit Committee or the Board in line with CRDB Bank’s credit manual.


Credit lines: All credit lines will be centralized by the Retail Banking Department and the Corporate Banking Department processes; all credit lines pass to the centralized Credit Department for commercial review. The SFU will then forward the applications to the Credit Committee, which is forwarded through to the Bank’s Credit Management Meetings or to the Board-level for decision-making according to their recommendation.


Guarantee Facility: The guarantee facility will be managed by GCF with support from respective CRDB departments.


Insurance: CRDB currently operates as bank assurance agent midline oversight through the Retail Department. CRDB onboards customers and directs them to insurers and insurance companies that are approved by CRDB. CRDB does not have any arrangement or contract that requires any banks that operate insurance products must have bancassurance arrangement. All recruitments of customers is being done and will be done under the branch networks being overseen by the Retail Banking Department. The Retail Banking Department has a manager who reports to the Chief Commercial Officer and who also reports to the Group CEO and Managing Director.


The applications passes through branches credit committees, then forwarded to the Management Credit Committee at the headquarters. If limits exceeds Management level the same will be forwarded to the Board for decision-making.


At the prescribed actions are being under the surveillance under the Department of Risk and Compliance headed by the Director of Risk and Compliance who reports direct to the Group CEO and Managing Director.


Disbursement: Disbursements will be undertaken under the Credit Department, as supervised by the Director of Credit who reports to the Group CEO and Managing Director.
