B. Agriculture for GCF funding request (max. 1988 words, approximately 2 pages)

Agriculture is one of the sectors most affected by climate change in the United Republic of Tanzania. The Ministry of Agriculture and Food Security develops a policy and strategy to focus on climate change adaptation and mitigation measures that will enhance food security and livelihoods for rural households and shifting agricultural activities to low-carbon economy agriculture. However, the transformation toward climate-resilient agriculture is slow due to limited access to finance, lack of public resources allocated in large-scale infrastructure needed to fully transform the sector. The public resource allocation for agriculture has been declining over time while private investment in agriculture is increasing. The Government of Tanzania has ratified the National Agriculture Climate Resilient Plan that identifies key adaptation measures that are urgent to protect Tanzanian agriculture from the adverse effects of climate change.

This project focuses on adaptation measures in agriculture by implementing a number of activities that will offer adaptation and resilient solutions linked to increased smallholder farmers' productivity and income. These include: (i) promoting the use of improved seeds, (ii) improving access to credit for application of industrial fertilizers and pesticides, utilize properly certified seeds and contain post-harvest losses; (iii) increasing access to markets through value addition and marketing services such as GCF, and bilateral aid) to this project proposal will enable smooth implementation of activities that can be scaled up in the future. The Government of Tanzania has identified agriculture as one of the main sources of finance for the agriculture sector come from both public and private sources, but even though there is a need to increase public investment in agriculture, the availability of resources needed to ensure the sector's adaptation to climate change is almost non-existent. Regarding public sources, investments come basically from Government budget and international organizations.

Public expenditure: Although more than 70 % of Tanzanians depend directly or indirectly on agriculture for their livelihoods, agriculture is allocated only 2.5 % of public spending; and even then, 31.9 % of this allocation is for the Ministry of Agriculture and Food Security. In 2018, all funders worldwide committed $203.09 million in development finance to Tanzania for Agriculture, of which $64.7m (31.9%) was provided by GCF, $50.8m (24.9%) was provided as ODA grants, while $38.1m (19%) was provided in the form of ODA loans.

Private finance: The main sources of private finance are Foreign Direct Investments (FDI) and credit from local banks. Private agricultural investments have been modest, especially from foreign sources. In 2017, FDI to Tanzania reached $358 million, down by 49% compared with the previous year. And 2017 only 4 % of FDI went into agriculture, fisheries and forestry together. Commercial Banks are extending credit to the agricultural sector at a declining rate. The share of credit extended to agriculture averaged 17%, down from 17.5% in 2018. Commercial bank lending to agriculture dropped 7 % down from 63.9% in 2018. This decline is attributed to the increase in non-performing loans (NPLs). In 2019, there was growth of credit by 65.8 % to agriculture, with commercial banks share of total credit increasing to 47%. However, this growth is not enough to cover the outstanding progress. CRDB Bank's agriculture portfolio indicates an overall declining trend due to increased risks that could result potentially in more payment defaults.

Constrained Banking business model in Tanzania - One of the main challenge faced by the Banking sector in Tanzania is the constrained banking business model which limits access for credit provision and investment in government securities. As majority of Banks in Tanzania, CRDB experience short-term liquidity problems due to high demand for cash from rural households, Agri firms and the Government to protect agriculture and other assets from climate change impacts. Most of CRDB capital come from deposits from its clients. CRDB primarily business


---
**Page 46**
---

model such as mobilizing savings, and to a lesser extent, other costlier, short-term, interbank financing, and institutional deposit to intermediate credit, resulting in high cost of capital, operating costs, and interest rate.

It is clear from these financial indicators that there is a very significant finance gap to support the development of agriculture/residence of the sector agriculture in the 6th most populated country in Africa (24th most populated country in the world). Despite the growing contribution of the financial system to the financing of agricultural activities, the lack of access to credit remains an obstacle and the adaptation of the sector. GCF concessional loans are needed offer an affordable lending facility for smallholder farmers. The combination of short-term concessional loans with grants granted to smallholder farmers. In the absence of GCF concessionally, the combination of short-term concessional loans with grants is not feasible because it would be too expensive and would just unaffordable for farmers, and both the loans and CAPEX investment are needed for farmers. The GCF grant money is needed to finance a loan at a similar financing conditions as it has currently, with the expectation that the risks remain.

The GCF grant money is needed to finance a comprehensive targeted technical assistance package that will enable a successful implementation of the project and ensure its sustainability. This technical assistance package will be designed in close collaboration with the local partners and expertise while supporting the organization of facilitated dialogues with the staff of the bank, the partner organizations, and other stakeholders to identify the most pressing needs of the target population as beneficiaries. A recent analysis of mobile services and tools recently promoted in Eastern Africa shows that these services and tools are being widely deployed by a large part of the population as beneficiaries.

These emerging services and associated useful tools are being largely deployed such as: 1) financial services; 2) livestock inputs; 3) agriculture biotech inputs; 4) mechanization, including agriculture equipment; 5) robotics; 6) messenger services; 7) access to resources such as weather stations, sensing and internet of things); 9) agricultural demand; and 10) access to marketing networks. The adoption of these services clearly integrate climate change adaptation challenges that will need to be tackled sooner or later in the future. These services are also very important for smallholder farmers providing concessional resources for investments and technical assistance to fill the gaps of targeted financial products.

B. Exit strategy and sustainability (page 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The exit strategy for the GCF is a straightforward repayment of the loan by CRDB Bank over the twenty-year time frame or tenor of the loan. By investing in a robust climate risk assessment tool and building capacity to identify and implement climate-resilient agricultural solutions, CRDB lending portfolio, this project will significantly contribute to reducing the risks perception associated to this sector. The project will also support the development of a pipeline of innovative climate-compatible agriculture solutions and technologies and to identify business opportunity in this sector. The project will be implemented over 2019-2023 with an initial investment of USD 10 million USD alongside GCF proceeds under a facility dedicated specifically to concrete adaptation measures. The implementation of this project will enable the identification of new businesses opportunities and reduction of risks associated with climate change in agriculture. The successful implementation by CRDB Bank to increase the share of its portfolio for adaptation in agriculture. The experience of CRDB Bank will potentially attract other institutions of the banking sector in Tanzania and beyond, enabling them to replicate similar projects. This project will also have significant environmental benefits, including emissions reductions. At the same time, the integration between climate change adaptation measures and financial products, supported by both the technical / financial legal expertise of the target beneficiaries, will also create a sustainable conservation impact.

Agriculture in developing countries must undergo a significant transformation in order to meet the


---
**Page 47**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 41 OF 78

B

related challenges of achieving food security and responding to climate change, which is highly variable and complex, and climate trends already indicate that temperatures are rising, and rainfall is becoming more erratic. The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) reports indicate that agricultural production needs to increase by at least 70 per cent to meet demands by 2050. Most estimates also indicate that climate change is likely to reduce agricultural productivity, production and yields in many regions. This will be exacerbated if the projected increase in demand for food is due to weather-related risks already impacting the agriculture sector, and without urgent adaptation the impacts of climate change on food security could be catastrophic. The project is designed with a focus on addressing challenges of achieving food security and responding to climate change, which is highly variable and complex, and climate trends already indicate that temperatures are rising, and rainfall is becoming more erratic. The project is designed so that its activities and transformation between phases will be flexible.

1. Collaboration with partners and government agencies to institutionalize Climate Resilient Agriculture: This project strongly highlights the requirement for partnering with stakeholders that hold the paramount role to ensure that the Tanzanian society remain committed towards climate resilient agriculture. The project will develop a strategy, implementation plan and development objectives and road maps for activities implementation as well as reporting matrix to track progress.

2. Replicating and Scaling-up Long-term Solutions: The project has chosen to work on agricultural value chains in order to ensure that the Tanzanian society remains committed towards environmentally sustainable paths for economic growth, which will involve trade-offs. One trade-off is between short term gains and long term resilience measures. This project will focus on food security gains. Therefore, investing in long-term resiliency measures is vital for ensuring long-term sustainability of the agricultural sector. The project will also ensure that it contributes towards GHG emission reductions and sustainable development as core-benefits.

3. Institutionalizing Climate-resilient policy and procedures on climate change: This project will institutionalize policy and procedures on climate change in all its activities for institutions involved, extension workers, and beneficiary farmers regarding climate change adaptation and resilient approaches to ensure that the Tanzanian society remains committed towards climate resilience. This will ensure the sustainability of continuous adaptation and resilient to climate change.

4. Contribute to the GCF’s goal of increasing access to finance for developing countries funds and enhance its lending appetite to the sector to ensure maintenance and sustainability of the project in relation to other agriculture commodities.

Component 2 of the programme is the facilitation of technical assistance (TA), capacity building, and technology transfer to support the implementation of the objectives of the programme as well as the continued and long-term capacity of financial institutions and other stakeholders to provide all segments of the population with access to climate-compatible finance.

This comprehensive targeted TA comprises five (5) capacity building sub-activities and five (5) policy interventions. It has the potential to reduce the high risks of lending associated with climate-compatible agriculture investments in Tanzania, including those related to climate change vulnerability, risk management, and agribusinesses. Capacity-building activities will include upgrading the bank staff's skills to perform credit risk assessments and integrating climate risk management within their agricultural lending portfolio.

The TA will enable the Government of Tanzania via governmental agencies to deepen its understanding of the potential for profitable investments in agriculture that are not only climate-compatible but also resilient, inclusive, and insights. Strengthened by this experience and evidence, the Government of Tanzania can successfully and sustainably implement a range of strategies to address climate change vulnerability, including reducing climate-related constraints with agricultural investments, investigate opportunities for profitable investments in the sector, and mainstreaming green growth into national development strategies.

**Financing:** TA activities will be financed by a GCF grant of USD 7.04 million.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF Investment Policy.

## D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will contribute to climate change adaptation and resilience measures to address climate risks in the agriculture sector (cropping sector) in the face of current climate variability and future climate change over the project's operational life. Prioritized measures will ensure that beneficiary farmer operations will be more resilient to climate change impacts, including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, through application of climate scenarios based on IPCC representative concentration pathways (RCP) to ensure climate-resilient crop production systems. The project will also contribute to the achievement of SDG 2 by ensuring proposed measures will lock-in conditions that could result in maladaptation. Serious consideration is given to the following: (a) the potential for increased food insecurity and/or reduced food availability as it is understood as in the most recent IPCC 1.5 special report ensuring that proposed adaptation interventions are not likely to increase inequality or exacerbate existing inequalities due to poor design; (b) conflict with the adoption of other fragile systems (e.g., rainwater harvesting, water storage, etc.) and/or the implementation of climate change mitigation measures such as soil carbon sequestration, agroforestry, and reforestation; (c) adaptation or increased greenhouse gas emissions in the future. Rather adaptations measures to be prioritized will ensure that they are more resilient to climate change impacts, including increasing yields and productivity, reducing inequality due to poor design; (d) conflict with the adoption of other fragile systems (e.g., rainwater harvesting, water storage, etc.) and/or the implementation of climate change mitigation measures such as soil carbon sequestration, agroforestry, and reforestation. The project will contribute to SDG 13 by promoting adaptation that is more stable, productive (economic), increasing yields or productivity, reducing inequality due to poor design; increasing the percentage of production systems that are resilient to climate change impacts, including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events; reducing the percentage of areas planted to vulnerable crops; increasing the percentage of production systems that are resilient to climate change impacts, including increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events. The project will contribute to SDG 15 by promoting adaptation that is more stable, productive (economic), increasing yields or productivity, reducing inequality due to poor design; appropriateness of climate resilience interventions with the view to adjust as necessary to address evolving climate change impacts. The project will also contribute to SDG 2030 by promoting adaptation that is more stable, productive (economic), increasing yields or productivity, reducing inequality due to poor design; appropriateness of climate resilience interventions with the view to adjust as necessary to address evolving climate change impacts.

A total of 4,903 vulnerable people (4.67% of Tanzanians) will be supported with climate-resilient and adaptation practices and technologies.


## D.2 Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

By supporting the development and integration of climate risk assessments in CROB Bank agriculture lending portfolio and investing in the most optimal adaptation technologies, this project will have a huge transformative effect on the agricultural sector in Tanzania. The current approach to addressing climate change will soon be no longer suitable for current practices. This progressive shift of agriculture practices and technologies will also contribute to SDG 13 by promoting adaptation that is more stable, productive (economic), increasing yields or productivity, reducing inequality due to poor design; appropriateness of climate resilience interventions with the view to adjust as necessary to address evolving climate change impacts.

The project will make a strong case for the important role of the private sector, in this instance the banking sector, in a variety of ways. The project will contribute to SDG 17 by promoting adaptation that is more stable, productive (economic), increasing yields or productivity, reducing inequality due to poor design; mobilization of resources at scale to progressively and timely shift the most important economic sector of Tanzania from climate change vulnerability to resilience. This will be achieved through a combination of economic development to proceed in a sustainable manner. This project incorporates specific features that will ensure that it is more stable, productive (economic), increasing yields or productivity, reducing inequality due to poor design; appropriate including most advanced technological solutions, the development of a local ecosystem around the project, and the creation of an enabling environment for private sector investment. The project will also foster endogenous innovation and ease the adoption of optimum adaptation solutions. Moreover, the project will contribute to SDG 17 by promoting adaptation that is more stable, productive (economic), increasing yields or productivity, reducing inequality due to poor design; regulatory reforms as well as macroeconomic stimuli to support the refocus of limited public expenditures on public goods that will stimulate a greater involvement of private sector in the provision of private goods and services.


## D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 49 OF 78

#### Economic benefits:
The project will contribute to a significant increase in crop yields with high potential to lead to higher incomes for farmers. Reducing significant risks associated with loss of land (permanently or temporarily) through improved resource efficiency and optimization of the application of external inputs.

#### Social benefits:
The development and promotion of sustainable and adaptive agribusinesses will contribute to poverty reduction, food security, and nutrition by increasing access to markets and improving income generation. The project will also contribute to farmers' communities’ moral and psychological well-being and relieve pressures associated with economic hardship. It will reduce the incidence of child labor, improve the education level of women and youth, limit rural exodus, and reduce conflicts over land and other limited resources.

#### Environmental benefits:
The use of the best available technologies and inputs will minimize environmental degradation and pollution by reducing soil erosion, preserving natural resources, maintaining soil nutrient balance, reducing land degradation, reducing pollution, and improving water quality. The project will also contribute to climate change mitigation by sequestering more land or affecting the environment. These technologies will strengthen the optimal use of natural, social, and human assets, reduce the negative impact of food production on the environment.

#### Gender equity and women empowerment:
By taking a deliberate, proactive, and positive approach to gender equality in project design, implementation, and monitoring and evaluation, the project will provide opportunities for women’s and men's active participation. Through a network of microfinance partners, women farmers will have access to credit facilities that will enable them to increase their income from small-scale farm operations and women-led agribusinesses. The project will also support the development of gender-sensitive policies, strategies, technologies, farm adaptation activities, and improved livelihood strategies. They will be prioritized in the selection process for climate change adaptation projects. These technologies will help ease the time burden on women; burden they face in their productive capacities. The project will also reduce the burden of unpaid work that women and girls fall on as a result of climate change impacts. The project will further explore and implement an opportunity to address their access to and control over resources and their decision-making power within households, communities, and institutions from gender perspectives, including girls’ education and women’s engagement in economic diversification activities. The project will also ensure that at least 50% of the budget is spent on 50/50 gender balance of potential beneficiaries during project execution.

#### Skills development:
The project will support the development of skills and knowledge development around the integration of climate risk assessments in different components of the project, including the identification of adaptation strategies (at CRDS and its network of microfinance institutions) capacity to use risk assessment tools and to identify and implement appropriate adaptation activities. Farms and agribusiness operators as well as other stakeholders in the agriculture value chain will be supported in the prioritization and adoption of optimal adaptation technologies and practices, including the development of climate-resilient advisory services and activities within operations, and local communities will be trained in advanced advisory services and activities related to climate change mitigation and adaptation technologies, such as the manufacture of low-cost locally adapted technologies. The wide range of climate change adaptation strategies and technologies that are being developed for the fundamental transformation towards climate-resilient agriculture practices with immense benefits to smallholder farmers.

#### Support recovery from COVID-19:
As a consequence of the COVID-19 pandemic and the measures taken by governments, the global economy is expected to contract by 3.2% in 2020, growing to only by 3.8 percentage points in 2020, from 5.8% in 2019 to 2.0% in 2020. This proposed project will help the country back into more resilient economy. By investing US$ 200 million over five years and the next five years onwards, the facility will reduce the consequences not only of the current crisis but also future climate-related shocks. The project is expected to have a significant positive impact on increasing domestic food production from small- to large-holder farmers; the credit line is likely to improve

### D.4. Sources of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Following strong growth over the past decade, the World Bank announced in mid-2019 that Tanzania’s gross national income (GNI) per capita had crossed $1,000 for the first time, placing it among lower-middle-income status. However, the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic have led to a less certain economic outlook. The International Monetary Fund (IMF) projects Tanzania’s real GDP growth in 2020 at -3.5% compared with expected 2.0% in 2020 (compared to +5.8% in 2019). Reflecting tightening economic conditions, domestic credit growth slowed to 6.9% in the first half (H1) 2020 compared to 8.8% in H1 2019. In growth of private investment, there has been a decline from +35.4% in Q1 2019 to -7.5% in Q1 2020 due to global uncertainty, reduced capital inflows, and increased investor risk-aversion.

A continued negative demand shock in global markets could further undermine the Tanzanian economy. The decline in Tanzania’s main export partners (slower growth in Tanzania’s trading partners has already reduced demand and prices for Tanzanian exports) is expected to reduce Tanzania’s real GDP by 15% of Tanzania’s GDP), and lower activity in the hospitality and tourism sectors. In response to these developments, the government has announced a series of measures aimed at mitigating the impact on social spending in FY202/21, including social protections and support for small businesses. Coupled with a decrease in fiscal revenues by approximately 6% of total public expenditure, there is a projected increase in public debt to GDP ratio from 45% in 2019 to 53% in 2020. The government authorities have been in discussions with the IMF and external lenders for emergency financial support.

Tanzania, facing rising temperatures, declining rainfall, more frequent droughts, and a significant increase in extreme weather events, is highly vulnerable to climate change. According to the World Bank’s estimates show that climate change could cause GDP per capita to decrease by approximately 15% (in a moderate scenario) or up to 40% (in a high-emission scenario). The agricultural sector accounts for about 23% of Tanzania’s economy and employs more than half of its population. Agriculture is the country's dominant sector, generating 25% of the GDP and employing approximately 75-80% of households, including the majority of those in poverty. Climate change has already had a significant impact on the declining productivity of maize and sorghum and is inflicting seismic social impacts with farmers placing at greater emphasis on drought-tolerant crops and those with greater resilience to pests and diseases.

Over the past two decades, the sector has experienced relatively high growth, seeing its GDP share from 20% in 1995 to 34.7% in 2018. However, despite this progress, Tanzania’s agricultural sector suffers at least USD 200 million in annual losses due to weather-related risks – predominantly drought and floods. The World Bank has supported the government of Tanzania with a series of projects including the Project Preparation Facility (PPF) for the Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Development (TACATD) project, which aims to increase agricultural productivity by 20% in one-third of Tanzania’s economic growth potential – marked as the adaptive deficit. As agriculture in Tanzania is dominated by smallholder farmers – 80-90% of agricultural land is held by smallholder farmers – and 98%

## D

### of economic activities where rural Tanzanian women engaged in farming, decreasing sectoral productivity disproportionately impacts the country's most vulnerable populations.

As growth slows, budget allocations towards agriculture have similarly declined. According to Tanzania’s Ministry of Finance’s Citizen Budget, sectoral allocations to the agriculture, livestock, and fisheries sectors have decreased from 10% of total government spending in 2014/2015 to 7% in 2019/2020. This represents a decrease of Tanzanian shillings to 459.74 million Tanzanian shillings. However, private sector financing in agriculture, due to limited access to credit and financial services, is even more constrained than public sector financing compared to financing directed towards manufacturing, trade, and other service sectors. With limited credit availability, only about 10% of farmers have access to credit from banks or microfinance institutions, while the majority of farming households have access to credit and 48% of those involved in agribusinesses self-finance their agricultural activities. This is further compounded by a lack of infrastructure such as roads, irrigation systems, improved technology, and resilience efforts. This is reflected by the low adoption of agriculture technology, as seen by cultivation still predominantly done by hand here rather than by tractor (82% v. 14%).

Tanzania’s National Climate-Smart Agriculture (CSA) Guidelines provide recommended technologies and practices that can be adopted to increase agricultural productivity while addressing the interlinked challenges of food security and accelerating climate change. The guidance targets decision-makers, including farmers, extension workers, government officials, private sector actors, and financial institutions in the private sector, the primary intended users are district development planners and extension agents. However, despite the availability of CSA guidelines, there is a lack of institutional capacity to deliver these financial mechanisms, as both public and private funds are needed to secure and support CSA uptake. A significant challenge is that many of the recommended technologies and practices are not currently within CRDB’s portfolio and are not being adequately financed by other financial institutions. These include climate-smart agricultural practices such as soil storage, agroforestry practices and technologies; soil and water conservation practices and technologies; conservation agriculture practices and technologies; soil and water management practices and technologies; crop diversification practices and technologies.

While the Bank of Tanzania and the Government of Tanzania have taken steps in response to the COVID-19 pandemic to support domestic capital markets, including increasing the daily limits on mobile money transactions and extending moratoriums on loan repayments for commercial banks and microfinance institutions, there remains a significant need to spur investment in resilient, sustainable agriculture.

Tanzania’s climate context, risks, vulnerabilities, and needs are further addressed in section B.1. Further information on the socio-economic challenges and barriers to finance for the agricultural sector and potential solutions to these challenges is provided in section B.2. The institutional capacity required to build capacity is covered in sections B.1 and B.4. TACATDP will provide CRDB, the Government, and other stakeholders with the opportunity to enhance delivery mechanisms, systems, and products required for sustainable agriculture financing. This will be achieved through a combination of technical assistance (TA) for institutional capacity including upskilling CRDB staff on mainstreaming climate risks within its agriculture lending portfolio and providing training in climate-resilient agricultural technologies. Technical assistance (TA) delivered in Component 2 of the programme, and more details are in B.3 and E.6.

As previously announced, this programme is in direct alignment with Tanzania’s national strategies and goals, contributing to the advancement and achievement of Tanzania’s intended Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) under the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The proposal was first advanced and approved as a readiness programme, with significant contributions by several domestic actors, with a stakeholders’ relations and outreach. The technical assistance provided will support the development of a national climate change policy framework, including policy support which governmental and public authorities, aligned with the stated targets of the project. By supporting the development of this policy framework, the project will ensure that Tanzania receives technical assistance and in close collaboration with Tanzania’s NDCs, this program will endeavor to ensure that the country is able to track its progress towards meeting its climate change commitments under the Kyoto Protocol, the indicators that will be used to track them, and the deliverables are all listed in Section E.6.

Furthermore, marked by the support of from the Office of the Vice President of Tanzania, GCF’s National Determination Contributions (NDCs) and the development of a national climate change policy framework contributions to the implementation and achievement of the following guiding policy frameworks:

- **Tanzania’s INDC**: In Tanzania’s INDC, submitted in 2018. It is estimated that an immediate and start-up financing of about USD 150 million is needed to enhance the country’s adaptation capacity and increase its resilience through 2030, increasing this investment to USD 4 billion per year by 2030, and resilience through 2030, increasing up to USD 1 billion per year by 2030. With the INDC adoption, Tanzania will be able to access climate finance from international sources such as GCF and investors that this project will directly advance, supporting Tanzania’s progress against the climate investment needs.

- **Agriculture**: (A) Improving the level of improvement of agricultural land and water management; (B) increasing yields through inter alia climate-resilient agriculture; (C) Protecting smallholder farmers against climate-related shocks including crop failures; (D) Strengthening the capacity of Agriculture research institutions to conduct basic and applied research; (E) Strengthening knowledge, extension services and agricultural infrastructure to target smallholders.

- **Water Resources**: (A) Promoting integrated water resources development and management practices; (B) Investment in protection and conservation of water catchments including flood control and irrigation systems; (C) Promoting wastewater reuse and recycling technologies; and (D) Utilizing renewable energy for water resource resources. The programme will directly contribute to the achievement of the INDC.

- **Livestock**: (A) Adopting climate change resilient practices and modern knowledge on sustainable pasture and range management systems; (B) Enhancing livestock production infrastructure and services; (C) Promoting livelihood diversification of livestock keepers; and (D) Promoting climate-resilient livestock breeds.

- **National Climate Change Strategy**: The national climate change policy framework includes overarching strategic interventions and adaptation strategies to build upon the country’s National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA). It also provides a comprehensive set of short-term and long-term adaptation activities. Among those objectives described are: To invest and promote appropriate water management technologies; To invest in exploration and extraction of underground water resources; To improve access to clean drinking water; To increase agricultural productivity; To reduce the vulnerability of smallholder farmers against climate-related shocks including crop failures.

zones to promote appropriate agricultural practices that increase resilience to climate change. To promote use of appropriate technologies for production, processing, storage, and distribution.

* **Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan (ACRP), 2014-2019**<sup>1</sup>: Building off the National Climate Change Strategy, the ACRP is intended to provide Tanzania's crop agriculture sector and stakeholders with a comprehensive framework for climate change adaptation. The plan identifies adaptation options, such as improving agricultural land and water management; accelerating uptake of appropriate technologies (e.g., improved seeds, irrigation systems); enhancing disaster risk management; strengthening knowledge and systems to target climate action. The ACRP, however, has not yet been fully implemented due to limited funding and capacity constraints.

The project will contribute significantly towards achieving its resilience actions and investment priorities aiming at: (a) increasing the productivity of smallholder farmers in Tanzania's most vulnerable against climate shocks; and (b) strengthening knowledge and systems to target climate action.


* **Development Vision 2025**<sup>2</sup>: Tanzania’s Development Vision 2025 was developed to guide national economic and social development efforts through 2025, with the objective of transforming the country from a least developed country (LDC) to a middle-income country with a high degree of human capital. The vision envisions a diversified economy based on agriculture, industry, and an agricultural-based economy to a diversified and semi-industrialized economy with a modern rural sector that is characterized by increased productivity, competitiveness, and efficiency. This vision ensures food security and food self-sufficiency. The diversification of the economy is essential for reducing poverty and improving living standards in Tanzania. Agriculture remains the largest industry (particularly agro-industries). The Development Vision 2025 includes five key pillars: (a) education and skills; (b) infrastructure development; (c) environment, health, and safety; (d) educated and skilled society; A competitive economy capable of producing sustainable growth and shared benefits. This vision sees the need to address climate change within each of these strategic pillars. The vision also recognizes that Tanzania’s agricultural sector is underperforming, incorporating and expanding upon highly-productive agricultural activities. As today, however, Tanzania's agriculture sector continues to be dominated by smallholder farmers. According to forth by Development Vision 2025 Agriculture, the backbone of the economy, continues to be largely rain-fed with a very low take up of modern technologies contributing to low and erratic productivity in the sector.


* **Agricultural Environmental Action Plan (AEAP) 2011-2017**<sup>3</sup>: The AEAP was prepared by the Ministry of Natural Resources and Tourism, and was prepared to further mainstream environmental protection in national development planning, agricultural strategizing, and project implementation. The AEAP calls for strengthening climate mitigation and adaptation efforts in the agriculture sector through a number of key strategies and actions. These include collaborative efforts, including with the financial, water, forestry, and water resources sectors. Key strategies are: (a) increasing the use of environmentally friendly technologies; (b) providing finance to support agricultural environmental issues; poor water management for irrigation; individual and community-based conservation practices.


* **Five-Year Development Plan (FYDP II)**<sup>4</sup>: The FYDP II includes agriculture and agro-processing among the listed priority sectors, emphasizing increased agricultural productivity, deeper value-chains, and supporting infrastructure through increasing production and productivity of food and cash crops; developing irrigation schemes; and supporting the livestock, forestry, and fisheries industries.

## Agricultural Sector Development Program (ASDP II)**

Adopted in 2018, the ASDP II was developed to foster the country's economic development and guide the implementation and prioritized interventions of the country's Development Vision 2035. Overall, it is a ten-year programme covering two (2) five-year implementation periods (the first being 2017/2018 – 2022/2023). The programme maps the path for the transformation of Tanzania’s agricultural sector through increased productivity and commercialization, prioritizing high-potential commodity value chains, and mobilizing capital by creating enabling conditions. The ASDP II is structured around four (4) main components which are:

- Sustainable Agriculture and Land Use Management which aims at expanding sustainable water use and land management for crops, livestock and fisheries;
- Enhancing Productivity through Value Chains, which will focus on increasing productivity, for some priority commodities;
- Commodity Development and Market Access Programme, which is aimed on improved and expanded marketing, competitive private sector and effective farmer organizations; and
- Sector Enablers, Coordination & Monitoring & Evaluation which will strengthen agricultural policies and institutions.

The programme acknowledges that the current transformation of agriculture offers an excellent opportunity for Tanzania to achieve its development goals. The mobilization of adequate investment is central to financing Tanzania’s strategy for sustained growth, and to its economic transformation. ASDP II aims at mobilizing a total amount of US$ 45 billion in private sector investments. Private investment is therefore essential. Of the total needed financing of US$ 45 billion, private investments are expected to account for US$ 30 billion.

The programme will enable the prioritization of adaptive, resilient, climate-smart agricultural investments and mobilize private investments for the agriculture sector as outlined in ASDP II.


## Tanzania Agriculture and Food Security Investment Plan (TAFSIP)**

TAFSIP is a Tanzanian government-led initiative to support the implementation of the Comprehensive Africa Agriculture Development Programme (CAADP). The TAFSIP stands as a framework for mobilizing resources, including private sector investment, towards agricultural development, achieving an average annual agricultural sectoral growth of 6% efficiency gains in productivity and income generation through efficient agricultural markets, and better integrating farmers into the national and regional market economy.

## D. Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The project estimates that it will mitigate climate condition deterioration by adopting climate compatible farming practices, agro-ecological intensification, and infrastructure. Additionally, the project increases institutional capacity to become more effective in implementation on climate resilient agriculture and climate change adaptation. The project will also ensure that Tanzania is better positioned with climate resilient agriculture financing beyond the life of the project thus providing a long-term return on investment. To achieve this, the project will use a combination of financial instruments such as insurance instruments for risk mitigation; capacity building as well as debt financing, the programme takes the key lessons from the existing projects in Tanzania and other countries that have successfully implemented similar programmes. The enabling conditions are strengthened, that risk is minimized and ultimately that the capacity of the banking sector of Tanzania is enhanced. The programme will facilitate banking interventions, in a manner that ensures that the financial instruments are affordable to smallholder farmers. A feasibility study has been conducted for the programme, finding that, at the moment, without CDF concessionality, the project would not be financially viable. This is because the project relies on commercial banks (through guarantees and insurance components), enhancing capacity (through the TA component) and supporting the development of a climate risk management framework to make the program.

An integrated CGI of eight (8) ARA technologies and practices have been carried out to assess the extent to which support is required for ARA investments to be implemented. Further, in certain instances the project may not be financially viable when considering conventional costs and benefits. On the other hand, when adding externalities, to capture the value created for society rather than just private sector actors, the project becomes more financially viable. ## Activity 1.3: Establishment of an insurance scheme dedicated to ARA and small- and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs)

### Activity 1.3.1: Oversight by CROB Bank
With coordination between CROB Bank, its insurance affiliates, and other financial institutions, the market study will be conducted on the insurance market in Tanzania, including the potential for a parametric insurance product. The market study will include input from market actors, including insurance companies, agricultural cooperatives, farmers' associations, stakeholders or the farmers and market demand for climate risk insurance products, particularly parametric insurance.

### Market study
A market study is proposed as part of the funding proposal (via GCF grant funding) that will partially be used to support the implementation of the insurance product. The cost-sharing ratio will be determined according to the country's financial capacity, including the phase and relevant weather and climate information.

### Component 2: Technical assistance (all existing activities, managed by CROB Bank)

#### Activity 2.1: Integration of climate risk management into CROB Bank's operations
Develop an online interface for CROB Bank staff to access climate risk management tools and climate change adaptation resources, including an online training support system with a focus on crop type, farming methods, and livestock production. Such a tool will allow users to easily identify potential climate risks and resilience opportunities in their farming activities.

#### Activity 2.1.1: Develop training modules and "training of trainers" sessions for CROB Bank staff, farmers, and other key stakeholders
Develop an online interface for CROB Bank staff to access climate risk management tools and climate change adaptation resources, including an online training support system with a focus on crop type, farming methods, and livestock production. Such a tool will allow users to easily identify potential climate risks and resilience opportunities in their farming activities.

#### Activity 2.1.2: Capacity building exercises and trainings for farmers and other key stakeholders
Enhance the capacity of farmers and other key stakeholders (particularly smallholder farmers) through training on adaptation strategies, business development, and other agricultural practices.

## Activity 2.3: Technical assistance for the integration of climate risk management within a CRDB bank programme

### Activity 2.3.1: Capacity building and training for CRDB staff on mitigating climate risk assessments or external service programmes

## Activity 2.4: Capacity-building and establishment of a parametric insurance programme

### Activity 2.4.1: Engagement and capacity building in parametric insurance products, including targeted training for key personnel and staff at lead banks and workshops with key personnel and staff at lead banks on the development and marketing of parametric insurance products for the agriculture sector, including support on developing a climate risk assessment methodology for protection, assessing current operations and capacity to implement such methodologies, and identifying gaps in existing institutional resources to manage the implementation

### Activity 2.4.2: Targeted training and expert policy and regulatory support for other relevant CRDB staff and officials at lead banks to ensure that Tanzania's regulatory space is conducive to the development of parametric insurance products where relevant stakeholders have the knowledge and capacity to implement such products, including support on developing a climate risk assessment methodology for protection, assessing current operations and capacity to implement such methodologies, and identifying gaps in integrating available data analysis into existing institutional resources. Identify financial institutions that have the appropriate and adequate legal framework to develop parametric insurance products incorporating with lending transactions and operations.

### Activity 2.4.3: External capacity building, marketing, and harmonization of climate risk management within organizations to increase awareness of parametric insurance products and identify knowledge gaps on integrating such financial mechanisms within existing institutional resources. Identify potential lead organizations in the sector seeking financing transactions and operations.

## Activity 1.5: Developing and financial TA for climate change scoring

### Supporting CIRD Bank in capacity building and training for financial officers at CIRD Bank, its finance officers, and other relevant staff on the following areas:
- **Activity 2.4.5:** Capacity building and training for financial officers at CIRD Bank, its finance officers, and other relevant staff on the following areas: insurance parameters; insurance products and property extending an ARAI policy.
- **Activity 2.5.1:** Supporting reviewing, developing, and implementing a national climate change scorecard using the ARAI tool.
- **Activity 2.5.2:** Capacity building and technical training in the use of the ARAI tool for CIRD Bank staff (both for lending operations as well as insurance related activities), such as pricing and underwriting.

### Training documents, manuals, and other products on legal frameworks and tools for climate change adaptation and enhancing parametric insurance.
- Facilitated workshops with key stakeholders in the Ministry of Environment, VPO to discuss the draft National Climate Change Scorecard.
- Targeted trainings with CIRD Bank staff (both for lending operations as well as insurance related activities), such as pricing and underwriting.
- Production of legal frameworks to support climate change adaptation and enhancing parametric insurance.

### Activity 2.6: Supporting coordination between the Ministry of Finance and other financial agencies, key financial institutions, and local actors in Tanzania on the implementation of the Tanzania National Climate Change Strategy

#### Capacity building workshops with Tanzania’s national focal point for the UNFCCC (UNCCD) and other financial agencies, key financial institutions, and local actors in Tanzania to lead the implementation of Tanzania’s National Climate Change Strategy.
- Facilitated workshops with key stakeholders in the Ministry of Environment, VPO to discuss the draft National Climate Change Scorecard.

#### Activity 2.6.2: Workshops and coordination exercises between the Ministry of Finance and other financial agencies, national, regional, and local divisions, ministries, and departments on the implementation of Tanzania’s National Climate Change Strategy.
- Facilitated workshops with key stakeholders in the Ministry of Environment, VPO to discuss the draft National Climate Change Scorecard.

### Activity 2.7: Policy interventions and innovations for enhancing agriculture and rural development (AARD) program
#### Developing and piloting an AARD (building upon Tanzania’s existing Agricultural Research and Extension Web Portal) to enhance access to information on climate change adaptation, assessment, and mitigation, and the policy interventions needed to improve government delivery.
- **Activity 2.7.1:** Coordinating policy dialogue between the Ministry of Agriculture and other relevant national focal points, local actors in Tanzania’s financial system to implement the Paris Agreement states and regulations, providing guidance on climate change adaptation and enhancing private sector engagement for adaptation and mitigation.
- **Activity 2.7.3:** Coordinating and capacity building with the Ministry of Agriculture and other financial intermediaries, local leaders, agrobusinesses, and farmers on developing a national assessment tool and techniques within agriculture resilience to enhance access to information on climate change adaptation and enhancing private sector engagement for adaptation and mitigation.
- **Activity 2.7.4:** Support to the Ministry of Agriculture and other relevant stakeholders in piloting an AARDs program that will include: capacity building with local actors in Tanzania’s financial system; training, including engagement with local stakeholders on

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk assessment and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

Please describe financial, technical, operational, macroeconomic/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF) risk management measures in detail. Include the probability of each risk occurring and the impact from being achieved. For probability: High has significant possibility; Medium has moderate possibility; Low has negligible possibility for occurrence. For impact: High has a high level of negative effect; Medium has medium-level negative effect; Low has low-level negative effect.

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Credit, liquidity and creditworthiness risks

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Credit risk is inherent to bank lending activities and are perceived to be higher for the agriculture sector and the agricultural portfolio of manufacturing sectors and industries. Credit risk is associated with the failure of the borrower to meet the obligations in accordance with agreed terms. It also relates to the lower ability of borrowers to repay their loans, which may result from a lack of income or the absence of a credit profile) may not be able to access credit. In the current context, elevated financing and liquidity risks are likely to increase economic risks from the coronavirus outbreak, weigh on the creditworthiness of domestic institutions.

**Mitigation Measures**

In order to target market activities in the agriculture sector, the programme targets credit risks through the deployment of blending, in particular through the guarantee credit enhancement facility which aims at delivering a blended loan product with a 50% grant component. The blended loan product will also serve to absorb some of the risk associated to loss of crops and subsequently loss of income by farmers.

CRDB also has a robust credit risk management framework, which anticipates, monitors, and manages potential risks that may keep the business insulated at all times. In 2019 CRDB implemented a stringent Non-Performing Loans (NPLs) containment strategy, which focused on optimising loan origination, moderating lending terms, and improving credit risk management. The strategy also aimed to strengthen the capacity of teams to improve loan appraisal decisions to reduce default rates. Furthermore, in 2019, CRDB was awarded the highest credit rating by Moody’s Investors Service, which is the highest rating received by a bank or financial institution in sub-Saharan Africa.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Macro, micro and budgetary risks

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Macroeconomic and budgetary risks: Tanzania experienced strong growth in recent years, with an average growth of 6.5% in the last decade, driven by high levels of export of natural resources, developments in the tertiary sector, and the establishment of a liberalisation programme (MF). In 2019, GDP grew at 7%, which was attributed to the expansion of the manufacturing sector and household consumption. Tanzania continued to perform well economically against a background of political stability in 2019. However, the impacts of COVID-19 caused GDP growth to drop to an expected 3.5% growth for 2020. The government has announced a stimulus package worth $4 billion, which is expected to provide some relief to the economy and cushion against the negative effects of the pandemic. In addition, there are concerns that the economic recovery will be slow due to the expected rise in non-performing loans amid a global recession due to COVID-19 and the


---
**Page 73**
---

subsequent to a domestic economic slowdown. The pandemic has put asset quality and bank capital under pressure. Furthermore, the coronavirus pandemic has led to travel restrictions worldwide, with negative implications for the Tanzanian tourism sector, aggravating an already wide current account deficit.

| Mitigation Measure(s) | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Low |

Selected Risk Factor: Climate-related vulnerability risks

Climate-related and environmental risks. Tanzania is one of the countries that are most vulnerable to climate change. The agriculture sector, in particular, is extremely sensitive to climate change. Shocks related to weather and climate might affect farm productivity and thus the ability of farmers to generate income. In addition, the agricultural GDP has been decreasing due to shocks related to climate-related factors, leading to a value-loss of agricultural GDP over the coming five decades is estimated at approximately USD 27 billion.

| Mitigation Measure(s) | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Low |

On the basis of the market studies and climate risks and vulnerability assessment (CRVA), this project has the potential to contribute significantly in building a cropping system, including through the use of state-of-the-art risk assessments tool for the identification of the optimum transformational adjustments. The project will also provide technical assistance to farmers and agribusinesses on risk analysis data at the macro and micro level to support direct climate investments, including in the agricultural sector. In addition, the project will contribute to the creation of a market for the provision of climate-resilient services for farmers and agribusinesses as well enhance productivity and build sustainability within the entire agricultural value chain.

Selected Risk Factor: Procurement risks

Procurement issues: Lack of compliance with procurement rules and / or limited capacity in procurement by executing partners.

| Mitigation Measure(s) | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Low |

All procurement for goods and services to be procured under the programme will be carried out in accordance with CRDB Bank's procurement policy and regulations. As required, and on a project-by-project basis, the project will provide technical assistance to executing partners in conducting procurement training for executing partners, as provided for in CRDB Bank's AMA signed with the GCF.

# Description

Some of the technologies needed to shift Tanzania’s agriculture towards climate resilience practices require high up-front costs and are unaffordable for smallholder farmers and small agribusinesses.

## Mitigation Measure 6: Capacity Building

A dedicated gap study on Technologies for Climate Adaptation Agriculture in Tanzania has been conducted in preparation for this program. This included a comparative analysis between the technology needs identified by the government and those of the private sector, as well as the needs within the agricultural sector. The concessional loan component of this programme specifically targets this risk by providing financing to smallholder farmers and small agribusinesses at below-market rates.

In Component 2 directly tackle the issue of enhancing technological knowledge, e.g., through digitalization and training of extension officers and access to extension services. This will ensure that all target groups will receive training and support, which, in turn, will support the long-term impacts of the measures.

## Selected Risk Factor 6: Knowledge risks

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

### Description

Smallholder farmers may not have the capacity or the opportunity to access the relevant knowledge (information, practices, social capital) that could support their uptake of on-climate smart practices.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The programme – through its TA in Component 1 – will provide smallholder farmers with relevant information for farmers, including the integration of climate risk assessments in agricultural practices & technology provision; tools to strengthen knowledge and extension services; and agricultural infrastructure. The programme also aims to develop a national strategy on climate change adaptation and programs to strengthen knowledge, extension services and agricultural infrastructures to target actions for farmers.

## Selected Risk Factor 7: Risk weak implementation from regulatory and supervisory bodies

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Select |

### Description

Regulatory risk: Tanzania has (on paper) a well-structured regulatory environment and several key improvements have been made in recent years to the legal, regulatory and governance environment in the country, particularly with important changes in some aspects of the regulatory framework in the banking sector. However, there are still significant challenges that remain, including weak enforcement and corruption remain elements that can potentially hamper fair and equitable implementation of regulations which could subsequently have a negative impact on the programme.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The programme is being developed and implemented in close cooperation with the National Designated Authority, the Vice President’s Office. Such close cooperation with authorities through the programme's design, implementation and monitoring will ensure that the programme can be effectively designed to work in close tandem with regulators and minimize and mitigate potential regulatory risks and barriers.

CRDB Bank Plc, as a leading Financial Services Provider in Tanzania, operates within the relatively highly regulated financial sector of the country. The bank is subject to supervision by the Bank of Tanzania (BoT). The International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS) has been adopted by Tanzania and the BoT has incorporated the IFRS into its rules and regulations – as have other key regulatory authorities such as the Securities and Exchange Commission, the National Insurance Authority, the Dar es Salaam Stock Exchange, and Capital Market and Securities Authority. CRDB Bank,

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 72 OF 76

## Box 3: Risk and key regulators' adoption and compliance with IFRS standards (including IFRS 9, IFRS 7, IFRS 15, and IFRS 17, among others) and practices mitigates potential corruption and inappropriate interference into banking practices.

Liberalization of the banking sector was one of Tanzania's major economic reforms during the early 1990s. Since then, the Bank of Tanzania has made several regulatory changes to strengthen the banking sector, including increasing periodic performance reviews for banks and introducing the Financial Consumer Protection Act (FCPA) in 2018 and a Circular Note No. 3/2018 dated January 25, 2019 for added disclosure and transparency, trainings and financial capacity; protection of consumers' assets from informal financial institutions; and promoting the use of formal financial services. The Circular Note has also emphasized the need for banks to take a more innovative approach and has encouraged innovative and alternative financial products under the approved Financial Products framework.

In addition, the Bank of Tanzania has been specifically assigned to include extensive technical assistance (TA) that will target and deal specifically with parts of the regulatory environment that have been identified as high risk and that have hampered Tanzania's agriculture development in target areas. Such regulatory bodies include the Ministry of Agriculture, Livestock and Fisheries; the National Environment Management Authority; the Bank of Tanzania; The Business Registrations and Licensing Agency; Tanzania Revenue Authority; and the National Bureau of Statistics.

These mitigation measures and programme activities will not only contribute to the success of the programme by mitigating the risk but will also enhance the capacity of these government agencies to support the resilience and investments growth of Tanzania's agriculture sector.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

At this stage of developing the TACATDP, with the exact location, size, and extent of the sub-projects are unknown, it was deemed appropriate to prepare for the ESMP to guide the implementation of the programme. As part of the ESMP, the EIA will be conducted in accordance with the World Bank’s Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) and CRDB Bank, programme partners, and other implementers in screening projects and proposals at the planning stage. In accordance with applicable World Bank safeguard policies, the GCF Environmental and Social Policy, the TACATDP will be subject to a comprehensive environmental and social impact assessment.

The ESMP was developed in accordance with the World Bank’s Operational Policy on Environmental Assessment (OP 4.01). The process involves literature review, field visits and public consultation. The ESMP is prepared by GCF as government entity at the Office of the Vice President Division of Environment, Ministry of Agriculture and the National Environment Management Council (NEMC). Other stakeholders consulted are the Regional Administrative Secretaries for Dodoma, Manyara, Mwanza, Singida, Arusha, Tanga, Kilimanjaro, Mara, Kagera, Rukwa, Morogoro, Pwani, Lindi, and large-scale farmers in the Dodoma, Manyara, Shinyangira, Mwingi, Njombe, Dar es Salaam, Unguja, Wazee, Tanga, Kilimanjaro, Mara, Kagera, Rukwa, Morogoro, Pwani, Lindi regions. The ESMP is based on the extent of envisaged impacts (national, regional, local, area, or site-level), of which various categories are considered in the assessment process. The ESMP will be updated as and when further stakeholder engagement as an ongoing process and plans to continue its engagement efforts throughout the project implementation.

The TACATDP has been assigned Environmental Category B based on the provisions of the World Bank Operational Policy on Environmental Assessment (OP 4.01). Some of the negative impacts identified relate to the management of construction work for crops or water, installation of solar-based systems, and other related activities. These impacts are generally site-specific, and relatively straightforward to manage. Thus, no sub-project, sub-component, or sub-activity is subject to a separate ESIA. The TACATDP will be subject to an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) that includes effective waste management; best agricultural practice; best land and natural resource management practices; the protection of biodiversity and cultural heritage sites; the safe handling and proper handling and application of pesticides during the implementation of all programme activities.

TACATDP is expected to have significant positive impact on Tanzania and the country’s most vulnerable communities. These impacts include, but are not limited to, the improved capacity on adaptation to climate change; improved soil health; reduced associated operation costs; lower GHG emissions; improved agricultural productivity; increased crop yields and better-quality crops; reduced market linkage; improved efficiency of inputs and outputs (to the extent that they are available); improved access to markets; improved food security; and improved quality of life. The key identified potential negative impacts include loss and disturbance of biodiversity, including cultural heritage sites; water resource issues associated with water resources; associated public health hazards; the depletion of local water supplies; and soil erosion.

The ESMP will be implemented through the existing administrative and management structures at CRDB Bank and GCF. The ESMP will also guide the Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA) for specific sub-projects if required. The screening exercise will guide the ESIA to determine if sub-project

#### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The analysis and gender assessment are based on the Tanzania Country Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Project (TACATDP)’s general framework to ensure the programme’s implementation follows GCC gender policy and all relevant gender policy frameworks in effect in Tanzania.

The “Gender institutional, legal and policy frameworks and programmes” provides an overview of the institutional, legal and policy frameworks that are relevant for this project.

Tanzania has ratified both the 2030 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) Agenda, among which SDGS focuses on Gender Equality, and the long term 2053 Agenda promoting “full gender equality in all life- domains” and “gender equality as a key to sustainable development”. The 2019 National Gender Policy for gender balancing and integration such that the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) is fully implemented. Finally, the Lima Work Programme on Gender (2014) provides general guidance on gender aspects at international level.

Specifically, on agriculture, the Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan 2014-19) provides recommendations of key investment to strengthen knowledge and systems to target climate action through conducting a comprehensive analysis of the barriers to climate change issues in the agriculture sector including:

- Climate change impacts on women and girls:
    - Developing recommendations and guidelines for mainstreaming gender into Climate Change and Adaptation policies, strategies, programs and budgets in respective areas of jurisdiction;
    - Identifying best practices in Tanzania and other countries;
    - Identifying gender-appropriate technologies for activities related to water management, climate-smart agriculture, etc.;
    - Capacity building and awareness on climate change for women farmers;
    - Resource mobilization and access to finance and inclusive resources.

The Plan also recommends developing a gender and agriculture coordination mechanism between the Ministry of Agriculture Food Security and Cooperatives’ gender desk, gender committee and Environment Ministry’s gender desk. The plan further recommends that the Ministry of Agriculture should mainstream gender in Climate Change and Adaptation at each stage of project, program, policy cycles. The gender component should be integrated into all stages of project development and implementation.

Tanzania needs to align climate actions with the three main frameworks driving gender equality under the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the Beijing Platform for Action and the Sustainable Development Goals – each of which have already been ratified by the Government of Tanzania.

An analysis of the gender-related barriers and challenges in Tanzania provides the core assessment and analysis of gender aspects that should be considered in the implementation of the TACATDP. Women in


---
**Page 78**
---

Tanzania faces inequalities in most of the aspects of their lives, especially regarding income generation, years of schooling and access to employment in climate change, there are challenges related to women's further inclusion in climate action that address women’s poverty and inequalities in decision-making.

There is a lack of a cohesive evidence database on women’s participation in agriculture. The low productivity in the agriculture sector experience lower agricultural productivity (defined as the monetary value of output per unit area) due to poor access to inputs, credit, markets, land tenure, ownership barriers, etc., labor access unbalancing, (ii) technical and technological barriers, (iii) financial barriers.

The implementation of the TACATDF will learn from ongoing “Actions and projects to reduce gender imbalance in Tanzania” and incorporate good practices from the actions and projects implemented previously. The CRDB has been a key player in promoting women’s empowerment through its initiatives. CRDB Bank as an active stakeholder in the fight for gender equality. CRDB Bank is committed to fighting gender inequality by implementing various initiatives that promote women's empowerment, especially in line with Tanzania's gender commitments. Moreover, the Bank implements deliberate initiative to advance gender equality and inclusion in all aspects of its operations. The bank has a strong commitment in place to continuously increase the number of women staffs, CRDB has a task force in place under the so called “Women Empowerment Task Force” (WETF) which is responsible for promoting and empowering women by sharing experience from various people who have dedicated outstanding performance in their fields of work/expertise.

For customer women Access to Finance CRDB has established the CRDB Makini (makini is a Swahili word meaning “young girl”) initiative, which aims at empowering women through the expansion Business banking segments i.e., SME, MSE & Agribusinesses in terms of loans. These existing segments have been given special attention and are being expanded to include more women entrepreneurs. In addition to regulatory requirements with guarantees, CRDB works with other stakeholders to assist woman access markets by providing them with training on business management, marketing, and sales skills, as well as assistance in the markets for their products. Below a summary of deliberate initiatives to empower women segment through CRDB Makini initiative.

1. CRDS normally offer loans at very affordable lending rates and minimum account opening balances compared to all other loan categories with reduced collateral requirements.
2. Capacity building - using own developed toolkit book woman are trained in Business and financial management.
3. Explored and offered practical training to best business practices and markets including but not limited hereinafter: Kenya, Uganda, Tanzania, Ethiopia, Rwanda, Malawi, Mozambique, Zimbabwe, Malaysia, Vietnam etc.
4. Established a mentorship center with Tanzania women apex Tanzania Women Chamber of Commerce. Women are attached to women who are doing well in various avenues for knowledge transfer and also for financial support.
5. Banks has a woman champion in every branch to serve women category and in all business, banking needs related to women.
6. Assist women to formalize their businesses through various legal and regulatory channels and also train them on how to use the services of CRDB.
7. To ensure the TACATDF is implemented through a stronger gender lens, the gender action plan proposed a framework to ensure implementation is gender sensitive programme by providing with “Problem Tree Analysis” and “Gender Action Plan”. The Bank has already started implementing the “Women Empowerment Programme” project in compliance with the Green Climate Fund’s requirements. The framework explains how CRDB will address gender equality issues, including women's empowerment, which gender equality lies at the heart of this project, that will help remove barriers for women in agriculture.

As regards the adaptation credit (loan) deployment, the following outcomes will be generated for women:

Further women’s economic integration and income generation by structuring the market and providing support for food safety
reducing women's vulnerability and structuring their activity in larger plots
addressing women's access to technologies, techniques and financial services, levels the field between men and women.

This efforts will help achieve 50% of TACATDP resources accessed by woman by the end of the project period. The technical assistance component (TA) will be provided through a combination of CROB’s credit to woman as of today at a grant. For the technology assistance component, the TA will help structure resilient and adaptation agriculture in Tanzania, reinforcing the sector and therefore benefiting women farmers. The TA will provide training on climate change adaptation techniques, enabling women adopt climate resilient and adaptation methods, knowledge for which women are more in demand than men.

CROB will hire a gender expert through a competitive recruitment process to help achieve and ensure proper reporting of all objectives stated above.

G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Additional information on CROB Bank’s procurement policy is available in CROB Bank Plc Procurement Policy Statement dated January 2019.

A detailed procurement plan is provided in Annex 10, and detailed information on the project's financial management, including the financial monitoring systems, financial accounting, auditing, and disbursement structure and methods, is elaborated in section B.4.

All disbursements will be made in full compliance with standing CROB Bank rules and regulations and in accordance with the terms of the agreement between CROB and the AWA for the organization. For full transparency and in line with best practices, grant resources will be kept in a separate account from the loan resources of the project. The CROB Bank compliance officers will ensure such activity is carried out.

Auditing will be handled by a qualified, internationally recognized auditing firm, competitively selected by CROB Bank.

CROB Bank has a Feedback & Complaints Handling Mechanism for customers to leave feedback and complaints regarding their experience with the bank. The mechanism can be accessed at any of the CROB Bank's head office. Customers can also raise issues directly with bank staff/relationship managers at any CROB branch if customers are not satisfied after their initial conversation, they can escalate their issue through the following levels:

Level 1: Takes 1-3 days and involves contacting the Call Centre team by phone or email.
Level 2: Takes 4-10 days and involves emailing the Head of Customer Experience.
Level 3: Takes 5-7 business days and involves emailing the Director of Retail Banking or the Director of Corporate Banking

CROB Bank's Head office handles matters relating to internal corporate compliance. There are more details regarding CROB Bank’s policies on anti-corruption, bribery and fraud in Annex B. In addition customers and employees can report corruption and other issues through an independent third-party platform via phone and email or they can fill out an anonymous form on CROB Bank's website.

CROB has robust Know Your Customer (KYC) processes and Anti-Money Laundering (AML) policies and procedures in place. In accordance with the requirements of the International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS) 16 on Leases, January 2019, CROB adopted the new International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS) 16 on Leases.
