G.4 Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all documents related to the funding proposals. Therefore, the programme funding proposal will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board and any other relevant bodies. However, the IDP also states that there are certain exceptions where confidentiality is one of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the document contains specific or significant portions that can be identified as having a confidential period purpose or harm.

Confidentiality: No confidential information. The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.

With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF and that it contains confidential information. In this case, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

Full version (with confidential information): This copy will only be provided to the Board and other relevant bodies together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accreditation policy.

Related copy for disclosure on the GCF website: The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of FP179

## Proposal: Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Programme (TACATOP)

### Accredited entity:
CRDB Bank Plc

### Project/programme size:
Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1. Impact potential
**Scale:** Medium

#### 1.1 Country background
Agriculture forms the backbone of the economy of the United Republic of Tanzania, accounting for 27 per cent of the country’s gross domestic product (GDP) and 67 per cent of jobs. Small-scale farmers still dominate the sector. Crop production is mainly divided into two categories: staple food crops that are grown to meet household needs and food sovereignty and security objectives as per the Government of Tanzania, are cassava, potatoes, bananas, pulses, wheat, rice, maize, sorghum and maize; strategic crops, which are mainly for export, include tea, coffee, cotton, tobacco, cashew nuts, cocoa beans, vanilla, cloves, pepper, spices, sugar and flowers. On average, crop production contributes 71 per cent of agricultural GDP and accounts for about 28 per cent of total GDP. Livestock production contributes to about 2.8 per cent per annum, accounting for approximately 64 per cent of agricultural GDP, while cash crops contribute 30 per cent of agricultural GDP and account for around 70 per cent of rural incomes. Considering these economic indicators, the sector is expected to continue to play a critical role in the economy of Tanzania.

#### 1.2 Environmental challenges
Extreme weather events such as droughts, shifting weather patterns and increased disease incidence will continue to threaten the sector’s productivity, and these have already caused a decline in agricultural GDP from 30 per cent of total GDP in 2005 to about 27 per cent by 2012. The World Bank has estimated this poor agricultural productivity to be worth US$4 billion that could cause up to 2 million Tanzanians to fall into extreme poverty. In 2013, the sector contributed 17 per cent of the economic growth of the United Republic of Tanzania may have been lost. Based on the climate change scenario projections for Tanzania, it is estimated that by 2050, the country will impact national food production, leading to a “projected decrease by 8–31 per cent by 2050” The value of the agricultural GDP in the United Republic of Tanzania is estimated at approximately USD 27 billion.**

#### 1.3 Agricultural land use
With agriculture playing an important role in the lives of the economically marginalized and vulnerable groups—where “80–90 per cent of agricultural land is held by smallholder farmers and 60 per cent of economically active rural Tanzanian women are engaged in farming.”


* **World Bank (2015): World Development Indicators.**
* **Final Report of the Tanzania Agriculture Sector Development Programme Phase II.**
* **Funding proposal, section B.**
* **Tanzania Government (2014). Tanzania Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan, 2014–2019.**
* **World Bank Group (2015): Tanzania Agricultural Sector Risk Assessment.**


---
**Page 87**
---

The adverse effects of climate change will disproportionately harm these groups and hamper the country’s sustainable development.”


4. Brief description of the programme. The proposed TACATDP seeks to transform the Tanzanian agriculture sector via a climate-resilient and sustainable development pathway. It plans to do this by (i) strengthening the resilience of the agricultural value chain; (ii) developing innovative financial products and services that will de-risk agricultural finance transactions in climate-sensitive sectors; and (iii) building capacity for climate change adaptation. The third component to develop climate parametric insurance, enhance capacity-building and policy-making, and support the development of a national climate-resilient agriculture strategy and entrepreneurial activities along the agri-production and post-production business value chain (VBC).

The components of the TACATDP are:

(a) Project Component 1 focuses on the establishment of a set of financial facilities for credit, guarantees and parametric insurance. This facility, which focuses on financing CSA initiatives, is being developed as part of the Tanzania Climate Resilience Bank (Tanzania CRA Facility) and is managed under CRDB. The proposed ARA CRA Facility will be available to: (i) smallholder farmers; (ii) medium-sized farm operators/agricultural micros, small and medium-sized enterprises (MSMEs); and (iii) large-scale commercial farms. The Tanzania Climate Resilience & Adaptation Guarantee Facility (ARA Guarantee Facility), however, will primarily cater to the most vulnerable customer farmers and MSMEs as the case may be.

(b) Project Component 2 focuses on the provision of TA, it will build capacity-building for the CRDB, government regulatory agencies (e.g. agricultural insurance, finance), various actors (e.g. farmers, extension workers, agri-enterprises, MSMEs, corporates) and policy makers. The targeted project beneficiaries (i.e. farmers, entrepreneurs, MSMEs, corporates) under Activities 3.1 and 2.1 to 2.5. Policy making initiatives will also be included under Activities 2.6 to 2.10. The proposed activities are as follows:

Activity 2.1 - Develop an online interface for CRDB and other interested FIs covering climate-related financial products, including climate risk assessment, insurance, credit scoring, modelling and associated climate risks; this mainly targets the CRDB personnel.

Activity 2.2 - Customer capacity-building on adaptation technologies and business development; this mainly targets the stakeholder farmers, entrepreneurs, MSMEs and corporates;

Activity 2.3 - Support for integrating and fully embedding climate risk assessments within the CRDB bank extension service programme; this mainly targets the CRDB personnel.

Activity 2.4 - Capacity-building for the establishment of new weather index insurance mechanisms; this mainly targets the stakeholder farmers, entrepreneurs, MSMEs and corporates;

Activity 2.5 - Support to CRDB Bank and relevant government agencies and districts, including training and organizational capacity-training to conduct agriculture resilience and adaptation outreach and sourcing; this mainly targets CRDB and relevant government personnel.


*United Republic of Tanzania (2014). Tanzania Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan, 2014–2019.
*CSA - Climate-Smart Agriculture

## Activity 2.6 - Support for the implementation of the United Republic of Tanzania’s National Climate Change Strategy: this mainly targets the Tanzanian Division of Environment, Vice President’s Office, national, regional and local divisions, ministries, agencies and other relevant government stakeholders.

### Activity 2.7 - Policy interventions and support for the Ministry of Agriculture and the development of the Agriculture Adaptation Data System: this mainly targets the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Fisheries (including its agricultural research and extension authorities) and key stakeholders in agriculture:

#### Activity 2.8 - Support to the Tanzania Meteorological Authority for climate information services: this mainly targets the Tanzania Meteorological Authority, CRDI, MFIs, and all key stakeholders in the agricultural sector.

#### Activity 2.9 - Support to the Tanzania Insurance Regulatory Authority regarding weather index insurance: this mainly targets the Tanzania Insurance Regulatory Authority, CRDI, MFIs, and all key stakeholders in the agricultural sector (including those working on the private sector).

### Activity 3.1 - Support to the Financial Sector Deepening Trust for the integration of climate risks into the United Republic of Tanzania’s financial sector: this mainly targets Financial Sector Deepening Trust, local FIs, CRDI and relevant government stakeholders.

#### Adaptation impacts

##### 3.1.1 Direct beneficiaries

* The TACATDP has 2,414,288 direct beneficiaries (1.2 per cent of the total population) and an additional 490,056 indirect beneficiaries (4.67 per cent of the total population) over its five-year loan period.
* The total number of farmers directly benefiting from the agricultural processing chain at 0.86 per cent, equal to 7,606 beneficiaries, service-providing farmers at 0.04 per cent, equal to 538 beneficiaries), and large agroprocessors (at 0.01 per cent, for 132 beneficiaries).
* Smallholder farmers will have access to one-year loans; 796 medium-holder farmers will have access to two-year loans; 6,584 smallholder farmers will have access to three-year loans; and 2,589 smallholder farmers will have access to five-year loans.
* The one-year loans (having an average size of USD 1,750) borrower for smallholders will be able to finance working capital needs in the short term. Medium holders will be made available to finance working capital and asset financing needs in support of CSA practices and technology adoption. Smallholder farmers will have access to five-year loans (average USD 26,984 per agroprocessor) and medium holders will have access to ten-year loans (averaging USD 12 million per agroprocessor). The average loan size for smallholders is USD 50,370. For technology needs of these targeted stakeholders along the agricultural CRV enhances their resilience in a changing climate.

##### Satisfactory climate rationale

* Climate variability and change are the most prominent drivers of agricultural production. For instance, El Nino Southern Oscillation (ENSO) explains up to 35 per cent of the global variation in maize yields over East Africa (Milly et al., 2014). A recent study over Tanzania, Rosenthal et al. (2011) found that a 13 per cent decrease in maize production is clear.


---
**Page 89**
---

associated with a “2 °C increase of seasonal mean temperature by 2050. Shemangai et al (2018) noted that the current climate variability and occurrence of extreme weather and climate events have affected beyond agriculture and brought major economic costs in Tanzania. This highlights the need for better understanding of future climate change to inform future plans and decisions.”


* The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) finds the climate rationale satisfactory. Participation in this assessment was done using CHIRPS (Climate Hazards Group InfraRed Precipitation) data, which incorporates station-specific rainfall data and therefore provides robust local precipitation estimates.


* Monthly station-specific datasets from the Tanzania Meteorological Agency have been employed for the validation of the projection model, and this is in addition to data from Climate Research Unit (CRU) TS 3.21 dataset, which has been used as a reference against globally distributed gridded reference-grids. The validation process thereby takes into consideration both station-specific rainfall estimates and CRU TS 3.21 data for the United Republic of Tanzania, which makes the model intercomparison even more robust.


* Both the trend and projection analyses using model outputs capture variability and spatial distribution of rainfall in the target region. The analysis also shows that there is now evidence that temperature is increasing and will continue to increase in the United Republic of Tanzania; most especially in the northwestern parts of the country, and in the northeastern parts of the country. The analyses also reveal that rainfall is changing, with a steady decline in wet season rainfall and an increase in dry season rainfall.


* A gradual increase in temperature and decrease in dry seasonal rainfall is expected to aggravate the occurrence of droughts in the target region. Local agriculturalists must prepare well to address growing drought, as this will be one of the major challenges that they are likely to face in the United Republic of Tanzania in the near future. This has been particularly observed by analyzing both Standardized Precipitation-Evapotranspiration Index (SPEI) and Standardized Precipitation Index (SPI). The decline is derived from changing rainfall and temperature data at monthly scales over the target region. A gradual increase in temperature and decrease in dry season rainfall is expected to aggravate droughts in the near future and it warrants immediate response measures to sustain agricultural production in the target area.


* TACATGP enables the private sector to invest in development, an activity often reserved for non-profit development banks. The TACATGP de-risks both the financial transaction and the large-scale infrastructure projects that are being developed by the private sector (e.g., solar PV, wind power, and hydropower). Furthermore, through a combination of financial instruments such as blended finance, green bonds, and carbon credits (both high-carbon and low-carbon) pathway of doing business. Furthermore, through a combination of financial instruments such as blended finance, green bonds, and carbon credits (both high-carbon and low-carbon) pathway of doing business. Furthermore, through a combination of financial instruments such as blended finance, green bonds, and carbon credits (both high-carbon and low-carbon) pathway of doing business. Furthermore, through a combination of financial instruments such as blended finance, green bonds, and carbon credits (both high-carbon and low-carbon) pathway of doing business.


* The TACATGP also supports the integration of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) interventions in the agricultural sector. The CSA (hereinafter referred to as ARA) interventions are as follows:


* Rainwater harvesting and storage structures (WHRSS), pertaining to efficient nature-based and artificial runoff reduction and drainage measures.


---
**Page 90**
---

Adapted crops (AC): these would include advanced modern undercover solutions adapted to specific crops and the local climate as well as crop shifting.

* Compost mature (CM): CM could cover other proven ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA) solutions selected through defined quality standard criteria;
* New varieties of seeds (TVS): TVS and GM could cover either more agriculture adaptation-based solutions applicable in the local context.
* Inter-cropping (IC): IC would cover the modern agriculture adaptation-based solutions applicable to the local context.

* Drip/brickle irrigation (DI): DI would cover improved, precision irrigation systems;
* Renewable energy solar pumps (RE/SP);
* Land restoration practices (demi-lunes/agroforestry management): Demi-lunes would cover agroforestry-based methods (e.g., Zai, half-moon techniques) and nature-based shading (agroforestry).
* Storage facilities (SF): SF would pertain to metal silos and storage facilities for crop protection.

A system of rice intensification (SRI): SRI would focus on crop management technologies.

The funding proposal has not indicated whether these identified CSA practices and technology needs have already been carried out in the target project areas by the target farmer beneficiaries or are new innovations. Nonetheless, the adoption of new or continuation of CSA best practices and technology applications are adaptation measures and will always contribute to agriculture resilience.

### Risk to adaptation impact

16. Baseline assessment: The Market Study and Stakeholder Consultation Report provided a limited baseline assessment for these assessments; the baseline conditions of the target farmers' prevailing situations (e.g., specific climate impacts or challenges affecting their livelihoods) are not fully captured in this report. This is because the study did not explore their needs and challenges relating to agricultural finance and aspirations (be they simple or ambitious), nor did it capture the financial resilience of the agricultural sector (specific threats; and lessons learned from the failures and successes of these climate initiatives).

17. Proxy baseline: The CRBB, however, offers a proxy to what a BAI or baseline situation is like in terms of the specific challenges that farmers face with respect to access to credit market share in the whole of the United Republic of Tanzania. This private sector perspective offers an important insight into the financial resilience of the agricultural sector and its ability with regard to ensuring a sustainable agricultural livelihood. Mindful of the fact that CRBB customers represent the creditworthy segment of the climate vulnerable farmers and MMEs, existing studies on the financial resilience of these groups are not necessarily representative of those conditions on the agricultural sector priorities, agri-finance needs, losses and damages due to crop yields, post-harvest losses, market access and issues and challenges facing the agricultural BVC from production, processing and markets.

18. Adaptation impact potential based on the submitted documents is Medium.


## Paradigm shift potential

Scale: Medium


---
**Page 91**
---

17. As a caveat relating to the assessment of the paradigm shift, TAF finds that the paradigm shift is dependent on the full baseline assessment and market study of the agriculture sector, the agri-finance subsector, the targeted vulnerable farmers and entrepreneurs, and the timely delivery of the complete package of financial products and services by the targeted providers of TACATIF.

18. The timely availability of a full array of ARA financial products and services as well as how these are packaged for farmers and MSEMs loan for CSA practices and technology needs or sustainable climate-resilient (or green) agri-businesses will determine the degree to which the paradigm shift is achieved in the targeted agriculture sector. As per the funding proposal, the final design and details will still be determined pending the result of the market study. The TAF team will ensure that the financial products and services are accessible and affordable for most vulnerable farmers and MSEMs as they or their projects will not be creditworthy or bankable.

19. For example, the potential for the targeted stakeholders (i.e., farmers, entrepreneurs and MSEMs) is Medium. The combination of the potential access to the ARA finance products and services with the willingness of the targeted stakeholders to absorb, adaptative or even transformative capacities along the agricultural BVC As to the degree of paradigm shift, the accredited entity (AE) needs to have a complete understanding of the full baseline assessment of farmers and MSEMs in the targeted agricultural areas of the TACATIF.

20. For all of the above reasons, the potential for the targeted stakeholders (i.e., farmers, entrepreneurs and MSEMs) in the agricultural BVC is limited and mainly steered from the lenses of the private sector stakeholders engaged in agri-finance. The current assessment shows that the targeted farmers and MSEMs are already heavily impacted by climate change and variability and extreme weather events, and now further exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic. As a result, they have been forced to reduce their income sources; they receive the loans can be gleaned from the environmental and social management frameworks of the financial institutions that provide them with credit. The targeted areas in general, farmers say that they will “continue with current farming activities such as growing maize and beans, but also diversify into other crops such as tomatoes” for the loan for improving irrigation infrastructures such as the installation of drip irrigation systems, construction of elevated water storage tanks, drilling boreholes as well as expanding their activity to include livestock rearing. MSEMs say that they will “continue with maintaining their existing location, not expand, and continue with their current BVC activities. Information such as this is critical for TAF to understand the potential of the targeted stakeholders in order to ensure a paradigm shift.

21. Similarly, a baseline assessment of the agriculture finance industry in each of the agricultural subsectors is available; only national or general assessments[1] from the literature reviews, from the perspective of the CRDB[2], and partners of the TACATIF have been presented. The baseline assessment provides an overview of the current state of agri-finance systems from both the formal and informal sources in these target areas. The details in terms of available financial products and services (formal and informal); eligible financing purposes; interest rates; tenors; manner of payment of debt; collateral and clauses of collateral; fees and costs and other conditions, financing gaps, needs, and challenges.


[1] Funding proposal, Annex A2, Appendix 10, page 2.

[2] Funding proposal, Annex A2, v.16th Technical Workshop on Needs-Based Climate Finance for East Africa 19-20 February 2020, Republic of Ethiopia.

[3] Funding proposal, Annex A2


---
**Page 92**
---

21. However, as ARA finance products and services will be the first of their kind in the finance sector in the United Republic of Tanzania, the success of TACFAP will depend on the ability of CRBD to demonstrate the de-risking of lending transactions in the agriculture sector and its potential for scaling up to other sectors such as energy, water resources, and markets. This success can trigger the replication of the CRBD-climate operationalization best practices across Tanzania’s financial institutions and development partners, leading to the creation of new climate-linked financial products and services (similar to ARA) that could strengthen Tanzania’s resilience against climate change impacts (e.g., increased temperatures, droughts, floods, and greenhouse gas mitigation), not just in the agricultural sector but also in other allied industries and sectors (e.g. manufacturing, transport, energy). The development and introduction of parameters for risk assessment and management tools will be critical to this process, including various climate risk mitigation schemes to manage the risks to other food or strategic export crops. This is a key area that needs further attention as it is not fully covered in the TACFAP, but could be fully explored further during and at the end of the programme’s life without it stifling the three primary objectives of the programme and undermining the overall impact of the programme. Overall, the paradigm shift for the agri-financial sector is clear and rated high.

21. The paradigm shift for the government is Medium. The success of TACFAP also depends on the government’s ability to integrate climate change considerations into the operations and decision-making process of the participating government agencies and divisions: Ministry of Natural Resources, Environment and Tourism (MNRET), National Meteorological Authority. This approach will enhance the capacity of each of the government organizations in their respective areas of responsibility to better manage climate risks within the government budgetary and development funding constraints. The paradigm shift potential for the government is in its ability to improve the policy and regulatory environment based on actual best practices, lessons learned from TACFAP, and other relevant experiences that will have a positive impact on the transformation of the agricultural BVC.

21. The paradigm shift for the private sector is Medium. Overall, this paradigm shift will clearly only be based on the efficiency and effectiveness of each of the above interventions focusing on (i) the behavioral change of the farmers moving from traditional or BUI farming to CSA (BUC), (ii) the adoption of climate-resilient technologies and practices, (iii) technology needs alongside capacity-building on climate and the use of climate information services for crop production planning, and (iv) the private sector’s willingness to invest in climate-friendly interventions along the agricultural BVC based on the bottom-up collaborative experience with the private sector.

### 1.2.1 Responsibility for knowledge dissemination and behavioral change

The project activities under Activities 1.2 (parameters/insurance) and 2.1 (on the establishment of an online interface and training support for the CRBD climate risks assessment tool) are highly replicable across the ARIs.

The project activities under Activity 3.1 (customer capacity-building on adaptation technologies and business development) will trigger the behavioral change to shift to CSA practices and use climate-friendly technology in agriculture.


### 1.2.2 Risk to paradigm shift


---
**Page 93**
---

27. The risk of achieving the paradigm shift relates to the degree of shift that will occur could be from increasing absorptive capacities, or enhancement of adaptive to transformative capacities. This is due to the limited baseline data and information on the targeted stakeholders, crops, climate change impacts, and the agricultural value chain (AVC). In particular, for the Sustainable Rural Market Enterprise (SREME) in agriculture crop production and BVC; there is a need to ascertain the baseline level of absorptive capacity of the farmers. This will help determine the extent to which they can absorb the farming and lending shift due to the TACATIP interventions. While the AE has uniquely positioned itself as an intermediary between the agricultural sector and the financial market, the AE, as it moves forward, still needs to present updated and expanded evidence-based information on the market, and stakeholder consultation amongst farmers. There would be a need for the AE to provide more detailed information on the impact of the proposed financial products and services relating to credit, guarantees and parametric insurance. The AE will also need to provide more details on the cost of implementation of the documentation requirements needed in processing loans, insurance and payments between farmers and lenders. This will help determine the extent to which the TACATIP can exacerbate the climate impact on the present and future sustainability of the agriculture BVC.

28. In view of the above discussions and balancing the overall impact potential and risks, TAP finds that the paradigm shift potential is Medium on the basis of the available information.


### 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

- Sustainable development finance and financial inclusion: TACATIP offers an innovative model for financing climate-smart agriculture (CSA) in BVC. The programme aims to address the financial availability, affordability, and accessibility, and financial exclusion. TACATIP is also a financial service provider that provides access to credit, insurance, guarantees, and parametric insurance. This will help farmers access finance and other services for CSA practices and technologies aimed at improving yields and boost crop sales through the BVC. The programme aims to protect them from the risk of onerous lending schemes or predatory loans from informal lenders.
- Economic outcomes: TACATIP is a facility that will help jumpstart the grassroots agriculture sector in BVC. It will provide access to finance for CSA practices, which are much-needed finance amongst the agriculture stakeholders who are suffering from the impacts of climate change, which are now exacerbated by the economic lockdown due to COVID-19 containment measures. TACATIP is also a financial service provider that provides access to address the funding needs for farmers' CSA practices and technologies to improve yields and boost crop sales through the BVC. The programme aims to protect them from the risk of onerous lending schemes (input, logistics): the flexible payment terms to match the farmers' trading cycles will enable them to access finance without incurring high interest rates.
- Gender mainstreaming: The funding proposal has included a Gender Action Plan (GAP). The GAP seeks to mainstream gender in the TACATIP from project design to implementation. A minimum of 30% of the total number of beneficiaries under Component 1 will be women, which include the accessibility of the AR facilities to women farmers or women-owned MSMEs (under Component 2). The same applies for the TA capacity-building activities, workshops, training and consultations under Component 2.
- Environmental co-benefits: The funding proposal has included a safeguards package in the proposed TACATIP. This includes the Social Safeguards System (SSS) and the Resettlement Policy Framework (RPF). Among the frameworks prepared are the ESMP and the resettlement policy framework (RPF).


---
**Page 94**
---

10. The ESF has flagged eight crops that have potential risks on the environment and society. These crops include: cassava, cocoa, coffee, cotton, maize, sesame seed, sugar cane and wheat. The funding proposal needs to safeguard against the following risks: child labour (for coffee) land acquisition (for all crops), loss of biodiversity (for cocoa, coffee, cotton, maize, sugar cane) and protected or conservation areas (for cocoa, cotton, maize). These risks are discussed in detail in Annex 10 of the ESF report. The ESF provides guidance on how environmental and social aspects of TACATDP can be addressed. This includes: identifying potential environmental and social subprojects at the planning stage and institute measures to address adverse impacts. According to the GCF Environmental and Social Policy requirements, the TACATDP has been assigned Environmental and Social Risk Management category B and Social Involvement category A which will cause involuntary resettlement.\11\12
11. The ESF report states that “to avoid risks associated for their potential land acquisition impacts and provide all the necessary procedures to follow in mitigating and minimizing involuntary resettlement associated with the proposed TACATDP,\13\14 it is assumed that the subprojects will be implemented on existing farms, or on small plots of land (e.g., 0.5 ha), plots or pieces of land areas reserved for agriculture with no or little need of resettlement with less significant environmental and social impacts.\15\16 The TACATDP includes several subprojects involve the “acquisition of land required for construction work for storage for crops or water, including the acquisition of land for production of bio and organic pesticides or for expansion of irrigation canals; the RFP is ready.”\17
12. Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): The TACATDP can be linked to other SDGs. Among these are: No Poverty (SDG 1), Zero Hunger (SDG 2), Good Health and Well-being (SDG 3), Affordable and Clean Energy (SDG 7), Decent Work and Economic Growth (SDG 8), Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure (SDG 9), Responsible Consumption and Production (SDG 12), Life on Land (SDG 15) and Partnership for the Goals (SDG 17)\18\19
13. Risk to sustainable development: The failure to monitor numerous smallholder farmer activities may lead to environmental degradation or maladaptation.
14. Overall, the sustainable development potential of the project is high.


### Needs of the recipient

Needs of the country:
As stated in the intended nationally determined contribution (INDC) of the United Republic of Tanzania, the country needs to improve agriculture via “increasing agricultural productivity and efficiency through improved access for smallholder farmers against climate-related shocks including through crop insurance,” and “strengthening knowledge management systems.”\20\21
However, the lack of a government budget means that it is not possible to fully implement these plans and strategies (e.g., National Climate Change Strategy, Agriculture Climate Resilience Plans,\22\23 Tanzania’s Vision 2050,\24\25 and the Sustainable Development Goals) and increasing crop yields under Components 1 and 2, among others. TACATDP Components 1 and 2 addresses these.


---
**Page 95**
---

### Needs of the farmers, entrepreneurs and corporates: As per the funding proposal, the farmers and corporates commonly need access to financial products that acquire technologies that will increase their climate-adaptive and climate-resilient capacities. Corporates used financial products to finance their production activities (e.g., irrigation systems, elevated water storage tanks, horticulture) as these will help improve the yields, quality, and salability of their crops in the market. The farmers need access to financial products that will enable them to increase their crop yields.

#### Market needs from the CRED perspective: Having a 45 ± 50 per cent market share of the agricultural finance sector, TNC has an average portfolio growth rate of one per cent, with total portfolios declining by 13.8 per cent between 2017 and 2020. This shows that agriculture value loans show a stable 0.41 per cent growth. Within the agriculture portfolio itself, one can note interest rates on agricultural loans are increasing. The TNC agricultural finance sector portfolio beginning in 2017 and ending in 2020 shows that corporate loans declined by 7.86 per cent while smallholder loans increased by 35.49 per cent.

Analysing the loans, the top-crops (86 per cent of the loan portfolios) are tobacco, sugar, cotton, coffee, horticulture, and tea. The main reason for this is that these crops have a high demand in demand from small farm holders and small and medium-sized enterprises (SME) loan growth in the rural TACOTAP area administration.

#### Needs of the financial sector: The financing sector needs a system by which to de-risk lending transactions with the farmers and entrepreneurs and co-risk the agricultural activities they (farmers) engage in. This is because climate change impacts the production, post-production and markets and what policy or regulatory interventions are needed to address these. TACOTAP squarely addresses these under Component 1.

### Country ownership: Scale: Medium

#### Alignment with national climate strategy and policies: The project is aligned with the Tanzanian INDC[1] (2015)34 which stresses the importance of adaptation and resilience in the sector. The Tanzania Climate Action Plan (TACAP) and its strategic agreements that TACOTAP will support are increasing yields through, inter alia, sustainable agriculture; protecting smallholder farmers against climate-related shocks, including through crop insurance schemes; improving access to credit for smallholders; strengthening basic and applied research; strengthening knowledge, extension services and agricultural infrastructure to target climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies in the sector (e.g., improved water management).35 Other relevant laws, plans and strategies include the National Climate Change Strategy,36 Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan 2014-2019,37 Development Vision 2025.38

# Agricultural Environmental Action Plan (2011–2017): Five-Year Development Plan

## Agriculture Sector Development Program* and Tanzania Agriculture and Food Security Investment Plan (TAFSIP)*

### Introduction

The TAFSIP is a joint venture set up with support amongst programme partners. Led by the CRDB, the TAFSIP has an ideal set of partners that have expressed support for and commitment to the programme; these partners have very close working relationships with and aspirations for CERD.

* **CRDB** is a credible private sector partner for GCF. As per the funding proposal market study in Annex 2, it presently has 20 per cent of its portfolio lent to the agriculture sector, which is equivalent to approximately TSh15 billion (US$8 million). CRDB is also the largest commercial bank of Tanzania. CRDB is currently the largest commercial bank since its inception in 1947 as the Land Bank of Tanganyika. It has a market share of about 30 per cent of all banking assets, and then CCB.* CRDB has a good track record in lending to the agriculture sector. As of December 2020, it had lent TSh581 billion (US$46 million) to the agricultural sector, including TSh529,400,494,602 within the agriculture portfolio; corporate loans amounting to US$3.45 per cent (MFI loans amounting to 7.5 per cent). The average loan term is about five years and seven months with an interest rate of 18.5 per cent. For the period 2017 to 2020, the four-year average loan exposure in agriculture is at TSh476,105,231,907; an average of 80.41 per cent comes from the corporate accounts, 13.35 per cent from microfinance institutions (MFIs), and 6.24 per cent from other banks.

* **CRDB** is partnering with the National Insurance Corporation (NIC). NIC is a government-owned insurance company that has been in existence since 1970. It offers both indemnity and parametric insurance schemes in the agriculture sector. CRDB is in the process of extending the existing memorandum of understanding (MOU) with NIC covering traditional insurance to cover parametric insurance.

* **CRDB** will use a guarantee from GCF in its loan portfolio and may also seek to involve different financial institutions such as the Tanzania Agricultural Development Bank (TADB) and African Guarantee Fund (AGF), which also have significant suppressed demand. CRDB has obtained letters of interest from PASS and TADB.

### Implementation

The TAFSIP is a joint venture set up with support amongst programme partners, led by the CRDB. The TAFSIP has an ideal set of partners that have expressed support for and commitment to the programme; these partners have very close working relationships with and aspirations for CERD. The TAFSIP is a joint venture set up with support amongst programme partners, led by the CRDB.

* **TADB** is a state-owned agriculture development bank that supports the agriculture value chains in inputs, production, warehousing, processing, distribution and marketing. It was established under the Company Act 2015 (Cap.34) of Tanzania. TADB has been working with CERD to strengthen the agriculture financing value chain and support the Government of Tanzania initiatives to shape and implement policies and strategies for sustainable development.* Its agricultural

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

#### Overview
56. GCF will allocate USD 100 million for TAGCAT-PD; 70 per cent of this will be in the form of credit, 20 per cent in grants, and 10 per cent as a guarantee. This will be matched by USD 100 million from other sources, including government funding, private sector investment, and blended funding. However, this USD 100 million will only be used in the proposed ARA Credit Facility to match the GCF USD 70 million loan, leading to a GCF to GRDB leverage ratio of 6:7.

10. Fifty per cent will be used for Component 1 (USD 89.48 million of GCF total funding), and this is broken down as follows: 70 per cent for the establishment the ARA Credit Facility (USD 70 million of total funding); 10 per cent for the ARA Guarantee Facility (at USD 10 million of total funding); and 20 per cent for the insurance scheme (at USD 20 million of total funding). GCF grant finance will be used for Activity 1.3 (Establishment of an insurance scheme) under Component 1.

11. The combination of financial products and services will be effective in de-risking the financial transactions between CBMIs and the agriculture-based borrowers. All creditworthy stakeholders, including smallholder farmers, will have access to a range of innovative CSA activities that can access the ARA Credit Facility that matches their needs in terms of rates and tenors. The combination of financial products and services will be effective in de-risking the financial transactions between CBMIs and the agriculture-based borrowers. All creditworthy stakeholders, including smallholder farmers, will have access to a range of innovative CSA activities that can access the ARA Credit Facility that matches their needs in terms of rates and tenors. The combination of financial products and services will be enhanced by the ARA Guarantee Facility and/or property insurance products. Thus, the effectiveness of this ARA Facility to grant credit especially to the target vulnerable farmers and MSMEs will be significantly improved.

12. The ARA Credit Facility will be handled by CRDB and through its nationwide 260 branch network, it will be able to reach out to a large number of stakeholders (representing about 75 per cent of total beneficiaries). This will make it available to the targeted base of stakeholders mainly composed of small and medium-sized farm holders (representing 73 per cent of total beneficiaries), SMEs (26 per cent) and Government agencies (1 per cent). The ARA Credit Facility will be structured as follows: short-term loans (up to 9 months or 1 to 2 years) for CSA activities and technology needs of farmers; medium-term loans (5 years or more) for the purchase of farm inputs, equipment, livestock, seeds, fertilizers, value processing, service providers and asset financing. CRDB will maintain its practice to structure the loan products in a manner that is flexible enough to cater to the specific needs of clients through “tailored” financial products to meet their needs such as aggregation and debt mutualization; leasing being an alternative form of credit delivery for farm inputs and equipment, among others as well as the promotion of low-cost and high-quality locally manufactured technologies (e.g. net shading and polyethylene). The combination of financial products will be effective and efficient in reaching out to the targeted stakeholders under this programme.

13. Parametric insurance: The ARA Insurance Facility will be developed and managed by CRDB in collaboration with a local insurance provider. CRDB will be responsible for determining what entity will be handling the underwriting risk. CRDB stated that “CRDB would not undertake the responsibility itself but will partner with a local insurance provider with expertise to manage risks.” CRDB will also be responsible for developing and implementing the insurance market studies and finalizing the scheme’s ultimate design. CRDB will work closely with insurance providers in the development of the insurance products, including the risk transfer and guide CRDB in the establishment of major design frameworks, terms and conditions of the proposed insurance product. The insurance portfolio will include: coverage types, premium rates, policy premiums, manner of claims payment, selection of fit eligible agro-ecological zones etc.) Among the key features of this insurance scheme is that it will be a risk-sharing mechanism with diversification as aligned with the CRDB credit policy; mitigation of risk of simultaneous payouts to farmers due to climate change; triggered by low yields due to the impact of extreme weather events, and sharing of confidential information between CRDB and government agencies in aid of policy and regulatory initiatives. Additionally, the insurance portfolio will be distributed to 8 selected agro-ecological zones (AEZs) with a focus on smallholder farmers concentrated within a single geographical area. The credit policy of the bank guides the diversification of its loan portfolio into different sectors and subsectors to avoid the risk of over-concentration into single

sector or subsectors (Footnote 4). The insurance companies that will be working with include the National Insurance Corporation (NIC), NIC is a government-owned insurance company having experience in piloting parametric insurance schemes in the agriculture sector. CRDB is in the process of developing a framework for the implementation of the ARA parameterized insurance as well. This structure supports the effectiveness and efficiency of the ARA parameterized insurance scheme.

### 4.1 ARA Guarantee Facility with GCF: As per the AE, the ARA Guarantee Facility will be managed directly by GCF; CRDB will only coordinate with GCF on what accounts need to be guaranteed. The TAFM will also provide technical assistance to GCF in order to support the effectiveness and efficiency of the ARA guarantee scheme for the target stakeholders.

### 5.1 The TAFM will specifically support the development and implementation of the Agriculture Sector Development Strategy (ASDS) which is being developed by GCF in the Finance and agricultural sectors. Seven per cent (USD 0.7345 million in grant funds) will be used to finance five capacity building activities, including training for project managers, technical interventions and technical assistance to advance ARA in the United Republic of Tanzania). These activities are expected to be completed by June 2018. The remaining seven per cent (USD 2.49 million or 2.5 per cent of total funding) will cover safeguards and gender; the rest is for project management (at USD 1.60 million, or 2 per cent of total funding).

### 5.2 Tanzania Agricultural Credit Agency (TACA) has identified several priority projects at this stage. In lieu of this, it has identified a set of eligible funding activities under the TACATPD which for now will be subject to financial analysis and has been cordoned off in the disbursement engine and possibly viable CSA activities.

### 5.3 From an economic development perspective, the identified CSA interventions offer significant potential for increasing agricultural productivity and income generation. These include: (1) SF (rank #2), (2) RWHS (rank #3), SHI (rank #4), SIS (rank #5); these two which have cost benefits of USD 0.7 million and USD 0.6 million respectively, are expected to increase the nature of agriculture and the profits of targeted borrowers amongst farmers and SMEs; the most financially viable CSA interventions for ARAs and demo-ARAs (internal rate of return (IRR) of the project is greater than or equal to 15 per cent, NPV of USD 0.7 million or more at a discount rate of 75 per cent, PP of 4.5), RWHS (IRR of 64 per cent, NPV of 3.5), and SHI (IRR of 92.8 per cent, PP of 1.5).

### 5.4 Net present value drawbacks: The funding proposal analysis of the costs may not have covered a more in-depth analysis of the costs, especially those that have been impacted by climate change such as increased rainfall variability and frequency, increased temperature, personnel costs (labour), seed costs, fertilization costs, disease and pest control costs and other contingencies.

### 5.5 Weak budget implementation/procurement plans: The funding proposal's budget plan adds up but more details are needed on the programme’s target beneficiaries, intended sub-sectors, and the expected outputs of each activity. These items need to be improved upon to demonstrate complimentary or support thus provide an improvement in effectiveness and efficiency of the proposed activities and delivery of the intended outputs.

### 5.6 Budget plan: As noted, the funding proposal's Annex - Detailed Budget Plan does not provide details on all the budget items (e.g., components of the automated weather stations (Activity 1.3-47); number of target farmers and MSME beneficiaries per workshop per year (Activity 2.8-50); number of participants in the agricultural extension training (agricultural information collection) cases) and some do not match (e.g., Activity 2.2 K and Budget figure and budget line 34). While there is a link to the main document of the funding proposal, while the funding proposal already has the details, these are not properly reflected in the budget

# Baseline assessments

The baseline assessment of farmer and MSME stakeholders needs to be carried out covering livelihoods, priority crops, farming practices from traditional to CSA (e.g., Zai measures), access to inputs (e.g., seeds, fertilizers, tools, livestock, water powered instruments for agriculture use), ecosystem resources (e.g., water, soil), assets and investments (e.g., land, livestock, tools, machinery, infrastructure), market access (e.g., transport, storage, power, cold storage facilities), loans, challenges, and needs in the agricultural production and post-production IFC. The study needs to cover the impacts of climate change, COVID-19 and sustainable development goals (SDGs) on the above aspects. It should also assess affordability (e.g., credit profile, loan processing, collateral requirements), affordability of insurance products (e.g., crop insurance, livestock insurance), and access to financial services (e.g., conventional banking products).

The baseline assessment of the agricultural finance market needs to be carried out for the country’s agricultural finance market covering the various agri-finance related products and services offered by formal sources (e.g., FIs, bank and non-bank FIs) and informal sources (e.g. private money lenders). The study should cover the following aspects: availability of the financial products or services; interest rates for available tenors; eligible financing activities; transaction costs; terms and conditions; eligibility criteria; fees and costs; and regulatory bodies overseeing the particular FI or non-bank FIs, and relevant laws.

Market study. A market study on the proposed financial products and services (i.e., credit insurance, guarantees, etc.) needs to be completed as a whole. Study should cover demand and willingness to pay.

Overall, based on the available information submitted and the need to further prepare the funding proposal, the effectiveness and efficiency is rated in medium.

## Conditions precedent to the first disbursement of GCF Reimbursable Funds in Relation to the Accredited Entity

### (b) Conditions precedent to the first disbursement of GCF Reimbursable Funds in Relation to the Accredited Entity

Delivered by the Accredited Entity, a plan for form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, of the final version of the Programme Implementation Manual ("PIM"), which shall include the contents referred to in paragraph (A) or (B) below.

- An updated market study showing evidence based data and information on the following items:
  - Conclusive mapping per project area of target farmers and agri-companies and MSEs as well as their respective supply chains, including value chain actors, intermediaries, distribution, and markets;
  - Value chain analysis (VCA) for credit, guarantee and insurance products by specific target farmer groups and agri-BVC stakeholders;
  - the current market demand from key value chain actors (i.e., farmer, entrepreneur, MSEs, corporations), and challenges; and needs due to impacts of climate for each targeted product;

# Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment findings

Proposal name: Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme (TACATDP)

Accredited entity: "The World Bank", Medium Category


## Impact potential

We took note of the ITAP assessment finding on the Impact Potential scoring it as a “Medium”. The programme will invest only in ‘fit for purpose’ adaptation and resilience measures to address climate risks in the agricultural sector. These measures are expected to have a positive impact over the project’s operational life. Prioritized measures will ensure that beneficiary farmer operators will be able to adapt to climate change over the project’s operational life. Prioritized measures will ensure that beneficiary farmer operators will not be exposed to significant financial risk.

## Paradigm shift potential

We took note of the ITAP assessment findings ranking this category as “Medium”. This proposal addresses key barriers hampering the mobilization of climate investments at scale to support the adaptation of the agricultural sector in Tanzania. The programme is expected to have a positive impact on farmers who have received relatively low-level investment due to perceived risks in the sector.

We believe that the programme will contribute towards building resilience and adapting the agricultural sector while building its resilience to climatic variables and the adverse effects of climate change. The Programme will deliver benefits for smallholder farmers, women, youth, and other vulnerable groups such as the elderly, a situation which will in turn support livelihoods, health and the sustainability of the environment.

## Sustainable development potential

We appreciate that ITAP finds the sustainable development potential of the project is “High”, as it intends to address key barriers hampering the mobilization of climate investments at scale to support adaptation measures much-needed finance amongst the agriculture stakeholders suffering from climatic change impacts.


## Needs of the recipient

We appreciate ITAP rating this category as “Medium”. However, there is a much-needed financing gap in order to assist the development of smallholder farmers and transform the adaptive capacity of Tanzania’s agriculture sector. CERD is Tanzania’s largest lender to the agriculture sector (55% of all credit to agriculture). The programme will provide an opportunity for CERD to diversify its lending portfolio, and CERD needs a system to de-risk the lending transactions and agriculture activities with farmers and entrepreneurs.

We believe this programme will transform agriculture in Tanzania and ensure the country progressively transitions to a more resilient system and people.


---
**Page 108**
---

Country ownership

This programme has strong ownership and support amongst program partners, as indicated by ITAP alignment with the country’s climate change strategy. Over 100 stakeholders from various sectors ranging from public to the private sectors that have expressed their support and commitment for TCASTOP. We believe this programme has support at the highest level from all stakeholders in the country.

Efficiency and effectiveness

We appreciate ITAP rating this category as "Medium". We shall extend our market study on the proposed financial products and services, i.e., credit, guarantees, and parametric insurance. The market study should cover demand for these products and services by agriculture-based borrowers. In addition, we will risk the financial transactions between CRIB and the agriculture-based borrowers geared for adaptation measures.

Overall results from the independent Technical Advisory Panel:

CRIB agrees to implement the conditions presented by ITAP.

# Summary

This Gender Analysis and Assessment for the Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme (TACATOP) is divided into four sections:

1 - The first section "Gender institutional, legal and policy frameworks and programmes" provides an overview of the institutional, legal and policy frameworks and programmes for gender in Tanzania. It underlines the importance of the country-wide commitment to gender equality and the need to ensure gender considerations in all national and sectoral policies (agriculture, climate change, gender, etc.).

2 - The second section "Gender overview, barriers and challenges in Tanzania" provides the core assessment and analysis of gender aspects in Tanzania. It highlights gender inequalities across sectors, especially through the review of inequalities in employment. It further emphasizes on inequalities faced by women working in agriculture around four main barriers:

(i) Access to land
(ii) Access to labor and time available for work,
(iii) Technical and technological barriers; and,
(iv) Market access

3 - The third section "Actions and projects to reduce gender unbalance in Tanzania" presents actions and projects implemented, ongoing, or in preparation to reduce the gender gap in Tanzania. This section promotes CROB Bank as an active stakeholder in the fight for gender equality.

4 - The last section contains a "Problem Based Analysis" of the Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme project in compliance with the Green Climate Fund's requirements. This section explains which barriers and challenges (identified in section two, however not yet addressed in the country) will be addressed by the project. It shows that gender equality lies at the heart of this project, that will help remove barriers for women in agriculture.

# Introduction

This gender assessment was performed under the Project Preparation Facility (PPF) for Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme (TACATDP) and seeks to present the issues, gaps and problems that should be addressed by gender-responsive project interventions.

This document provides a high-level overview of the gender situation and key gender matters in Tanzania, with particular reference to the Green Climate Fund (GCF).

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) has elaborated guidelines on Mainstreaming Gender in Green Climate Fund Projects with UN Women to support the integration of gender equality in climate change interventions. The GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan is aligned with the GCF objectives and guiding principles, and a gender-sensitive approach is embedded in the GCF’s project design and implementation policies, which have been adopted since the Fund's inception.

Gender responsive approaches are essential to encouraging a paradigm shift towards low emission and climate-resilient development that addresses the needs of women and men [1].

In accordance with the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan, the core elements of gender mainstreaming required in GCF project design are a gender analysis, gendered actions and gender-sensitive Monitoring, Evaluation, Reporting and Learning (MERL) systems. The gender analysis is based on the involvement of men and women to discuss the project idea, and analyze their roles and responsibilities, and at project proposal formulation stage by reflecting gender issues across proposed activities and outputs. This is achieved through the engagement of a gender focal person and/or group of gender experts. Gender analysis is at the core of mainstreaming gender considerations in the project cycle and provides an understanding of the existing gender inequalities and gaps in relation to climate change, and the potential contributions of women and men to climate change.

The following gender analysis and assignment is aligned with GCF requirements of:

- Identifying and analyzing gender issues relevant to the project;
- Reporting findings of country/region/gender diagnostics and undertaking project-specific analysis;
- Reflecting the results of consultations on the project objectives or components with relevant stakeholders, including women's organizations, men's organizations, youth groups, gender advocates and women's civil society organizations, local and national authorities;
- Conducting a problem tree analysis to highlight how the project addresses the barriers and challenges identified.

   *Tanzania has also ratified key international instruments driven toward the fight for gender balancing and integration such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) which is an international bill of rights for women. Consisting of a preamble and 30 articles, it defines what constitutes*

discrimination against women and sets up an agenda for national action to end such discrimination.

Discrimination against women is stated as follows:
* any distinction, exclusion or restriction made on the basis of sex which has the effect or purpose of impairing or nullifying the enjoyment, equipment or exercise by women, irrespective of their marital status, in full and equal measure with men, of human rights and fundamental freedoms in the political, economic, social, cultural, civil or any other field [2].

By accepting the Convention, Tanzania commits to undertake a series of measures to end discrimination against women.

To incorporate the principle of equality of men and women in their legal system, abolish all discriminatory laws and adopt appropriate ones prohibiting discrimination against women;
- To establish an independent body and other public institutions to ensure the effective protection of women against discrimination; and
- To ensure elimination of all acts of discrimination against women by persons, organizations or enterprises.

Having ratified this Convention, Tanzania is legally bound to put its provisions into practice. It is also committed to submit national reports, at least every four years, on measures it has taken to comply with treaty obligations. Tanzania has submitted its last report in 2014 [3].

### 1.3.2 Fourth World Conference on Women - Beijing Platform for Action (1995)

The Beijing Platform for Action came out of the Fourth World Conference on Women held in Beijing in 1995. Among its statement, "The Platform for Action emphasizes that women share common concerns that can be addressed through a global strategy and that women are entitled to equal access to all areas of gender equality around the world. It respects and values the full diversity of women's situations and conditions and recognizes that some women face particular barriers to their empowerment" [4].

The Beijing Platform sets strategic objectives and actions that have served as guidance for Tanzania to establish its national strategies and plans, such as the 2008 National Strategy for Gender Development.

#### Women and Power
- Education and Training of Women;
- Women in Leadership;
- Violence against Women;
- Women and Armed Conflict;
- Women and Economic Security;
- Women in Power and Decision-making;
- International Mechanism for the Advancement of Women;
- Human Rights of Women;
- Women and the Media;
- Women and the Environment;
- The Girl-child.

### 1.3.3 Lima Work Programme on Gender (2014)

In 2014, the Conference of Parties (COP) established the first Lima Work Programme on Gender (WPG) [5] to advance gender balance and integrate gender considerations into the work of Parties and the Secretariat in implementing the Convention and the Paris Agreement so as to achieve gender


---
**Page 116**
---

responsive climate policy and action. COP21 (2015) decided on a three-year extension of the UNFCCC and COP25 (2019). Parties agreed to a 3 year Enhanced Lima Work Programme on Gender and its Gender Action Plan. The Enhanced Gender Action Plan sets out objectives and activities under five priority areas, twenty activities and thirty-five outputs. They aim to advance knowledge and understanding about gender-responsive climate action and its coherent mainstreaming. The five priority areas are the following:

- Capacity-building, knowledge management and communication;
- Governance, participation and women’s leadership;
- Coherence;
- Gender-responsive implementation and means of implementation;
- Monitoring and reporting.

### 1.2. Gender national frameworks and programmes

The first integration of gender in a ministry happened in 1973 with the creation of the Ministry of Community Development, Women and Children, established as the national machinery dealing with women’s affairs. The current Ministry of Community Development, Gender, Elderly and Children (MoHCDGEC) appeared in 2016. It is responsible for spearheading gender equality and the empowerment of women.

The MoHCDGEC is a fully-fledged ministry with a mandate for overseeing the implementation of Government policies and strategies as well as coordinating and monitoring implementation of these policies for gender equality and women’s engagement at all levels. The Ministry lacks financial and human resources despite its importance over the transfer of posts and salaries (and access to financial resources), preventing it from being fully operational and reaping it from properly implementing its mandate.

The MoHCDGEC is not the only structure fighting for gender balance but remains the principle and overarching one. Indeed, as stated in the National Strategy for Gender Development [2008], sector ministries, local governments, private sector, civil society organizations, and the private sector, local communities and development partners all play an important role [6],[7].

The Government of Tanzania has adopted policies that advance gender equality and youth inclusion in a supportive enabling environment. In 2000 and 2004, the Government has amended the 1977 Constitution to include Article 35 which guarantees equal rights for men and women. The Constitution of the United Republic of Tanzania now contains Article 9 providing a juridical foundation for gender equality in all spheres of life. It states that every person is entitled to freedom, enjoying freedom, justice, fraternity and consent. It also stipulates that the State authority and institutions are obliged to direct their policies and programmes towards ensuring this outcome [8].

The Government also formulated the Women and Gender Development Policy (2005) and National Strategy for Gender Equality (2016). The policy is a comprehensive framework for gender equality and integration and to align the country with international frameworks. However, pure gender policies, the gender dimension is also integrated in sectoral plans, programmes and strategies such as:

- The National Vision 2035 (2014);
- The Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan 2014–2019 (2014);
- The National Five-year Development Plan 2016/17 – 2020/21 (2016);
- The Rural Electrification and Energy Access Project (REEAP) [ASPID, 2016];
- The Climate Smart Agriculture Guideline (2017).


---
**Page 117**
---

- Climate change plans, programmes and strategies, especially through the National Strategy on Gender and Climate Change (2013).

### 1.2.1 Women and Gender Development Policy (2000)

The Ministry of Health, Community Development, Gender, Elderly and Children has facilitated the formulation of the Women and Gender Development Policy in 2000 aiming to ensure that the gender perspective is mainstreamed into all policies, programmes and strategies. Gender focal points were established at all levels of government and in all public institutions and development agencies. These focal points were in turn responsible for gender mainstreaming in their respective plans and programmes.

Thanks to this policy, Tanzania has seen improvements in education, health and water sectors, and in the economic empowerment and participation in decision-making of women.

### 1.2.2 National Strategy for Gender Development (2008)

This policy goes further in reinforcing gender integration in national strategies, programmes and plans than the Women and Gender Development Policy of 2000 by highlighting major issues and exposing remaining gaps in the implementation of policies and programmes. It makes a more explicit call on actors and stakeholders, suggests co-ordinated mechanisms to facilitate the participation of the various actors, and proposes a more focused and more result-oriented implementation of gender policies than the previous policy.

Major challenges remaining on the way to gender equality and equity come from the patriarchal system customs and traditions, leading to the following needs:

- Ensuring that all laws are gender-sensitive;
- Ensuring that macro and micro policies are gender sensitive;
- Ensuring that the Legal Framework is gender sensitive;
- Strengthening women's capacity for leadership development;
- Ensuring sustainable partnerships and collaboration;
- Ensuring that all institutions address gender inequalities;
- Ensuring that all institutions address gender disparities;
- Providing adequate capacities for coordination, monitoring, evaluation, advocacy and follow-up of implementation of policies, strategies, plans and programmes.

Therefore, the objectives of this policy are:

- Speed up progress implementation of Women and Gender Development Policy;
- Speed up implementation of national, regional and international commitments on gender equality and women's empowerment;
- Integrate gender issues into macro and sectoral policies;
- Link-up with collaborating partners and civil organizations;
- Enhance capacity building for planning, coordination and research.

To that intent, twenty areas of gender concern have been identified, each subdivided into objectives (medium and long-term development goals), strategies (means to achieve objectives), activities (tasks in implementing the strategies), responsible persons or institutions (implementing agencies), actors (responsible persons or institutions, with the ministry as lead institution), and a timeframe.

Several actors are designated to implement this strategy, under the coordination of the Ministry of Health, Community Development, Gender, Elderly and Children, itself responsible for (i) coordinating the implementation of the strategy and work closely with other actors, (ii) providing professional backstopping services in areas such as gender analysis, capacity building, advocacy and sensitization programmes, (iii) collecting and disseminating information programmes and (v) maintaining National Gender Database.

The Ministry relies on the support of:

- Other ministries, such as:
  * Sector ministries, responsible for sector policy guidance, supervision, coordination implementation and monitoring activities;
  * Central ministries responsible for providing general guidelines on gender advocacy and sensitization;
  * National level institutions completed with regional and district level ones responsible for capacity building, advocacy and sensitization programmes and disaggregated data;
- The private sector supporting private actors' action roles with roles and responsibilities to build local capacity, empower communities, participate in monitoring and evaluation, mobilize community resources and provide technical support.
- Communities playing a role in planning, implementing and monitoring community activities;
- Finally, external support will be sought with development partners making use of existing systems and processes to provide financial, technical and other support services.

The mapping of above-mentioned stakeholders is represented below:
