### 1.3 Gender aspects in other national frameworks and programmes and in agriculture

#### 1.3.5 Tanzania Development Vision 2025 (TDV 2025) (1998)

The National Government Vision 2025 (NGV 2025) is the guide for economic and social development efforts in Tanzania. The objective of this Development Vision is to awaken, co-ordinate and direct the people's efforts, minds and Tanzania national resources towards core sectors to enable the country to attain its development goals and withstand the expected intensive economic competition.

This document outlines the gender aspects that have been considered in the strategy. Women have played a key role, both as final beneficiaries of the strategy and as stakeholders to be involved in its implementation. Vision 2025 aims for five overarching goals: (i) High Quality Livelihood, (ii) Peace, Stability and Unity, (iii) Good Governance, (iv) A Well Educated and Learning Society and (v) A Strong and Competitive economy.

Overall, Tanzania aims at a good quality of life for all, to address social issues such as education, health and the environment and ensure that the involvement of people in development is central to national development. Those results should be achieved for both women and men, and girls and boys explicitly.

Nevertheless, it only outlines one gender specific objective under the "High-quality livelihood" section: "Gender equality and the empowerment of women in all socio-economic and political relations and cultures". This shows that gender balancing remains a cross-cutting goal even though more emphasis could be made.


---
**Page 121**
---

# 1.3.2 Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan (2014 - 2019) [2014]

The Agriculture Climate Resilience Plan [10] provides an overview of the agriculture sector in Tanzania through its main components and highlights the importance of climate change by 2050. Key observations regarding gender aspects are that smallholder farming is predominant in the country with women more likely to be smallholders than men. This puts women at higher risk to climate change. The plan also notes that women have been found to often more rapidly adopt climate smart agricultural practices as women have been found to often more rapidly adapt labor-saving climate smart agriculture practices.

This Plan highlights some of the key interventions of key institutions to strengthen knowledge and systems to target climate action through coordinated gender mainstreaming assessment on gender and climate change issues in the agriculture sector including:

- Climate change impacts on women and girls;
- Development of guidelines and guidelines for mainstreaming gender into Climate Change and Adaptation related policies, strategies, programs, and budgets in respective areas of jurisdiction;
- Identifying best practices in Tanzania and other countries;
- Identifying gender-appropriate technologies for activities related to water management, climate change adaptation, and mitigation, including those that increase the value addition;
- Capacity building and awareness on climate change for women farmers; 
- Incorporating gender into the planning process and access to productive resources.

The Plan also recommends developing a gender and agriculture coordination mechanism between the Ministry of Agriculture Food Security and Cooperatives' gender desk, gender committee and Environment Management Unit. The Environment Management Unit would work with the gender desk to mainstream gender in Climate Change and Adaptation at each stage of project, programme, policy cycle. The plan also highlights the need for developing a national strategy on gender mainstreaming in Climate Change and Adaptation related policies, strategies, programs and budgets.

# 1.3.3 National Five-Year Development Plan (2016-2027 = 2020/21 to 2031/2016)

This development plan aims at "Nurturing Industrialisation for Economic Transformation and Human Development" [11]. It is structured around four priority action areas:

(i) Interventions for growth and industrialization;
(ii) Interventions for fostering human development and social transformation;
(iii) Interventions for creating enabling environment for enterprise development; 
(iv) Interventions for getting the right policy mix.

The strategy is further anchored on three pillars:
(i) Industrialization as a champion of economic and technological transformation;
(ii) Competition as an active process to move from comparative advantage to competitive advantage;
(iii) Regional integration and geography as the context for industrial development and economic growth.

Overall, the gender dimension is only briefly described and little implementation activities or policy changes are formulated. Still, the strategy highlights that interventions to ensure gender balance are


---
**Page 122**
---

needed to unleash women’s potentials to contribute to the envisaged social-economic transformation and more importantly as a matter of human rights. In addition, it sets enhancing gender equity through affirmative action in accordance with international commitments and existing policy and legal frameworks as a key objective.

More relevant to gender aspects integration in agriculture and climate change actions are the aim to reach gender mainstreaming and to integrate Gender in the monitoring and evaluation process via sex-disaggregated data.

Finally, despite limited operational objectives, the strategy dedicates two axes of intervention to gender issues:

- The social protection axis through “Women’s and Girls’ Empowerment Programme”, participation in economic activities, child marriages and early pregnancies addressed under this initiative.
- The agricultural axis through “Women and youth empowerment through agriculture”; including, inter alia, serving the agro-processing sector, linking farmers with financial institutions, and improving the value chain of products addressed under this initiative.

### 3.3.4. Agricultural Sector Development Programme Phase Two (ASDP II) (2016)

The Agricultural Sector Development Programme Phase Two (ASDP II) provides the general guidance for agriculture development in Taita. It sets as development objective to transform the agricultural sector (crops, livestock & fisheries) towards higher productivity, commercialization level and sustainability of production systems. The programme is gender-sensitive and inclusive. The implementation of this programme includes a variety of agriculture stakeholders with special attention to women and youth.

The women’s role is highlighted through the first cross-cutting objective: “Promoting gender equity and ensuring that women and other vulnerable groups have equitable access to resources”. Indeed, the Programme aims at increasing the share of women in decision-making processes. It also ensures that the benefits obtained from rural labor and enhance value addition. In particular, it states that agribusinesses incentives policy should enable to grow the market share of women’s products.

The programme also recognizes the importance of gender equality and equity on all levels of the institutional framework and sets several outcome indicators on gender mainstreaming:

- Participation of women in decision making and implementation of the solutions in agriculture;
- Equal access to resources;
- Deduced disparities in the access to benefits of major programmes;
- Provision capacity building for gender empowerment;
- Targeting of participative support programs for women.

### 3.3.5. Climate Smart Agriculture Guidance (2017)

The National Climate Smart Agriculture Programme (NCASP) aims to accelerate the uptake of climate smart agriculture practices in order to increase productivity and climate resilience in the agriculture sector (crops, livestock and fisheries) and in the reduction of greenhouse gas emissions. It provides guidance for adaptation and mitigation in building resilience of agricultural farming enterprises, improving and increasing agricultural productivity.

The guidance emphasizes on the mutual interactions between agriculture and climate change. Major impacts of climate change on agriculture are the changes in rainfall for this rain fed agriculture, a


---
**Page 123**
---

general decline in agricultural productivity and in agro-diversity, the prevalence of crop pests and diseases and the overall degradation of water and land resources. On the opposite, agriculture plays an important role in climate change through deforestation and CO2 emissions due to land use change and agriculture. In this context, Climate Smart Agriculture, defined as “Agriculture that sustainably increases agricultural productivity and incomes; enhances food security; improves rural livelihoods and ecosystems at landscape scale and includes techniques [including, intercropping, conservation agriculture, pasture and manure management], and innovative practices, programmes, and policies, (improved access to markets, inputs, credit, extension services, etc.) that are based on a systems-eco- agro-ecological approach to soil, nutrient, water and ecosystem management with explicit attention to the importance of preserving genetic resources of crops and animals including wild relatives, critical in developing climate change adaptation strategies.”


Given the norms and traditions in relation to the roles of women in agricultural production as well as land tenure system, these technologies and practices face limitation when gender dimension is not taken into account. The use of these technologies and practices that are based on agro-ecological resources does not only increase hardships for women, but it places an extra burden on the entire agriculture sector, the broader economy and society as a whole. Given the importance of gender in Climate Smart Agriculture (CSA), this document provides recommendations to policy makers regarding the particular priorities, needs and realities of men and women for the design and implementation of such actions.


The Gender responsive policy and practice recognize and address the specific needs and realities of women and men, based on the social construction of gender roles. Gender transformative interventions seek to transform gender roles and promote more gender-equal relationships between men and women in all aspects of life. These interventions are designed to have an impact in broad political, economic, and socio-cultural structures. Because gender-transformative approaches seek to challenge the underlying causes of gender inequality, they tend to be long-term strategies that focus on interpersonal, social, structural, and institutional practices to address gender inequalities. The guidance therefore formulated gendered recommendations for each of the packages of technologies and practices in CSA. These recommendations include: ensuring that all CSA technologies and practices, including gender considerations in the selection of solutions, proceeding to gender analysis of the technologies and practices or ensuring women and girls can enhance their capacity to adapt to climate change.


### 1.3.6. Gender in climate change plans, programmes and strategies


Tanzania, as a signatory of the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) developed several National plans, programmes and strategies, among which the First National Communication (2008), Second National Communication (2010), Third National Communication (2014) and Intended Nationally Determined Contribution (2016). Those major climate change plans, programmes and strategies all identified gender as one of key sector to fight against climate change and its impacts on the environment and human beings. Nevertheless, despite gender aspects being overlooked in those documents, Tanzania developed a complementary document, the National Strategy on Gender and Climate Change (2013), to integrate gender aspects in its climate change response programmes.


---
**Page 124**
---

### 3.3.6.1 First National Communication (2009)

Tanzania designed its First National Communication (NC1) including technological and policy options for mitigation of greenhouse gas emissions as well as assessment of vulnerability and adaptation to climate change impacts. Tanzania assessed the greenhouse gas emissions and set its mitigation targets in energy, agriculture, forestry, water resources, tourism, human settlements and health sectors. Adaptation options covered water resources, crop production, grassland and livestock, coastal resources, marine resources, wildlife, tourism, and industry. Agriculture was highlighted as a key component of the national climate strategies both in mitigation and adaptation. Nevertheless, gender aspects were overlooked in this communication, mostly being used for some background presentation [14].

### 3.3.6.2 National Adaptation Programme of Action (2007)

Tanzania developed its National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA) to enhance its ability to deal with climate change and to address its climate change-related vulnerabilities in several economic sectors. The NAPA preparation process involved looking at the effects of climate change as a threat mainly to the agrarian population that still depends on subsistence agriculture for their daily livelihood. Tanzania identified water resources, crop production, grassland and livestock, coastal and marine resources, wildlife, tourism, and industry as priority sectors. It set agriculture, forestry, water, coastal and marine resources, wildlife, tourism, and industry as adaptation priority sectors, and prioritizes project activities in those sectors. Agriculture was highlighted as key sector to adapt to climate change. The only reference to gender aspects is the inclusion of women in the implementation of the NAPA through participatory reforestation in Kilimanjaro mountain* project that aims to build stakeholders’ (among which women) capacities on climate change adaptation and biodiversity conservation [15].

### 3.3.6.3 Second National Communication (2014)

The Second National Communication (NC2) further develops Tanzania climate overview, assessment and planning, and builds on the learning derived from NC1 implementation. Mitigation actions focused on energy, agriculture, forestry, water resources, tourism, human settlements, land use and forestry sectors. Vulnerability assessments and adaptation measures were elaborated for water, health, agriculture, livestock, coastal and marine resources, wildlife, tourism, and industry sectors. Agriculture is a major sector for both climate mitigation and adaptation. Gender aspects were also overlooked in this document, mostly being used for some background presentation [16].

### 3.3.6.4 Nationally Determined Communication (2018)

Tanzania designed its Nationally Determined Communication (NDC) to provide a concise and structured presentation of its climate strategy to enhance adaptive capacity to climate change, increase resilience in the face of climate change impacts, and contribute to global efforts for climate action. Mitigation options were developed focusing on energy, agriculture, forestry, water resources, tourism, human settlements and health sectors. Similarly to the National Communications and to the NAPA, gender aspects are not a feature at the center of the strategy; however, gender aspects are not mentioned at all this time [17].


---
**Page 125**
---

# National Strategy on Gender and Climate Change (2013)

The National Strategy on Gender and Climate Change [18] outlines six thematic areas (agriculture, water, health, energy, forests and integrated coastal management), addresses barriers faced by women as agents for change, and identifies cross-cutting dimensions such as capacity building, awareness raising, and policy change.

The strategy is expected to guide national efforts for addressing the impacts of climate change on gender inequalities as women suffer from historic disadvantages: their limited access and control over decision-making processes in agriculture, forestry, water management, energy, health, and education. Gender inequality can worsen the impacts of climate change while, taking steps to narrow the gender gap and empower women can reduce these impacts. Climate change and gender inequality are therefore interlinked and must be addressed together for sustainable development.

Empowerment of women is a key strategy in addressing gender inequalities and achieving gender equality and henceforth impedes efforts to achieve wider goals such as poverty reduction and sustainable development.

The disproportionate burden of climate change on women can be countered by their empowerment and recognition. Women are powerful agents of change and their leadership is critical. Women can enhance strategies related to integrated coastal management, forest, agriculture, water, health and energy. For example, women have a better understanding of the impacts of climate change than men and women understand the process of climate change and share information on counteracting its negative effects.

The strategy aims at empowering women by providing them with access to education, real access to knowledge, awareness, capacity building, resources and technology, which are prerequisites in influencing climate change. Likewise, it is fundamental that women participate more actively in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies. The principles of women empowerment underlie this strategy, as two globally recognized complementary principles.

For each priority sector, a situation analysis and a gender assessment are presented with examples of action, including the identification of key actors and stakeholders involved. Indicators of success and responsible stakeholders for the agriculture sector, seven objectives are set:

(i) Building and strengthening institutional understanding on gender, climate change and agriculture;
(ii) Improving agriculture related infrastructure to adapt to the effects of climate change;
(iii) Enhancing local community participation of women and men in components of agricultural sectors;
(iv) Land tenure and customary law to incorporate women's access and title to land;
(v) Strengthening early warning system in agriculture to facilitate weather forecasting;
(vi) Improving gender equality in extension services;
(vii) Enhancing participation of women and girl through formal and informal education in the agricultural sector.

In addition, seven cross-cutting objectives have been set for capacity building, awareness and policy change:

(i) Ensuring that gender responsive climate change criteria are fully integrated in relevant national policies;
(ii) Enhancing the mainstreaming of gender and climate change in the implementation of national development plans;
(iii) Developing a sustainable financing mechanism for climate change challenges including gender projects.


---
**Page 126**
---

(iv) Strengthening the institutional framework for climate change governance and improved coordination at all levels;

(v) Promoting legal and regulatory framework for gender and climate change with specific emphasis in empowerment of women for accessing and controlling resources;

(vi) Building capacity of women and men to participate effectively in decision-making processes that mainstream gender and climate change in relevant government sectors; and,

(vii) Increasing women and men awareness on climate change and its impact on relevant sectors.

2. Gender overview, barriers and challenges in Tanzania

2.1 General gender overview, barriers and challenges in Tanzania

2.1.1 Overview

Women in Tanzania suffer from inequalities in most of the aspects of their lives, especially regarding income generation, years of schooling and access to labor, as shown below [39]:

Human Development Index

|  | Female | Male |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 0.5 | 2015 | 2015 |
| 0.4 | 2010 | 2010 |
| 0.3 | 2005 | 2005 |
| 0.2 | 2000 | 2000 |

The Human Development Index (HDI) is a summary measure of achievements in three key dimensions of human development: a long and healthy life, access to knowledge and a decent standard of living. As shown alone, in addition to the low Human Development Indexes (HDI) of 0.737 worldwide women are particularly at risk. The gender gap has slightly narrowed over the years, but has remained relatively stable since 2015, showing little improvement in women’s situation compared to men in Tanzania despite several measures implemented by the government.

The major component explaining unequal HDIs between men and women is the difference in generating income:

Estimated gross income (2017 PPPS)

| --- | --- | --- |
| 1500 | 2005 | 2005 |
| 3000 | 2010 | 2010 |
| 4000 | 2015 | 2015 |
| 6000 | 2020 | 2020 |

Indeed, the smaller gender gap for the HDI in 2013 and 2014 can be explained by the temporary reduction of the income gap. Nevertheless, recent years have seen a faster increase of male incomes than that of women, highlighting the improvements made in the country, but the lasting gender inequalities. These income inequalities are even more relevant when considering the sole agriculture sector as detailed in the following sections.

An element partially explains the improvement of incomes and of the population's life conditions is the increasing time spent at school.

Once again, women lag behind men, but the gender gap is reducing over the years giving hope that women's overall situation will get better soon. More specifically, while men’s years of schooling have recently stabilized, women’s years of schooling are still increasing. This is one of the most important gender gaps, able to have a positive impact at all levels (professional opportunities, literacy and participation in the political life, increased productivity...).

Finally, inequalities appear clearly when comparing employment rates and unemployment ratios between men and women.

Female to male ratio has always remained higher than 1 and even higher than 1.5 since 2011, showing a much larger likelihood for women to be unemployed than men. This can further be illustrated with the evolution of labor force participation. Both men and women tend to be more at risk of unemployment, as the participation rate decreased almost every year, for both men and women (only exceptions are in 2008 and 2013). Women have a higher likelihood of being unemployed than men on average, once again showing that women are more at risk of being unemployed than men. Those observations are further explained and completed in the following sections.

### Barriers and challenges

Gender inequalities are the major factors that have increased women's vulnerability to climate change, compared to men. Although governments in developing countries have endeavored to formulate gender-responsive budgets, their implementation has been constrained by (i) lack of technical capacity in gender equality, (ii) limited financial resources for gender equality initiatives, (iii) limited or no resources allocated towards implementing Gender Responsive Budget Initiatives, (iv) inadequate research and gender-disaggregated data, (v) negative cultural practices, and (vi) dominance of gender-based sectoral policies especially those that influence local development initiatives.

For Tanzania, annual budget guidelines on women's action are not clear and more generally, the importance of gender equality in national economic planning is low. However, it should be noted that most national level policy in Tanzania recognize the need for gender equality by specifying issues of concern, but fall short of reflecting clear strategies, actions, or means of addressing the issues highlighted.

This situation leads to gender unbalancing towards women. Indeed, the Gender Development Index is 0.946 (GDI, measure of gender inequalities in three dimensions: health, education and command over economic resources). Tanzania belongs to Gender Development Index group 3 out of 5 (group 5 being the countries with highest unbalance) [18]. The government has increased its efforts to reduce gender unbalance and provide similar opportunities to women.

To identify main barriers to gender equality and equity, Tanzania has developed a Country Gender Plan (CGP). This plan is based on the findings of the Gender Equality Assessment Study for the development of gender balance. Synthesis presentations of the main observations are:

| Priority Sector | Main Observations |
| --- | --- |
| Economy | Despite a decline of women's poverty incidence over the last years from 34.4% in 2007 down to 28.2% in 2012, women remain more struck by poverty than men and are less likely to be employed. Women are more likely to work in agriculture, an agricultural sector that is less dynamic than the overall economy thus generating an intrinsic delay in women's economic development. Overall, while women have made some progress in terms of education, the proportion of unemployed women remains higher; youth unemployment rates for girls, women are more likely to be employed in informal sectors and in small-scale or even enterprises than men. Challenges arise from inadequate gender mainstreaming in the economy, especially due to inadequate policy, regulations and support systems, as well as planning budgeting and monitoring through gender |


---
**Page 130**
---

# Healthcare

The rate of maternal mortality is rising; women have better access to health insurance funds (National Health Insurance Fund and Community Health Fund) and better follow-up of pregnancy/birth, reducing maternal mortality rates and early childhood deaths. The ratio of female-to-male infant mortality has fallen against 38% for men). The main challenges are the persistence of gender-based violence, which is often linked to traditional cultural issues, poverty, inadequate information and access to care, cultural biases preventing women from diagnosis. Women also lack a comprehensive program that addresses their needs in terms of health services. There should be comprehensive health gender assessments and increase national budgeting for health services, including reproductive health services, maternal and child health, and other epidemics and invest in outreach services in regions and districts.

# Education

There is parity between girls and boys at primary school but there is a much lower rate of girls to boys at secondary and higher education levels. Most specifically, there is a low representation of women in Science, Technology, Engineering and Mathematics (STEM) fields. Women's access to education is improving; female literacy rates lag behind that of males. Main challenges for female students are the lack of adequate facilities, poor quality of teaching, low catchment at secondary and tertiary levels, and the low enrollment of girls in STEM subjects. There should be a focus on increasing the number of women teachers and achievement rates, enhance the willingness of local communities to invest in girls' education, and provide a more enabling school environment to girls (e.g., improved sanitation facilities, access to water, etc.). There is also a need for role models, support for female empowerment.

# Politics

Women are underrepresented at all levels of political representation, especially in the Parliament with Tanzania being one of the countries with the highest share of women in parliament. Women's representation (37%) in 2015 was attributed to temporary special measures targeting 50-50 gender representation. Further challenges for women are headed by women as are some key political positions within the central government and local authorities. Women are underrepresented at all levels of decision-making bodies and at their top especially at local level where the decision-making process remains male-dominated. The main challenges to women's participation relate to the patriarchal ideology limiting women representation in politics, and to inadequate awareness and enforcement of the 2010 Election Act, that constrains women's access to political power and influence. Women are also underrepresented during the electoral process. Tanzania will need to institutionalize gender sensitive policies and strategies for women's participation.

# Violence and abuse

An increasing number of women are breaking down gender-based violence laws. The most common forms of violence against women include physical mutilation (14.6% of women), physical (31.5% of women) and sexual violence (21.0% women has experienced sexual intercourse against their will). Tanzania only provides for the criminalization of rape in the Penal Code, but does not provide for the removal of issues such as early marriage. Main challenges to be addressed by Tanzania are the lack of adequate legislation on gender-based violence and shortcomings in enforcement of the law; and to rely on adequate resources. Further challenges include inadequate awareness among women about their rights or religious frameworks (especially the 1971 Marriage Act), and often times customary and religious laws prevent women from accessing their legal rights.


---
**Page 131**
---

## Rights and legal framework

As a signatory of the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women, Tanzania is accelerating the integration of gender aspects in its laws and policies. The country has made significant progress since 2014 by amending some accessing courts of law, but a lot of work remains to be done to remove barriers for women in accessing justice. Despite the constitutional amendments in 2014 updating the Constitution further advancing gender equality, several barriers remain to develop an adequate legal framework for women such as the low legal literacy rate, lack of awareness on the new laws (e.g., inheritance, divorce, inheritance, etc.). Also, the lack of gender disaggregated data does not allow to monitor and report on progress in gender equality. The information currently used to inform policy, practice and planning Tanzania will therefore need to develop a gender-disaggregated statistical framework for monitoring, reporting and monitoring gender in legal sector institutions as early as possible.

## Climate change

Structures and policies are being set up to spearhead interventions and promote the integration of gender perspectives into national climate change strategies. The country has developed its National Climate Change Strategy and Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (REDD+) Strategy, which both aim at ensuring that women can mainstream gender in climate change, but women's response to climate change remains weak due to lack of access to information, resources, and decision-making power, especially financial capacity. Challenges to further integrate women in climate change actions include women’s poverty and the inequalities in decision. There is also a need for more research on women’s vulnerability and adaptation potential knowledge and mechanisms for addressing the impacts of environmental and climate change on women's livelihoods. The country needs to develop a national gender response plan. Tanzania needs to align climate action with the three main frameworks: National Climate Change Strategy, REDD+ Strategy, and Sustainable Development Goals, each of those frameworks having already been ratified by the country.

## Information and communication

Women in Tanzania working in the agriculture sector experience lower agricultural productivity (defined as the monetary value of output per unit of land) and higher vulnerability than men for four main reasons: low ownership of land, lack of access to credit, inadequate infrastructure, and institutional barriers. [4] financial barriers.


---
**Page 132**
---

In addition, gender differentiated farm activities suggest that women and men have different experience and knowledge of the natural environment. Differences also exist with regard to owning and controlling resources, in particular land. As a consequence, men are more likely than women to be able to adapt to risks emerging from climate variability and the associated natural disasters [8]. Empowerment of women is therefore essential for ensuring that they can contribute effectively to adapting small-scale agriculture to its adverse effects, all the more so as low agricultural productivity tends to reduce yields per-hectare and leads to more intense farming—resulting in overcultivation, soil erosion, and land degradation.

Employment: Women are more likely than men to be unemployed. In addition to the financial unbalances between women and men, this is also due to the fact that they tend to be unemployed because they have to handle household work; men tend to attend school when not employed. This major difference further increases the unbalancing: prolonged school attendance leads to potential more attractive

# Vulnerabilities: When employed, women again have to face several challenges in addition to financial ones.

A very high percentage of them are vulnerable workers, and they tend to suffer from work related injuries and accidents [20].

## Female headed households and household work

Women spend more time than men on housework, while men spend more time on income generating activities further increasing economic unbalancing. Women in male-headed households (51%) are more likely to be the sole breadwinner compared to female headed households (25.4% in 2017 [3]). As such, women are more likely only to rely on a single income provider, contrary to male headed households where men can rely on women to provide income as well. Indeed, women in male-headed households earn less than their male counterparts by about $8 for male headed households [28]. This situation further puts women risks in risky financial positions.

## Economic differences: Finally, women earn lower incomes than men across all sectors. Women working in agriculture earn 10% less income compared to men and this is the largest difference with men, compared to the difference across all sectors; income in agriculture are much lower than in other sectors, making women working in agriculture the lowest earning population.

Overall, the gender gap in agriculture is estimated to have an annual cost of $259 million (0.48% of GDP) This highlights the large impact both unconditional (strict difference in the value of output per hectare between male and female) and conditional (further comparison taking into account that women farmers are more likely to be small scale, low productivity and less equipped to adapt to climatic conditions) gender balancing solutions could bring, with respectively an expected increase of agricultural productivity of 10% or 30%. In addition, reducing the gender gap could increase crop output by about $259 million (0.48% of GDP), which would translate into a reduction in poverty by 0.7% as women tend to spend more of their income on children's health and education [22].

Below is a focus on the four main barriers highlighted at the beginning of the section [3], [27].

### 2.1. Land ownership barrier

Due to legal frameworks, customary and traditional land ownership norms, Tanzanian women face several land tenure challenges. They hold only few and small plots, often of lower quality and productivity and have limited room to enjoy the benefits of land they own.

Tanzania's land reforms, such as the Land Act and the Village Land Act of 1975, are the aim of eradication of customary land ownership norms. Article 20(3) of the Village Land Act stipulates: "the right of every adult woman to acquire, hold, use, deal with and transmit land by or obtain land through the operations of a will shall be to the same extent and subject to the same restrictions as that of any other person". This means that women have equal rights over property and have the same land rights men. Indeed, laws provide for strong protections of women landowners. Recognize a woman's right to acquire, hold, use, deal with and transmit village land; recognize a woman's right to be discriminating against women, and allocate to a certain number of seats on the councils, which administer occupancy rights and adjudicate land disputes in rural areas.


---
**Page 134**
---

Despite legal protections and reforms, customary land tenure norms, vesting control of property in men continue to influence decisions and practices on rural areas while women’s rights to land is reduced to their roles as wives or daughters. By some estimates, 82% of land in Tanzania is administered under customary land tenure. Women have usufruct rights through spouses or male family members but cannot formally own the land. This means that women are not recognized as owners and thus not part of a family’s land. Of that land, the man allocates small garden plots to his wife so she can cultivate food crops to provide for her household, which is a primary responsibility of women. However, this does not give women secure property rights.

This situation translates into 73% of landholders being men, leaving only 27% of women landholders. As owners, women tend to have smaller and fewer plots, average land size of 0.6 hectares against 1 hectare for men. Women are more likely to be involved in subsistence farming while men are more likely to be affected by floods, landslides and the resulting soil degradation, against which they cannot afford to invest. Women’s plots tend to be smaller and less fertile than those of their husbands and use less hired labor. While most of the plots are irrigated by flooding (71%) and buckets (38%), considerable gender differences exist with regards to irrigation. Additionally, female landholders are older on average than male landholders (50% of women aged 45–64 years compared to 29% for men), and 25% of female landholders are older than 55 against 15% for men. It suggests that women are likely to access land at a later stage of their (productive) life. Finally, women cannot use the land as collateral in case of debt or other financial needs because they do not own it and thus, when her husband’s clan may take possession of the land and also the rest of the household’s material goods.

### 2.2.3 Labor access unbalancing

Women in Tanzania face several labor access constraints. They almost single-handedly handle housework and provide cash-necessary for households needs. They are also often reduced to unpaid or low paid productive activities and cannot rely on male labor the same way male landholders rely on female productive labor.

Women’s productivity on their plots is constrained by social expectations as they perform significant quantities of unpaid care and domestic work. Women are also expected to provide the cash-necessary to meet household maintenance needs and when cash is short this requires engaging in off-farm casual wage labor. This reduces women’s time available for agricultural activities, especially during peak availability for on-farm and off-farm work, reducing their opportunities to develop time consuming climate-smart agricultural techniques and impacting cash and non-cash resources women are able to generate from their plots. Women’s limited access to land also increases their economic vulnerability to climatic variation.

Women often contribute unpaid labor on their husband’s plots of land or at least expected to work on it in hand with other family members, on their own plots. Women do not control the output of the work they proceed for their husbands. Such labor forced by their husbands often comes hand in hand with gender-based violence and women use this as a tool to enforce labor allocation decisions regarding the use of their wives’ land on their plots and to ensure personal survival through care and domestic work by women, and especially food preparation for men.

Finally, and at high impact, women farmers use less hired labor and are less able to afford adequate amounts of farm inputs. Women’s limited access to credit also limits their ability of labor used in farming for female landholders. Overall, the inability to acquire adequate amounts of male family labor generates 97.3% of the gender gap in agricultural productivity in Tanzania, around US$102 million. On the opposite, women are hired more often as casual laborers than men and their mean wages are


---
**Page 135**
---

almost three times lower than those of men in agriculture. This might have consequences in terms of productivity and profitability of women farming activities and at the time-burden overload as both male and female farmers tend to use more female than male casual labor.

### Technical and technological barriers

Women in Tanzania frequently have unequal access to key agricultural inputs such as land, labor, knowledge such as the access to irrigation, pesticides, fertilizers and implements, and improved seeds and tools. 

92% of plots held by women are irrigated by flooding against 81% by bucket, as opposed to respectively 63% and 23% of men's plots. Very few women benefit from mechanical irrigation systems such as sprinklers, drip irrigation or micro-irrigation. Women have less access than men to improved agricultural technologies, notably pesticides, fertilizers and implements. Lower use of pesticides and inorganic fertilizers is a key factor explaining the gender gap in agricultural productivity. Women's access to agriculture inputs is also significantly lower than that of men, explaining 8% of the gender gap in agricultural productivity. Bringing lesser use of pesticides and inorganic fertilizers along with poor quality tools and implements together also contribute to the gender gap in agricultural productivity.

Where farmers do use improved seeds, men are more likely than women to use improved seeds, creating a further gender gap in agricultural productivity. Women have less access than men to agricultural extension service officers, which results in a lack of quality technical advice at the village and household level. Access to productive resources such as land, modern inputs, technology, education and financial services is a critical determinant to increase women's agricultural productivity.

### Financial barriers

Women in Tanzania face several financial barriers as they make lower incomes than men, tend to grow food crops (also called priority crops) while men grow cash crops (also called strategic crops), and have limited access to credit solutions such as microcredit, loans or grants. 

Farm activities are the important source of income for rural households and account for approximately half of household incomes across all expenditure quintiles. Self-employed women in agriculture earn significantly less than men, although there are some significant regional variations. They are more likely to be involved in subsistence farming rather than cash crop production.

Despite their work on jointly operated plots being greater than that of their husbands, households cannot always afford the inputs needed for joint farming and often when they do, cannot meet household requirements, and when wheat crops will be disposed of at certain control of the cash generated. 

There is also some evidence that women are more likely than men to spend earned income on their family needs (pia for clothing, health care, school fees and such as expenses for household maintenance are considered to be the responsibility of a wife), while money earned by husbands is not shared in the same way. Women have less access to credit solutions because they do use it. Furthermore, men use gender-based violence to take control of cash and non-cash resources generated by women, including loans that women take from village savings and loan groups.

Men in Tanzania are largely responsible for cash crop farming and income-generating activities while women take charge of food crops, help with cash crops, and provide the bulk of unpaid labor for


---
**Page 136**
---

Page 133

household duties. Self-employed women in agriculture are more likely to use their land for subsistence farming than for commercial farming. Due to unequal bargaining power within households, men are more likely to influence the coping and adaptation strategies adopted within male-headed households therefore favoring cash crops over food crops, increasing risks related to food security.

Few sources of credit exist for women in Tanzania because they lack formal property rights necessary as collateral. Microcredit, though available, is limited and only 6% of Tanzanians borrow from microcredit institutions. Group lending is an established practice in Tanzania but effective borrowing often avoids these small enterprises by expanding underdeveloped because of banking regulations.

**Page 137**
---

3. Actions and projects to reduce gender unbalance in Tanzania

### 3.1 CRDB Bank gender actions and policies

CRDB Bank is committed to fighting gender inequalities and inequities in Tanzania. To that intent, it has developed its own Gender Policy, aligned with Tanzania's gender commitments. By means of implementation, CRDB has designed ad-hoc women-oriented products and solutions, such as the Malkia Account.

#### 3.1.1 CRDB Gender Policy

CRDB Bank has developed a Gender Policy in 2019 [19], based on the premise that Tanzania as a country recognizes inequality as a major obstacle to socio-economic development of its people. This policy outlines all gender-related aspects of CRDB Bank's activities, defining gender as "social attributes and opportunities associated with being male and female and the relationships between women and men in society". The policy applies to all CRDB Bank's staff, business units, departments, branches and subsidiaries, and to other CRDB stakeholders (suppliers, implementing partners such as Tanzanian public and/or private sector institutions).

Internally, the policy implementation is under the responsibility of the Board of Directors and the Senior Management and Directors [especially that of the Director of Risk and Compliance], and of the director responsible for gender equality. The policy applies to all CRDB Bank's staff, business units, departments, branches and subsidiaries, and to other CRDB stakeholders (suppliers, implementing partners such as Tanzanian public and/or private sector institutions).

CRDB Bank highlights that gender equality is both a development goal in itself and a precondition for the achievement of other development outcomes. The bank has taken and continues to take various measures to ensure that its policies, plans, strategies and programmes are gender-sensitive and aim at achieving full and equal participation between women and men. The policy, aligned with CRDB Bank's internal policies and international and national agreements and policies applicable to Tanzania and CRDB Bank, aims at:

(i) Reduce gender disparities in leadership positions;
(ii) Transform gender relations;
(iii) Promote gender equality management;
(iv) Strengthen organisational structures and systems for gender equality by incorporating into policies, plans, strategies and programmes;
(v) Recognise the different needs of women and men; and
(vi) Guide different bank's business units on gender issues.

Most relevant to CRDB Bank's action in climate and agriculture, the bank reminds that resource shortages are gendered (worsening shortage and lack of access to water, energy and food sources), the latter being a major driver for migration. Women and men do not experience the impacts of climate change and environmental degradation equally. Women will be more likely carrying additional work from unpredictable climate and worsening crop failures. The bank also reminds that women are more vulnerable in terms of their exposure to climate change that acts as a magnifying glass (increased risks of gender inequalities in the access to and control of resources and decision making power, of women poverty, of undermining women's


---
**Page 138**
---

# Malik Account

CRDB Bank has created a bank account solution, the Malik Account, tailored to women’s needs [30]. The Malik Account is designed to help women meet their financial obligations and personal goals, including in investment, education, business and healthcare by:

- Easing loan conditions;
  - Providing attractive loans: annual lending interest rate of 14% against 24% before Malik Account.
- Providing attractive interest rates on deposits: from 1.5% to 2.5%, depending on the amount deposited.
- Providing special insurance products with discount [31].

CRDB Bank has also come up with a new scheme for women to access loans without collateral [32]. Overall, Malikia Account aims to help women boost their business. In addition to specific financial products, women can also benefit from counseling from “Malika Rafah,” women experts providing business and financial education on how to run their businesses [31]. More than 80,000 women have already opened Malikia accounts showing its success [34].


---
**Page 139**
---

3.2. Other projects in agriculture

3.2.1 Policy Action for Climate Change Adaptation in East Africa

The "Policy Action for Climate Change Adaptation," led by Climate Change, Agriculture, and Food Security between 2014 and 2020, is a project seeking to inform and link policies and institutions from national to local levels on climate change adaptation. It involved countries such as Ethiopia, Kenya, Uganda, and Tanzania. Scientific communities and policy actors analyzed policy formulation and implementation processes, trade-off analysis, future socio-economic and climate scenario development, and gender equality in agriculture. The project aimed to provide evidence for better informing the implementation of climate-resilient policies, especially to integrate gender-responsive adaptation strategies into national agricultural policies. This increased appreciation of and actions towards including gender-sensitive policies in their implementations.

Evidence was generated showing gender mainstreaming gaps in policy documents at national and sub-national levels and gaps in gender budgeting at a local government level. Almost half of the documents reviewed did not mention women or gender, and when they were mentioned, women were portrayed as marginalized and vulnerable, with a disconnection in gender integration between the national and district and lower levels of governance [35], [36].

3.2.2 Land tenure projects

Land tenure assistance projects, such as "Strengthening Women's Land Tenure Rights in Matabeleland," led by the United States Agency for International Development (USAID) and DAI between 2015 and 2021, aim support women with land tenure issues by creating customary certificates of occupancy for individuals who have been occupying land. However, many women were not aware that they had these rights or were not aware about them. These certifications give more incentive to women to participate in agricultural activities, which can lead to increased food security and better nutrition, improved access to credit and better use of resources for communities, and greater investment and agricultural productivity at national scale. Thanks to those rights, women are granted equal stakes as couples' properties and can focus more on agricultural activities [37], [38], [39].

3.3 Other actions and projects to reduce gender unbalance

Women face several vulnerabilities in several sectors. Among other sectors where actions are currently, or have recently been, implemented by international and local actors are:

- **Education projects such as "Akwasi Waseem": "Let them Learn" led by the USAID and the Barne Foundation. This project aims to provide education opportunities for vulnerable girls who dropped out of school, to create a friendly and more supportive environment for girls at school and to provide alternative educational pathways. Many girls face several barriers to finishing their education, including early marriage, pregnancy, poverty, gender-based violence, and emotional violence as estimated 44% of adolescent girls in Tanzania have given birth or are pregnant before the age of 18 years, while by age 35, an estimated 57% of women married have dropped out [40], [41].**

- **Access to legal support: projects such as the customary project led by USAID and Women in Law and Development in Africa provide legal and gender-based violence services [42].**


---
**Page 140**
---

Reinforcement of the participation in the political life projects such as "Womenize Womenizers" led by the USAID and the UN Fund for Women between 2018 and 2021, increase female participation in political parties and electoral processes. Several projects aimed to counter closing civic and political space, bolster freedom of expression, express the advocacy and concerns of women and girls, and strengthen democratic institutions. These projects also aim at sustainable systems free from corruption, and strengthen human rights among vulnerable populations, especially increasing the diversity of both candidates and internal party decision-makers [42], [43], [44].

Access to health and protection against HIV: projects such as "Sudar" led by the USAID between 2018 and 2021, aimed at providing access to comprehensive sexual and reproductive health services including family planning and HIV testing and counseling. Such projects especially focused on vulnerable populations as nearly one in three girls experiences some form of sexual violence before age 15; one in four women over the age of 15 report having experienced rape or attempted rape, and one in five women over age 15 reports experiencing sexual violence in her lifetime. Therefore, the utilization of antiretroviral therapy (ART) among people living with HIV is only at 60%, while 90% of people diagnosed receiving sustained antiretroviral therapy, and 90% of people under retroviral therapy to have viral suppression [45], [46].

Access to sanitation and hygiene: projects such as "Women's Empowerment and Entrepreneurship Initiative" led by the USAID, Dining for Women and African People & Wildlife between 2018 and 2021, improve access to sanitation and hygiene [47], [48].

Empowerment of women and girls: projects such as "Women's Empowerment and Entrepreneurship Initiative" led by the USAID, Dining for Women and African People & Wildlife between 2016 and 2021, aimed at empowering women and girls to take control over their lives. These projects aim to provide financial independence to women and allow them to gain status and decision-making power above the traditional household roles limitations. Several projects also aimed to train groups of vulnerable women in business skills such as bookkeeping, marketing, and sales. Other projects included communications skills, contraceptive methods and gender-based violence to help women take control of their finances and raise up their communities, and understand their health risks and opportunities to build bright futures [49], [50], [51].

## PROBLEM STATEMENT

**PROBLEMS IN AGRICULTURE WORKING IN AGRICULTURE ARE MORE VULNERABLE TO CLIMATE CHANGE EFFECTS AND CANNOT ENJOY ECONOMIC BENEFITS OF THEIR ACTIVITIES**

| IMMEDIATE EFFECTS | Limited agricultural output and productivity | Limited income and financial support | Lower time and resources available to implement climate change activities |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

| EFFECTS | Food insecurity | Risks of poverty | High overall vulnerability |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

|  | Limited opportunities to support reducing carbon emissions and adapting to climate change | Unemployment |


---
**Page 142**
---

The overall objective of the TACADIP is to support farmers with adaptation tools, knowledge, and capacity to enable best adaptation options to current and future climatic contexts and agronomic specifics; it will therefore intend to address several of the barriers and challenges specifically identified for women, either directly or indirectly through the following major components:

- Adaptation credit:
  (i) Promotion of long-term investment projects in agriculture to create a reliable market for agricultural products, enhance food security and encourage climate resilient and environment-friendly farming practices.
  (ii) Increase of the financial inclusion of smallholders farmers and increasing their productivity by making high cost technologies accessible and establishing service delivery models that are financially sustainable.
  (iii) Introduction of simplified and affordable financial products with potential sustainability.
  (iv) Promotion of climate resilient agriculture to encourage organic farming practice and reduce the utilization of uncertified seeds, industrial fertilizers and pesticides, and the importation of agricultural inputs.
  (v) Aggregation of smallholder farmers into intensive farming activities through large scale environmental friendly infrastructures.
  (vi) Support for women in intensifying their farming around climate resilient agriculture practices, as well as additional support to promote saving accounts, health insurance etc.

Output (i) will reinforce women's economic integration and income generation by structuring the market and providing support for food safety instead of food safety almost entirely relying on women (barrier 2.1.4). Outputs (ii), (iii) and (iv) will directly influence women as most of these smallholders are female-headed households, which is a key barrier to financial inclusion (barrier 2.1.5) and (v) will address the unequal access to technologies, techniques and financial solutions, levelling the field between men and women.

Guarantee support: Structuration of a guarantee facility to support partial credit risk guarantees for agriculture lending operations, due to specific challenges such as high credit risks, elevated interest rates and required financial documentation that smallholders cannot afford. Working with CRB Bank will ensure that the most financially vulnerable populations they will highly benefit from such credit risk guarantees as CRB Bank will be able to lend money for projects that would otherwise not be possible (barrier 2.1.6). By meeting the technical requirements, CRB Bank will remove the highest barrier preventing women from accessing financial solutions (barrier 2.1.4).

Technical support: The implementation of technical assistance packages and policy intervention in:
(i) Building institutions' capacity to implement adaptation measures and adopt climate resilient agriculture finance;
(ii) Designing and developing climate risks assessment tools to evaluate the viability of specific projects;
(iii) Training farmers on climate resilient and adaptation solutions;
(iv) Supporting the extension of services in order to apply Good Agricultural Practices in agriculture;
(v) Supporting policy interventions to attract private sector actors in agriculture resilience and adaptation lending operations.


---
**Page 143**
---

Outputs (i) and (ii) will help structure resilient and adaptation agriculture in Tanzania, reinforcing the sector and therefore providing better business opportunities for both women and men (barrier 2.2.6). Outputs (iii), (iv) and (v) will provide tools, training and support to adopt climate resilient and adaptation methods, knowledge for which women are more in demand than men, as seen in the barriers and challenges section (barrier 2.2.3).

# Gender Action Plan

## I- Methodology

This Gender Action Plan has been developed along with a Gender Assessment as technical annexes for the Tanzania Agriculture Climate Adaptation Technology Deployment Programme (TACATDP). Funding Proposal is submitted to the Global Environment Facility (GEF) Trust Fund. It is aligned with several of the GCF’s objectives including with GCF’s own Gender Action Plan:

- **Gender-balanced stakeholder consultations at all stages (at least 40%, with a target of 50% for all consultations):**
  
- **Objective to promote gender equality in climate action (gender equality at all stages and solutions tailored to gender specific context, such as more attractive loans or guarantees for women due to their vulnerability):**

- **Women and men will report improvements in their quality of life:**

- **Reduction of the vulnerability of both women and men:**

- **Increase in the adaptive capacity of women and men to respond to the impacts of climate change:**

- **Prevention of economic losses increase in economic opportunities and mitigation of gender-related risks.**


## II- Objectives

The overall objective of the TACATDP is to support farmers with adaptations, knowledge, and capacity to enable better adaptation options to current and future climate changes and agronomic specifics. The TACATDP aims at improving a strong gender lens, by increasing targets for women farmers groups and female agribusinesses. More specifically, it intends to provide financial and technical solutions to farmers and women due to their high vulnerability to climate change in Tanzania. Several social benefits are to expect from the project, especially among the employed vulnerable population of women and youth.

The TACATDP aims to achieve the following main gender-focused results: (i) Equal access to agriculture financing solutions (loans, guarantees and insurance) in terms of overall amount funded, number of project/farms funded, and type of farm funded; (ii) Sensitization of all agriculture value chain stakeholders, policy makers, and other relevant actors in respect to gender issues in agricultural operations, and equal access to credit for women and men farmers to adopt climate-resilient agricultural options; (iii) Gender responsive and equal participation of women at all stages of the project, during its design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation phases, including in decision-making processes, etc.; and (iv) Integration of gender considerations into all the deliverables (parametric insurance, online infrastructure, new/updated policies, manuals, etc.) based on sex-disaggregated data.

Focusing on gender aspects, TACATDP offers balanced opportunities for women’s and men’s active participation in project activities. By using mobile phones and mobile-banking facilities, the project will apply methods and approaches to increase the engagement of small-scale female farm operators and women in agricultural value chains. The project will also promote the use of climate-smart agriculture practices, productivity-enhancing technologies, farm adaptation activities, and improved livelihood strategies. They will be prioritized in capacity-building activities. It is expected that promoting the use of labor-saving technologies will help ease the time burden on women, burden they have to share productive activities.


---
**Page 148**
---

project will also reduce the burden of unpaid work that often disproportionately falls on women and girls in every region of the world. The project will further exploit and implement an opportunity to address their access to and control over resources and their decision-making ability over their work hours, opening greater opportunity for other socio-economic perspectives, including girl's education and women's engagement in economic empowerment and increase participation in social life. The project will focus on balancing gender on 50/50 gender balance.

Finally, all stakeholders involved in the project implementation will have to sign CRDB Gender Policy upfront and will have to follow and enforce this policy through the whole project. This will ensure that financial facility beneficiaries are not exposed to any form of violence against women and girls and institutional behaviors and practices, especially in the Technical Assistance and for capacity building components.

In addition, CRDB will put as eligibility criteria the signature of a "statement of non-exclusion criteria" related to gender policy that will include a disposal for violence against women. If any allegation against a farmer beneficiary is made during the project implementation period or ever after, it will be investigated. In case the financial facility beneficiary would be condemned by a court for any violence against women, the financial facility will be stopped immediately with penalties.

### III- Gender Action Plan

Below is the Gender Action Plan prepared for the TACATDP project:

#### Activities
*Impact Statement: Financial solutions and products (loans, guarantees and insurance) and technical assistance to support development and adoption of climate change adaptation technologies and solutions. These solutions will increase agricultural resilience in Tanzania, especially for women smallholder farmers. The programme will ensure that at least 40% of the total number of beneficiaries are female smallholder farmers. They will therefore promote gender equality in climate action and increase the adaptive capacity of both men and women. The programme will ensure gender aspects integration at all levels, during the project implementation (consultations, training, awareness raising) and after project implementation (monitoring and evaluation). The programme will also ensure that at least 40% of the total number of beneficiaries are female smallholder farmers aware of, and allowed access to insurance schemes; at least 80% of female smallholder farmers accessing extension services; at least 50% of women entrepreneurs trained in business management; at least 30% of medium and large farms dedicated to female owned farms; at least 40% of the total number of beneficiaries are female smallholder farmers aware of, and allowed access to insurance schemes; at least 80% of female smallholder farmers accessing extension services; at least 50% of women entrepreneurs trained in business management; at least 30% of medium and large farms dedicated to female owned farms.*

*Output statement: At least 40%, with a target of 50%, of both amounts provided through financial support (loans, guarantees and insurance schemes) and numbers of projects receiving those supports; at least 30% of medium and large farms dedicated to female owned farms; at least 50% of women entrepreneurs trained in business management; at least 40% of the total number of beneficiaries are female smallholder farmers aware of or livelihood diversification options, equally available to men and women; 80% of local financial institutions, financing agricultural activities, sensitive to gender issues and able to provide credit to women; at least 50% of women entrepreneurs trained in business management; 100% of female smallholder farmers aware of, and allowed access to insurance schemes; at least 80% of female smallholder farmers accessing extension services; at least 50% of women entrepreneurs trained in business management; at least 30% of medium and large farms dedicated to female owned farms.*
